THE AMASA STONE MATHER 
MEMORIAL PUBLICATION FUND 


The present volume is the eleventh work published by the Yale 
University Press on the Amasa Stone Mather Memorial Publi- 
cation Fund. This Foundation was established August 25, 1922, 
by a gift to Yale University from Samuel Mather, Esq., of 
Cleveland, Ohio, in pursuance of a pledge made in June, 1922, 
on the fifteenth anniversary of the graduation of his son, Amasa 
Stone Mather, who was born in Cleveland on August 20, 1884, 
and was graduated from Yale College in the Class of 1907. 
Subsequently, after traveling abroad, he returned to Cleveland, 
where he soon won a recognized position in the business life of 
the city and where he actively interested himself also in the 
work of many organizations devoted to the betterment of the 
community and to the welfare of the nation. His death from 
pneumonia on February 9, 1920, was undoubtedly hastened by 
his characteristic unwillingness ever to spare himself, even when 
ill, in the discharge of his duties or in his efforts to protect and 
further the interests committed to his care by his associates.

The Kingdom of St. James 


The Calling of a Prophet 


T was the month of February, 1844. On the eastern 
| bank of the Father of Waters lay the mushroom city 

of Nauvoo, sprung in half a dozen years from an open 
prairie to the metropolis of Illinois. Over it ruled supreme 
the founder of Mormonism, Joseph Smith, prophet, seer, 
revelator, and translator. For a decade and a half he had 
been laboring to build up a new kingdom of God on earth. 
To the distant corners of the globe missions had gone out 
in true apostolic fashion, without scrip or purse, burning 
with zeal for the faith, to preach the new gospel of the dis- 
pensation of the fulness of times. Marvelously successful 
had they been. In the face of ridicule and social ostracism, 
despite persecution and mobbing, the numbers of believers 
steadily increased. Nauvoo was a true émperium in imperio. 
To Smith had been granted, by a complacent state legisla- 
ture, a city charter which still stands unique in American 
municipal annals. With a military title such as no other 
American save the immortal Washington had ever held, he 
commanded a body of soldiery more numerous and better 
drilled than any the sovereign state of Illinois itself pos- 
sessed. And now he was waging a campaign to obtain for 
himself the presidency of the United States. 

To Nauvoo by river and by stagecoach route came a 
steady stream of travelers; pilgrims from other states and 
from foreign lands, journeying to the city appointed by 
God for the gathering of his saints; unbelieving gentiles, 
on secular missions bent; curious tourists, intent merely on 
sight-seeing; and honest doubters, come to investigate at 


o) THE KiIncpom oF St. JAMES 


first hand the pretensions of the new religion. One of the 
latter class was James J. Strang, a young lawyer from Bur- 
lington, Wisconsin. Since he was to become the Alpha and 
Omega of the Kingdom of St. James, he will repay our care- 
ful scrutiny. To recover after the lapse of a century and 
more of time the common facts about the life history of an 
obscure individual is ordinarily a difficult, if not impossible, 
task. Fortunately, in the present instance, our subject has 
left behind a considerable mass of manuscripts, together 
with an extensive body of material in print, dealing with 
his personal and public career. From these we may draw 
not merely the obvious biographical facts which the writer 
consciously set down, but also much important indirect in- 
formation upon the character and idiosyncrasies of the fu- 
ture prophet and king.* 

Jesse James Strang, or James Jesse Strang as he was 
known in after life,j was born on Poplar Ridge Road, in 
the town of Scipio, Cayuga County, New York, March 21, 


* The records alluded to will be described more at length in succeeding 
footnotes. The account of Strang’s earlier years which immediately follows 
is chiefly derived from two documents, a fragmentary autobiography and a 
like fragmentary diary. The autobiography was begun by Strang in 1855, 
the year before his tragic death. The manuscript terminates abruptly, in 
the middle of a sentence, before the story of its author’s childhood has been 
concluded. For it see Henry E. Legler, A Moses of the Mormons (Mil- 
waukee: Parkman Club Publications, Nos. 15 and 16, 1897). The diary (or 
as much of it as has escaped destruction) was kept by Strang during the 
years 1831-36. A half dozen years since it was in the possession of Henry 
Denio of Lamoni, Iowa, a grandson of Strang, from whom the present writer 
secured the loan of it for the purpose of studying and copying. The note- 
book in which the diary was kept was much worn and mutilated. The 
surviving portion of the diary is printed for the first time, post, as Ap- 
pendix II. 

+ In the diary Strang frequently wrote his name across the top of the 
page. In 1831 this entry is uniformly “Jesse J. Strang.” Beginning with 
the following year the forms “Jesse J.” and “James J.” are intermixed, 
until in 1834 only the latter form appears. The maiden name of Strang’s 
mother was James, her father being Jesse James. Thus it is apparent that 
Strang was named for his maternal grandfather, and that beginning about 
the year 1832 he gradually reversed the order of his given names. 

A somewhat curious reflection occurs in this connection. In the Strangite 


Tue CALLING OF A PRopHET 2 


1813. His childhood was one prolonged period of illness, 
so much so that until he had children of his own, “happy 
in their infantile gambols,” the recollection of his early 
years ‘“‘produced a kind of creeping sensation akin to terror.” 
Like his parents before him, Strang was slight of frame and 
short of stature. In contrast to his sickly body (which he 
seems to have outgrown in later years) his intellectual en- 
dowment was unusual. In childhood his teachers and other 
acquaintances regarded him as mentally incompetent, “little 
more than idiotic’; but this judgment seems to have been 
erroneously derived from his physical condition. 


Long weary days I sat upon the floor [he records in the auto- 
biography], thinking, thinking, thinking! Occasionally asking a 
strange uninfantile question and never getting an answer. My mind 
wandered over fields that old men shrink from, seeking rest and 
finding none till darkness gathered thick around and I burst into 
tears and cried aloud, and with a voice scarcely able to articulate 
told my mother that my head ached. 


This unusual mentality was matched by an unusual 
memory. To the end of life Strang distinctly recalled as one 
of childhood’s few bright spots, his mother “carrying me in 
her arms, nursing me, and conversing with her sister about 
me.” Before he was a year and a half old his parents re- 
moved to another community, yet so distinctly did he re- 


Bible, the Book of the Law of the Lord, which purports to have been trans- 
lated by Strang with divine aid from the “plates of Laban” which the 
ancient Jews kept in the Ark of the Covenant, verse 6 of chapter xx on the 
“Calling of a King” reads: “He hath chosen his servant James to be King; 
he hath established him a Prophet above the kings of the earth; and ap- 
pointed him king of Zion; by his own voice did he call him, and he sent his 
angels unto him to ordain him.” Two alternative deductions from this state- 
ment are possible: either the divine author of the Law of the Lord did not 
know that the future king of the earth would be named Jesse by his parents ; 
or the finite author of the reputed sacred record had forgotten, when com- 
posing it, that in earlier life he had gone by the name of Jesse rather than 
James Strang. 


4 Tue Kincpom oF St. JAMES 


member the place of his birth that after an absence of 
twenty years he was able to recognize the location when 
riding through the place. 

In February, 1816, when Strang was almost three years 
old, his parents removed to Hanover, Chautauqua County, 
New York. Here in a frontier community the child grew 
to manhood, and in this immediate vicinity his life was 
passed until his removal to Wisconsin in the early forties. 
The autobiography carries the story of his life to the age 
of twelve, and the diary takes it up at the age of eighteen. 
The missing period is of no great importance, for the career 
of a frontier farm boy from his twelfth to his eighteenth 
year can have furnished little worthy of positive record. 
In the miserable schools which the region then afforded 
Strang procured the bare rudiments of an education. To 
some men, however, is given the ability to acquire knowl- 
edge without the aid of formal schooling; Abraham Lincoln 
was one such person and Jesse J. Strang was another. The 
diary of his early manhood discloses a keen appetite for 
reading, and a taste for argumentation which amounted 
almost to a passion. The former found satisfaction in the 
reading of such papers and books as the community af- 
forded, the latter in assiduous attendance upon debating 
societies and lyceums. 

In these latter activities Strang trained himself in the 
arts of argumentation and public address. He seems easily 
to have outshone his associates in these exercises, and like 
Lincoln he became a master of invective and satire. On one 
occasion his opponent in debate accused Strang of ‘quoting 
history which [he] had never read and quoting it falsely.” 
Strang replied with reasons calculated to show that he had 
read the passage in dispute and demanded that the accuser 


THE CALLING OF A PROPHET &§ 


present his proofs. This elicited the somewhat remarkable 
proposition that one has the right to make any accusation 
he pleases, and it stands good until the accused has dis- 
proved it. Strang thereupon recorded in his diary his in- 
tention to “train” his opponent shortly; as the sequel, a 
few weeks later he recited before the debating society a 
poem in blank verse, reviewing the accuser’s shortcomings 
in a manner so savage that even the author deemed it pru- 
dent not to recite the whole in public.* The youth who at 
eighteen had thus learned to excoriate a friend in playful 
debate, a few years later, provoked by a theological dispute, 
could pronounce a curse upon the inhabitants of an entire 
city, which after the lapse of fourscore years one can hardly 
read without a creeping of the flesh. 

A result of Strang’s reading and disputations of early 
manhood which seems strangely incongruous in view of 
his later career as a prophet was that he became a pro- 
nounced skeptic. Again a comparison with the life of Abra- 
ham Lincoln is in order, for at the same time Strang in 
western New York and Lincoln in central Illinois were 
reading Volney’s Runs and Paine’s Age of Reason, and 
with like results. Lincoln discussed them with his associates 
in the village grocery, and Strang in his debating society, 
and both became freethinkers. The debating society in which 
Strang was active seems to have been a center of freethink- 
ing, for there are indications in the diary that the orthodox 
in the community sought to suppress it. Probably Strang 
was the leader of this liberal group, for in the spring of 
1835 the godly-minded haled him before the grand jury 

* The opening lines were as follows: 

“To him who rides on slander’s foaming steeds 
Who loves to charge a friend with basest deeds 


Who speaks for naught but wrathful vengeance wrong 
Who hopes to rise by slander’s blasting tongue.” 


6 THE KINGDOM OF ST. JAMES 


on a charge of false swearing in connection with testimony 
given in a suit at law.* Then, the youth records, . 


Forestville spit its venom in vain. Its highways and lanes and 
ditches were emptied to go and cry against me. All Hell was raked 
for false witness. Young men whose beards were not grown ha- 
rangued the multitude. Old sinners whose feet trembled beneath 
their emaciated bodies and whose eyes stare horror-struck at the 
gaping grave appeared in open day and pleaded jurors to perjure 
themselves and ruin me. Clergymen and their pious followers 
prayed the overwhelming vengeance of the Allmighty on the heaven 
daring Infidel who[m] Providence had thrown in their power. 


But the jury returned no indictment, and the “heaven 
daring Infidel” was left free to continue his scandalous way. 

A few months later, while working around a mill race 
Strang fell into the swollen river and escaped drowning as 
by a miracle. His reflections during the brief space of time 
when, gasping for breath, he fought the mad current are 
faithfully recorded in the diary: 


Brief has been my life, for then I remembered it all; few are my 
friends, but none were forgotten; few also my enemies, but it was 
a melancholy thought that of the most relentless I was perhaps to 
die unavenged. [He pictured, too, his funeral obsequies.] Friends 
sobbing around; my poor, heartbroken mother sinking by my pale 
remains; those who in life had injured me deep as hatred could 
conceive or malice could invent standing in clusters round talking, 
perhaps that the hand of god had done it and then offering their 
consoling (ah! vile) assistance. 


Evidently the iron had entered the soul of the youth who 
could indulge such reflections as these in the imminent 
presence of death. 

During these early years the future prophet was out- 


* The suit was over the title of his brother, David, to a piece of real 
estate. 


THE CALLING OF A PROPHET 7 


wardly occupied with teaching school, working on his fa- 
ther’s farm, and studying law, the pursuit of which he en- 
tered upon during his twentieth year. Inwardly, his mind 
was a strange complex of conflicting ideals and ambitions. 
Already he had devised a system of cipher in which much 
of the diary is recorded. On his nineteenth birthday he re- 
flects that he is “‘as yet no more than a common farmer. It 
is too bad. I ought to have been a member of Assembly or 
a Brigadier General before this time.’ But he has already 
made one great discovery, the key, there is much reason to 
think, to his future remarkable career: ‘“That is that I am 
eager and mankind are frail,” and “I shall act upon it for 
time to come for my own benefit.” “My head is filled with 
projects,” he writes a few months later. He hopes to form 
a literary association, and in addition a “mutual instruc- 
tion military academy” for young militia officers. At the 
same time he is “‘only waiting for a printer” to begin pub- 
lication of a “literary and scientifick journal.” This project, 
however, is only of minor importance; it is to be undertaken 
merely to introduce the hopeful dreamer to the public. 
Between engaging in the fur trade, speculating in the 
United States or in foreign countries, entering upon a mili- 
tary career, and other possible lines of activity, Strang’s 
choice of a career finally settled upon the law. He took up 
its study determined to excel, but strangely diffident, for 
once, as to his ability to do so. He is willing to study seven 
years, if necessary, but if in the end he cannot excel he will 
take up a mercantile career; for he ‘“‘would rather be the 
best hunter in an Indian tribe” than a commonplace mem- 
ber of the New York bar. He was admitted to the bar of 
Chautauqua County in October, 1836, and practiced law 
at Ellington, where he resided. But he did not follow his 
chosen profession very assiduously for during the next 


8 Tue Kincpom oF St. JAMES 


half-dozen years he served as village postmaster, temper- 
ance lecturer, and for two years as editor of the Randolph 
Herald. 

Meanwhile a chain of circumstances had been slowly 
forging which was to transfer him from the community 
where he had spent his life thus far to the newer and rawer 
frontier of southern Wisconsin. In recent decades the name 
of Chautauqua has become far famed by reason of a certain 
type of religious-educational endeavor. Probably few people 
of the present generation are aware of the fact that long 
before the Chautauqua movement originated this vicinity 
was a prominent center of several new types of religious 
activity. In particular it was a stronghold of Mormon- 
ism, many of whose early converts were from Chautauqua 
County. Near by (in Allegany County) was the home of 
Sidney Rigdon both before and after his spectacular career 
as Joseph Smith’s chief lieutenant. Jamestown, the county 
seat, was in 1833 an active center of the new faith, and 
Rigdon, its most fluent expositor, sojourned here for a 
time.* Strang’s parents were charter members of one of 
the pioneer Baptist churches of the county, which they 
joined in 1817,7 and this religious influence dominated his 
early years. A youth of his active mentality, however, could 
not remain ignorant of the manifestations of Mormonism 
in his own county. About the time he was admitted to the 
bar, indeed, he formed a marriage connection which brought 
him into close personal contact with the new faith. His 
bride was Mary Perce, the daughter of a Baptist clergyman, 
and one of her sisters was the wife of Moses Smith, who 


* Centennial History of Chautauqua County (Jamestown, 1904), I, 176- 
fe 

+See Andrew W. Young, History of Chautauqua County, New York 
(Buffalo, 1875), p. 427 for the list of charter members. Strang’s father, 
Clement Strang, was the latest survivor of the group. 


THE CALLING OF A PROPHET 9 


had been since 1832 an ardent Mormon.* Smith was a man 
of sterling character and much natural ability, a pioneer 
of the best type. In 1835 he migrated to Racine County, 
Wisconsin, in company with Benjamin C. Perce, his brother- 
in-law, who was, of course, the brother of Mrs. Strang. In 
the autumn of the same year the two men explored the west- 
ern end of the county bordering on Fox River. Smith marked 
his claim on the present site of Burlington, while Perce lo- 
cated a claim two miles farther west, the site of the future 
sacred city of Voree. Friends and relatives soon followed 
these first-comers to the new location, and due to Smith’s 
proselyting zeal and qualities of leadership the new com- 
munity became a considerable local center of Mormonism. 

Although Strang had acquired through his marriage in 
1836 an intimate connection with members of the Mormon 
faith, we have no evidence to indicate that he himself paid 
any serious attention to the new teaching while he remained 
a resident of Chautauqua County. In the summer of 1843, 
however, he followed the westward path of his wife’s rela- 
tives to Burlington, Wisconsin, where he soon fell under 
the influence of Mormonism. Moses Smith had removed to 
Illinois before this time, but his brother, Aaron, was living 
at Burlington and, like Moses, was an ardent Mormon. 
With Aaron Smith, Strang journeyed to Nauvoo in Febru- 
ary, 1844. Whether he came to scoff and remained to pray 
remains unrecorded. At any rate, under the personal in- 
struction of Joseph and Hyrum Smith and Sidney Rigdon, 
at this time the three highest dignitaries of Mormonism, 
the erstwhile ‘‘cool philosopher” and ardent infidel quickly 
became a convert to the faith. A born orator, possessed of 
a brilliant if erratic mind, and equipped with a remarkable 


* A long and appreciative account of Moses Smith’s career accompanies 
his obituary notice in the Voree Herald, June 24, 1849. 


10 THE KiIncpom oF St. JAMES 


fund of information, the neophyte evidently made a strong 
impression upon the Mormon leaders. He was baptized by 
the Prophet himself and sealed with the keys of the Melchi- 
sedic priesthood, and later was ordained an elder.* 

At this time the Saints were discussing anew their need of 
a suitable country where they might settle and be free from 
gentile molestation. Joseph Smith was a man of very de- 
fective education, but he knew how to levy upon the knowl- 
edge possessed by others. With Strang, who was “greatly 
learned in geography,” he discussed a project for exploring 
the far West to learn what facilities it offered for Mormon 
settlement. Of more immediate import for our story, Strang 
advised that a settlement of Saints be made at once on 
White River near Burlington, advancing plausible economic 
reasons in support of the proposal. Accordingly he was en- 
joined by the church leaders to return home and prepare a 
more careful report to the Prophet concerning the desira- 
bility of developing a Mormon center of influence on the 
spot he had pointed out. This was done, and on May 24 
Strang mailed his report to Smith at Nauvoo, setting forth 
minutely the advantages which would accrue to the Mor- 
mons from building a town in southeastern Wisconsin, and 
especially recommending the site on White River two miles 
west of Burlington, where Benjamin Perce had located in 
1835. 

The response to this letter was sufficiently startling. At 
Nauvoo, despite the outward appearance of prosperity, due 
to a combination of internal dissension and gentile perse- 


* The facts concerning Strang’s conversion have been drawn from the 
Chronicles of Voree, pp. 7 ff. This is one of the official record books (sacred 
in the eyes of the faithful) of the church which Strang established. Until 
half a dozen years ago it was in the possession of Wingfield Watson of 
Burlington. The present writer caused a photostatic reproduction to be made 
of it, and this reproduction is preserved in the Wisconsin Historical Library, 
in Madison.

cution the rég¢me of Joseph Smith was tottering to its fall. 
It ended tragically on June 27, 1844, with the murder of 
the Prophet and his brother Hyrum by a gentile mob in 
Carthage jail. At the identical moment the Prophet was 
thus slain in Illinois, the angel of God appeared to Strang 
in Wisconsin, ‘“‘accompanied by a numerous heavenly train,” 
and anointing his head with oil, divinely ordained him to 
the rulership of the Saints on Earth.* 

Meanwhile, on June 18, nine days before this angelic an- 
nunciation, Smith at Nauvoo had written a letter to Strang 
which was received by the latter at Burlington on July 9. 
On the receipt of Strang’s report, it stated, the Prophet had 
inclined to the opinion that Strang was under the influence 
of an evil spirit. But God had appeared to him on the hill 
of the temple, clothed with light as with a garment; moon 
and stars went out, the earth dissolved in space, and Smith 
was caught up on wings of cherubim to strains of heavenly 
music. To him was revealed his approaching martyrdom, 
and the appointment of Strang as his successor in the pro- 
phetic office. On the prairie by White River, in the counties 
of Racine and Walworth, he should establish a new gather- 

* The authority for the angelic visitation is, of course, Strang himself. 
His narrative of the event was first printed in The Diamond, a religious 


disquisition published at Voree in pamphlet form in 1848. It was subse- 
quently reprinted in Strang’s The Prophetic Controversy (St. James, 1854), 
pp. 13-14. 

+ Chronicles of Voree, pp. 1 ff. Printed also in The Diamond. The Mor- 
mon factions opposed to the Strangite group angrily maintained that this 
letter was a forgery. See, e.g., statements of Crandell Dunn and John Taylor 
in the Liverpool (Eng.) Millennial Star, October 15, 1846. The present 
writer, early in the course of this study formed the opinion that if Smith’s 
letter were still in existence an examination of it would suffice to determine 
the question of its authenticity. After several years of vain groping it was 
found and is now in the writer’s possession. Perusal of it disclosed, however, 
that the body of the letter had been written by some clerk or other person 
and only the signature was (or purported to be) in Smith’s hand. To detect 
a forged signature alone is obviously less easy than to detect an entire 
forged letter, and a closer familiarity with Smith’s handwriting than the 
writer can claim would be requisite to determine the issue in question. 


12 Tue Kincpom oF St. JAMES 


ing place for the sorely beset Saints; here a temple should 
be built and many mighty works of God be manifested; 
the people would have rest and peace and wax fat and pleas- 
ant in the presence of their enemies; and the place should 
be called Voree, which, being interpreted, means “garden 
of peace.”’ 

Into the realm of the miraculous the plain historian may | 
not enter. It calls for no comment, therefore, that while 
this letter was being written James J. Strang at distant 
Voree was enjoying his first vision.* In spirit he was carried 
to the top of the hill on the west border of Racine, with 
Gardner’s Prairie to the north and the White River marshes 
to the south. Before him was spread out a city built of stone, 
with gardens and streets, shops and people. The vision 
changed, and the people with lively pace and smiling coun- 
tenance thronged the streets. Again the vision changed, and 
all were assembled in a vast hall, and James J. Strang, 
surrounded by wise men and counselors and priests and elo- 
quent orators arose and taught the people; and the spirit of 
prophecy witnessed to him that these things should come to 
pass. 

Such was the divine appointment and such the dream 
that directed Strang’s further pathway in life. In contrast 
with the splendor of these heavenly visions, the immediate 
pathway which he must tread was thorny enough. The assas- 
sination of Joseph Smith plunged the Church into a state of 
confusion from which even yet it has not recovered. The 
leadership of a people numbering scores of thousands was 
a matter of no slight importance, and over the succession 
to Joseph developed a permanent schism in the ranks of 
Mormonism. The immediate situation at Nauvoo was in 


* Chronicles of Voree, pp. 9-10; printed in the Voree Herald, August, 
1846. 


THE CALLING OF A PROPHET 12 


the hands of the twelve apostles, and under the able leader- 
ship of Brigham Young they grasped and held the power 
which had fallen from the hands of Smith. Two companion 
rivals contested this dominance with Young and the Twelve. 
Sidney Rigdon, one of the founders of Mormonism, and sole 
surviving member of the First Presidency, was one of these, 
but his opposition was soon disposed of. Less spectacular, 
perhaps, than Rigdon’s, but far more persistent and menac- 
ing to the designs of Young and his associates, were the 
claims put forward by Strang. 


CHAPTER II 


The Struggle for the Succession 


HEN a monarch departs the earthly stage leav- 
ing behind him a well-ordered government with 
a definite scheme of succession, the transition of 
rulers need occasion no trouble or disorder. But when, on » 
the contrary, the governmental organization is rudimentary 
and no recognized scheme of succession exists, ambitious 
rivals are certain to contend for the scepter, with consequent 
schisms and turmoil. Joseph Smith was the creator of Mor- 
monism, yet although he was supposed to enjoy familiar and 
frequent intercourse with the Omnipotent, his many inspired 
pronouncements afforded the thousands of followers, who 
had staked their all in this world and in the world to come 
upon his teachings, no clear indication concerning the right- 
ful succession to the authority he had resigned. There en- 
sued for Mormonism, therefore, as night succeeds the day, 
a prolonged period of internecine strife and tumult. Faction 
contended with faction and to a ribald gentile world was 
presented the spectacle of the foremost leaders among the 
Saints denouncing one another in language charged with 
venom no less bitter than they had hitherto employed 
toward their gentile persecutors. The fair fabric of the 
Church was rent in tatters; today, despite the passage of 
three-quarters of a century of time, the divisions then en- 
gendered still endure, with no present prospect that the 
ancient unity will ever be restored. 
Only in the light of the considerations here briefly sug- 
gested can the reader prepare himself to understand the 
violence of language and the bitterness of feeling which 


THE STRUGGLE FOR THE SUCCESSION 15 


pervades the remainder of our narrative. For Strang, the 
initial steps in the assumption of the prophetic calling af- 
forded a foretaste of the turmoil which was henceforth to 
beset his pathway to the end of life. On July 26, 1844, ac- 
companied by Aaron Smith, he set out from Burlington on 
his first mission to acquaint the Saints abroad with his 
ministry and to invite their allegiance and support. At 
Florence, Michigan, a conference of elders was held to 
whom Strang stated his case. According to the Strangite 
record,* it was agreed that Moses Smith and another elder 
should proceed to carry the revelation to Nauvoo; mean- 
while, those elders who accepted Strang’s appointment were 
to preach it on their own responsibility, while those who 
doubted were to maintain silence. Two of them, however, 
Elders Dunn and Green, who were the presiding high priests 
for Michigan, attempted to prevent Strang and Smith from 
proceeding farther on their mission. An uproar followed, 
which Strang ended by informing his opponents they could 
not command him “whom God had placed at their head,” 
and withdrawing from the premises. 

Quite different was Dunn’s story of this affair.f He 
affirmed that, detecting flaws in Strang’s letter of appoint- 
ment, he subjected him to a process of cross-questioning 
which the aspiring Prophet was unable to meet. Finding 
he had no license to preach, and that his claims were with- 
out merit, Dunn proceeded to cut him off from the Church 
and to report this action to the authorities at Nauvoo, where 
it was duly published.+ 


* Chronicles of Voree, pp. 13 ff. 

+ Printed in the Liverpool Millennial Star, October 15, 1846. _ 

+Dunn represents that when he exposed the fraud in connection with 
Strang’s letter of appointment the latter’s “head dropped as if he had been 
knocked down.” This, if true, affords the only instance we have found in 
Strang’s entire career where he was overcome in the field of argument. 


16 Tue Kincpom oF St. JAMES 


Strang and Aaron Smith continued their eastward mis- 
sion as far as Sandusky, while Moses Smith wended his 
way in the opposite direction to Nauvoo. The Prophet was 
hopeful of meeting the twelve apostles of the Church who 
were returning to Nauvoo after the death of Smith.* In 
this he failed, and after a spell of illness returned by boat 
to Wisconsin, landing at Southport} on September 4. 

Thus far the knowledge of Strang’s doings had been 
concealed from the people around Voree, “lest they should 
be induced to ask unreasonable prices for their lands.” On 
January 5, 1845, however, the first church meeting was held 
at the house of Aaron Smith at Voree, and elders were ap- 
pointed, who from this time began to preach throughout 
the surrounding region the appointment of Strang and the 
gathering at Voree. Not until autumn, however, did the 
advocates of the new Prophet attract any particular public 
notice. In a second divine revelation, given out by Strang 
in January, 1845, with a view to encouraging his followers 
in their ministry, it was promised that if they would faith- 
fully serve God He would give to Strang ‘‘the plates of the 
ancient records” which should be a witness between God 
and those who served Him.§ 

On September 1, 1845, accordingly, Strang experienced 
his second angelic visitation, the divine messenger coming 
this time to reveal to him the ancient records.|| Twelve days 
later, Strang called together four of his followers and 
told them what he had thus learned. Under his direction 


* At the time of Smith’s murder most of the church leaders were absent 
from Nauvoo, prosecuting his campaign for the presidency. On receiving 
news of his death they hastened to make their several ways homeward to 
the capital city. 

+ The modern city of Kenosha. £Chronicles of Voree, p. 14. 

§ Revelation of June 17, 1845, in Chronicles of Voree. 

|| The revelation is in the Chronicles of Voree, pp. 21 ff.; it has been

James J. Strang, pp. 5-6. 


THE STRUGGLE FOR THE SUCCESSION 17 


they proceeded to the “‘hill in the east of Walworth, against 
White River in Voree,” and there, digging under the roots 
of an oak tree a foot in diameter, found certain metallic 
plates, carefully cased up and covered with “an alphabetical 
and pictorial record.”* Such a discovery naturally roused 
great interest in the surrounding region. Strang records that 
several thousand people came to examine the ground, and 
the whole country “was filled with the fame of the occur- 
rence.” Into our story at this point flits for a single pleasant 
moment a man whom the modern world of business should 
ever hold in grateful memory. C. Latham Sholes, inventor 
of the typewriter, whose cheerful click encircles the globe, 
was then the editor of a country paper in the nearby town 
of Southport. The fame of the supernatural discovery led 
him to pay a visit to Voree to investigate the matter. 


The prophet appears to us [runs his editorial report of his in- 
vestigation] a very intelligent man, devoid of anything like enthu- 
siasm; and, so far as we could judge, honest and earnest in all he 
said. The men who subscribe the statement [concerning the finding 
of the plates] are said to be among the most honest and intelligent 
in the neighborhood; and, taking it all together, it is something to 
stagger ordinary credulity. 

The popular opinion will doubtless call it a humbug. So should 
we from the natural impulse of our mind. But when the testimony 
appears in opposition to such impulse, we are content—to have no 
opinion about it. 


The finding of the Voree plates may well be viewed as 
the touchstone of Strang’s prophetic career. That the plates 
were found and dug up under his direction is as well 
established as any fact can be by human testimony. It is 


* Chronicles of Voree, pp. 25 ff.; Strang, Prophetic Controversy, pp. 35- 
36; Watson, Revelations of James J. Strang, pp. 6-8. The four “witnesses” 
who dug up the plates were Aaron Smith, Jirah B. Wheelan, James M. 
Van Nostrand, and Edward Whitcomb. Like the witnesses to Joseph Smith’s 
plates, most of these later apostatized and repudiated Strang. 


18 Tue Kincpom oF St. JAMES 


quite conceivable that Strang’s angelic visitations may have 
had only a subjective existence in the brain of the man who 
reported them. But the metallic plates possessed a very 
material objective reality; and we can hardly escape the 
conclusion either that Strang knowingly fabricated and 
“planted” them for the purpose of duping his credulous 
followers, or that they were what they purport to have been, 
ancient records divinely preserved, in the discovery and 
translation of which Strang was divinely guided. If the 
former alternative be accepted, it follows that Strang’s 
entire prophetic career was a false and impudent imposture; 
if the latter be the true one, we are confronted by the sad 
fact that of all the people now on earth only a few score 
at the most have comprehended it. 

On the day after the plates were dug up (September 14) 
the faithful assembled at the house of Aaron Smith to in- 
spect them and to listen to an account by Strang and the 
four witnesses of the manner in which they had been ob- 
tained.* A week later they again assembled, together with 
an “immense concourse’ of outsiders. The story of finding 
the plates by divine direction was repeated, and in addition 
Strang read the translation which he had made by the aid 
of Urim and Thummim three days before.| Whatever this 
translation may have signified to the faithful, to the un- 
believer it presents a strange jumble of unintelligible non- 
sense.t The inscription purports to be the record of “Rajah 


* Chronicles of Voree, p. 29. 

+Urim and Thummim were two transparent stones set in the rims of a 
bow which an angel had provided for Joseph Smith to use in translating 
the Book of Mormon. After its translation they were restored to the angelic 
custody by Smith. Believers in Strang’s claim to the prophetic succession 
would, of course, regard his possession of Urim and Thummim as an addi- 
tional evidence of his divine calling.

THE VOREE PLATES 


Reproduced from a pamphlet issued by Wingfield 
Watson. The plates have long since been lost. 


THE STRUGGLE FOR THE SUCCESSION 19 
Manchore of Vorito.” With this single appearance, “Rajah 
Manchore”’ vanishes from the stage of human history. But 
the concluding sentences of his “record” possess a signifi- 
cance which even the gentile mind can grasp. ‘“The fore- 
runner men shall kill, but a mighty prophet there shall 
dwell. I will be his strength, and he shall bring forth thy 
record. Record my words and bury it in the hill of Promise.” 
The “forerunner” was, of course, Joseph Smith, and the 
“mighty prophet,” James J. Strang; and the clear intent of 
these statements was to evidence to the world that Strang 
had been appointed by God from ancient times to be his 
vicegerent on earth. 

The organization of the Church, which had remained in 
abeyance for more than a year after the revelation of 
Strang’s divine appointment, now proceeded apace. Stephen 
Bartholf was sent on a mission to Nauvoo to convey thither 
the news of the discovery of the ancient plates.* In two 
weeks he returned with an inquiring elder of the Church at 
Nauvoo, and in a conference of the faithful held at Bart- 
holf’s home it was unanimously agreed to publish to all 
the Saints the testimonies of God’s work in the upbuilding 
of Voree, and the title of Strang to the First Presidency of 
the Church. 

The obvious import of this resolution was a declaration 
of war upon the twelve apostles at Nauvoo, who under the 
masterful guidance of Brigham Young had seized control 
of the Church after the murder of Smith in 1844. The 
charge of the publication was intrusted to Prophet Strang, 
and he proceeded to bring out, in January, 1846, the first 
issue of the Voree Herald, which was to be henceforth the 
official organ of the Strangite movement. The Herald con- 


* Chronicles of Voree, pp. 31 ff. 


20 Tue Kincpom oF St. JAMES 


tained, aside from “James the Prophet’s’’ first pastoral 
letter to the elders of the Church, the testimony of the four 
witnesses besides other documents and arguments concern- 
ing Strang’s divine calling.* Instead of at once confronting 
the Twelve at Nauvoo with this evidence, however, Strang 
proceeded to develop a rear attack upon their entrench- 
ments. The Twelve were at this time bending every en- 
ergy to the task of removing the faithful to the far West, 
there to set up in the wilderness a theocratic nation. Cap- 
tains of hundreds were appointed, under whose leadership 
not only the Saints of Nauvoo but those abroad who could 
be persuaded to undertake the migration, were being or- 
ganized. Scattered over northern Illinois were hundreds of 
Mormons, many of whom were preparing to join in the 
great journey toward the setting sun. Upon these trustful 
people the zealots from Voree descended with the discon- 
certing information that the leadership of the Twelve was 
a usurpation and their projected westward migration a 
device of the devil. With words of burning eloquence 
Strang pleaded with the misguided Saints; with masterly 
argument he convinced them. 


Let not my call to you be vain. The destroyer has gone forth 
among you and has prevailed. You are preparing to resign country 
and houses and lands to him. Many of you are about to leave the 
haunts of civilization and of men to go into an unexplored wilder- 
ness among savages, and in trackless deserts to seek a home in the 


* No copy of this first issue of the Herald seems now to be in existence; 
it apparently contained the greater portion (pp. 1-12) of what Strang later 
reprinted as The Diamond: Being the Law of Prophetic Succession and a 
Defense of the Calling of James J. Strang as Successor to Joseph Smith 

. . See Chronicles of Voree, p. 49. A letter of Reuben Miller to Strang 
from Nauvoo, February 15, 1846 (the date on the letter is 1845, but the 
contents disclose that the actual date was 1846), states that five thousand 
copies of the paper had been struck off at Nauvoo for local use. The letter 
is preserved among the personal papers of James J. Strang, now in the 
present writer’s possession (cited henceforth as the Strang MSS). 


THE STRUGGLE FOR THE SUCCESSION 21 
wilds where the footprint of the white man is not found. The voice 
of God has not called you to this. His promise has not gone before 
to prepare a habitation for you. The hearts of the Lamanites* are 
not turned unto you, and they will not regard you. When the herd 
comes the savages shall pursue. The cloud which surrounds by day 
shall bewilder, and the pillar of fire by night shall consume and 
reveal you to the destroyer, and the men in whom you trusted when 
you rejected the promises of God shall leave you early and not be 
found of you in your greatest need. 

Let the oppressed flee for safety unto Voree, and let the gathering 
of the people be there. Let the evil who have gone to the holy city 
be rejected, and given to the law. Let the Twelve go out and preach 
the gospel to the nations according to the command of God, instead 
of staying at home as a prominent mark to bring cruel enemies on 
their brethren. Let the filth of Zion be cleansed and her garments of 
peace put on. Let neither gun nor sword be lifted in defiance, nor 
rest be taken upon arm of flesh, and the city of our God shall be 
saved and the Temple of his holiness be unpolluted by the hand of 
the gentile. 


At St. Charles, Illinois, on January 6, Strang encountered 
Reuben Miller, one of the captains appointed by the Twelve 
to organize a company of one hundred at Ottawa for the 
western journey. A set debate between Strang and Miller 
was arranged in the presence of some sixty of the Saints, 
but after Strang had talked four hours “in his rapid man- 
ner” Miller confessed himself unable to answer the argu- 
ments that had been advanced.f Therewith the hopes of 
the Twelve for at least one of the migrating companies went 
glimmering. The result of a series of such meetings over 
northern Illinois was that many who had been loyal ad- 
herents of the Twelve subscribed to Strang’s teachings, 
while others, bewildered by the conflicting claims of Strang 
and the Twelve, adopted a questioning state of mind and 
a readiness to demand of the latter that they give proof of 


* The Indians. + Chronicles of Voree, pp. 52 ff. . 


Ip) Tue Kincpom oF St. JAMES 


their authority to lead the Church. High Priest Reuben 
Miller, who had come to Ottawa to organize a company and 
lead it into the wilderness, returned instead to Nauvoo 
bearing a warning from Strang to the Twelve that they 
desist from further iniquitous procedure and present them- 
selves at Voree before April 7 to undergo trial upon the 
charges that would be preferred against them.* 

This was carrying the war into Africa with a vengeance. 
Among the men to whom this ukase was delivered} by a 
man who was himself but a neophyte in the Church were 
several of the ablest leaders Mormonism has ever produced. 
They were not likely to be greatly awed by the summons, 
but they could not fail to be concerned over the evident 
impression Strang’s teachings were producing upon many 
of their followers. The depth of this concern is evidenced 
by the bitterness of their language and the violence of their 
measures toward Strang. On January 24, 1846, the Twelve, 
over the signature of Brigham Young as president of the 
Church, and Orson Hyde as clerk, addressed a lengthy letter 
of admonition and warning to the branches of the Church 
in the vicinity of Ottawa. In it Strang was described as 
“an excommunicated member of the church.” His “pre- 
tended”’ revelations and letter from Joseph Smith were 
characterized as “a base and wicked forgery,” and their 
author ‘‘a wicked liar.” 


* Chronicles of Voree, pp. 54-56. The letter of Reuben Miller to Strang, 
February 15, 1846 (cited supra, p. 20), discloses that Strang already had an 
active following in Nauvoo, and appeals to him for advice and direction as 
to what attitude the Saints shall assume with respect to the migration to 
the far West which was just being launched by the Twelve. 

+ They were Brigham Young, Heber C. Kimball, Parley P. Pratt, Orson

Woodruff, Lyman Wight, and Willard Richards. Page was shortly won 
over by Strang, while Lyman Wight embarked upon a church movement of 
his own. 

t According to the Chronicles of Voree, three hundred converts were won 
to Strang during the January mission into Illinois. 


THE STRUGGLE FOR THE SUCCESSION 22 


Is it not surprisingly strange [the warning continued], that Joseph 
Smith should appoint a man to succeed him in the presidency of the 
church some seven or ten days before his death, and yet not tell it 
to the High Council, nor to any of the authorities of the church . . . 
If Mr. Strang had received his appointment from Joseph, why did 
he not come here upon the death of Joseph and take charge of the 
church, and lead them to Voree 2? Because his own guilty heart would 
not let him come near the Furnace of trial . . . Is it not surprisingly 
strange that when Sidney Rigdon was tried and cut off from the 
church for his lying visions and revelations to secure the presidency, 
no one arose to declare that Joseph Smith had written a letter of 
appointment to J. J. Strang [MS torn: appointing him to the vacant 
place ?] ? No! Because he [MS torn: never wrote such a letter ?] 

But Rigdon had his John C. Bennett to manufacture revelations 
for him purporting to come from Joseph Smith appointing him 
[Rigdon] to be his [Joseph’s] successor: so also, Strang has un- 
questionably had his accomplice in fixing up his letter of appoint- 
ment. The two breathe the same spirit, for by one spirit were they 
both indited. And I say unto you, beloved brethren, that Joseph 
Smith never wrote or caused to be written Strang’s letter of appoint- 
ment. It is a lie—a forgery—a snare. And let this testimony be had 
in remembrance before God, and let it stand against him and you 
if you shall persist in following him. Our garments are clear of your 
blood. [After much further argument designed to establish the au- 
thority of the Twelve to lead the church, the warning concludes] : 
Now Brethren, if you shall think propper to listen to the falsehoods 
of Mr. Strang after this letter is laid before you, Bro. Miller is in- 
structed to cut you off from the church. We hope you will see your 
error and flee from it, and save yourselves from the snare of decep- 
tion and the Devil.* 


Despite this pious hope and urgent admonition, Orson 
Hyde of the Twelve, to insure the thwarting of Strang’s 
designs upon the faithful, shortly embarked upon the thorny 
pathway of divine revelation. Even Joseph Smith’s power 

* Address (MS) of Brigham Young, on behalf of the Twelve, “To the 


branches of the church in the neighborhood of Ottawa,” January 24, 1846, 
preserved in Strang MSS. 


24 Tue Kincpom oF ST. JAMES 


of announcing revelations was put under censorship after 
the establishment of Nauvoo, and Brigham Young, despite 
much urging, steadily refused throughout his long career 
as ruler of the Saints to indulge in revelations.* Hyde’s 
revelation, dated March 14, 1846, was put forth with af- 
fected modesty for the benefit of the Church of Latter Day 
Saints, every member of which was “‘left to judge through 
whom it was given and from what source it came.” 
Notwithstanding this humble introduction, the Lord, 
through his mouthpiece, Orson Hyde, left no room for doubt 
concerning the character of Strang’s pretensions: 


Evil men ambitious of power, must needs arise among you, and 
they shall be led by their own self-will and not by me . . . Behold 
James J. Strang hath cursed my people by his own spirit and not 
by mine. Never, at any time have I appointed that wicked man to 
lead my people, neither by my own voice, nor by the voice of my 
servant Joseph Smith, neither by the voice of mine angel: but he 
hath sought to deceive and Satan helpeth him: but before of old 
was he one that was ordained to gather the tares of the field, and 
mine angels have chosen him to do it because he was a wicked man, 
even as Judas was chosen to betray his Lord. But his spirit and ambi- 
tion shall soon fail him, and then shall he be called to judgment 
and receive that portion which is his mete. And his treacherous fol- 
lowers who have forsaken the counsel of their brethren and turned 
from the covenants of their God, and have cast aside the tenderest 
ties, must drink from a bitter cup. 


“Strangism is dead here,’ wrote Hyde in a note trans- 
mitting a copy of his revelation to one of the Ottawa Saints, 
“and some who were carried away with it for a little time 
have returned to their church and confessed their errors and

sins.”* The tactics of the Twelve by which it was killed (it 
will shortly appear that this announcement was much too 
optimistic) are not without interest. Hard upon Strang’s 
summons to the Twelve to appear before him for trial, came 
Moses Smith to Nauvoo, intent upon advocating his leader’s 
cause in the Temple itself. Prevented by the Mormon 
leaders from entering the sacred edifice, he mounted a large 
stone and conducted an open-air meeting, until driven into 
flight by a Brighamite mob.f Such measures on the part of 
the Twelve only served to increase the public interest in 
Smith’s message. Changing their course, therefore, they gave 
him a hearing in the Temple. At the conclusion of his ad- 
dress their ablest speakers were put forward to refute and 
confound him; after which, denying him an opportunity to 
reply, a vote was carried, amidst the “clamor and scream- 
ing’ of the assembled congregation, cutting him off from 
the Church and forbidding anyone to hear him or any other 
advocate of Strang, or even to read the Voree Herald. 
Notwithstanding, Smith and those who had come with 
him continued for some time to advocate the cause of Strang 
in Nauvoo and the country round about, and they succeeded 
in turning many from the “delusions of Brighamism.”’ In- 
cluded in the number were some of the most important 
officials of the Church at Nauvoo. From the ranks of the 
Twelve came John E. Page, while William Smith, brother 
of the martyred Joseph and “‘chief patriarch” of the Church, 
also subscribed to the claims of Prophet Strang. Of more 
solid worth than either of these was William E. Marks, 
president of the stake of Nauvoo. William Smith was at 
best a poor creature, who before long was to desert Strang 
as he was now deserting the Twelve. But it was a distinct 


* Thid. 
+See account of Smith’s life in Voree Herald, June 14, 1849; Chronicles 
of Voree, pp. 58-60. 


26 Tue Kincpom oF St. JAMES 


triumph for Strang’s cause that along with William the 
whole Smith family (with the single exception of Joseph’s 
widow), including venerable Lucy Smith, mother of the 
founder of Mormonism, adhered to Strang’s cause and pub- 
licly certified their belief in his appointment at the hands 
of Joseph.* 

At the beginning of April, 1846, a general church con- 
ference was held at Voree, and here the work of organizing 
the Strangite Church was carried to completion. Of the 
Twelve, John E. Page, having made his peace with Strang, 
was recognized as a member in good standing of the Strang- 
ite council of twelve, and the case of Wilford Woodruff was 
laid over because of his absence on mission to England. The 
nine contumacious apostles (there was one vacancy in the 
ranks of the Twelve), having ignored Strang’s summons, 
were put to trial with all due solemnity, and in their absence 
were cut off from the Church, their priesthood taken away, 
and themselves “delivered over to the buffetings of Satan.” 
It is a sad commentary on Mormonism that the worst accu- 
sations ever leveled at its adherents by an angry and fre- 
quently unjust gentile world can be matched line for line 
with similar accusations made by the Saints themselves in 
the course of their interminable factional and personal con- 
troversies. The Saints have long complained, and with 
justice, that the world is ever ready to believe all manner of 
evil against them; but they seem seldom to have realized 
that their own testimony against one another affords color 
to the worst of gentile accusations. In the trial of the nine 
apostles, they were formally charged, among other things, 


*See certificate signed by members of the Smith family, printed in

+ Conference minutes in Chronicles of Voree, pp. 72 ff. 


THE STRUGGLE FOR THE SUCCESSION 27 


with “abolishing the liberty of speech and the press by com- 
mand and violence” ; “systematically plundering the church 
of its property for the use of themselves and their favorites” ; 
“teaching that polygamy, fornication, adultery, and con- 
cubinage are lawful and commendable”; “that lying, to 
build up the church of God, is justifiable, and that secret 
wickedness will not be brought into judgment, and carrying 
out these principles in constant practice.’ And all these 
charges were formally sustained by the testimony of Mor- 
mon witnesses, and unanimously voted proved by the church 
council which sat as a jury. 

The detailed testimony* by which these dreadful accusa- 
tions were sustained sheds interesting light upon the ab- 
normal state of mind and of morals which prevailed among 
the leaders of the Church at this time—the more convincing 
because it was largely given by men who had themselves 
participated in the activities described. 

In the matter of church doctrine, Jehiel Savage related 
that Orson Hyde had interrupted a religious discussion with 
the assertion that Mormons had nothing to do with the Book 
of Doctrines and Covenants; they were “not to be governed

time. 

“John P. Green, one of the witnesses to the letter containing James’ ap- 
pointment, was mysteriously missing about the time that the Twelve set 
themselves up in Nauvoo. 

“Perhaps numerous ones have disappeared in the same way. There was a 
gang of thieves and robbers scattered through that portion of country adja- 
cent to Nauvoo; and several of them were known as Mormons, and were in 
favor with Brigham.” 


28 Tue Kincpom oF St. JAMES 


any more by the Letter but by the Spirit.” John E. Page, 
but recently a fellow member of the Twelve with Hyde, 
affirmed that the latter, after teaching a confessedly false 
interpretation of scripture, justified it as policy. Savage 
added that in a like case Brigham Young had said, “I don’t 
know and don’t care whether it is correct or not, so good 
grows out of it.” Another witness quoted Brigham as saying 
he “would not give a straw” for the Book of Covenants, and 
still another, that it was no sin to do anything, even to kill 
aman, to save a brother who was in danger; as many women 
as would cling to his garments he would save, ““do what they 
might.” 

Such teaching in the realm of church doctrine naturally 
prepared the minds of the faithful for corresponding stand- 
ards of conduct. As to this, the testimony showed that 
the Twelve had hesitated at no measure, however extreme, 
to enforce their will at Nauvoo. The police, said T. S. Ed- 
wards, numbered two or three hundred members. They 
were organized as deacons with a bishop at their head, and 
Brigham had charged the bishops to see that the deacons 
did their duty. The purpose of this organization of the 
deacons James M. Adams explained to be ‘‘to consecrate 
property” for the benefit of the Church. By way of illus- 
tration he related that an apostate had a large fat hog. 
Adams and another “brother” butchered the hog and di- 
vided it. In like manner they appropriated a heifer and a 
steer, sending both hides and most of the beef to the Temple. 
Further details of extensive stealing of oxen and other 
property were given, and the witness affirmed that it was 
the duty of the deacons, if any apostates or enemies lived 
near by, “to get them into their homes and call in help and 
put them out of the way.” 

This general accusation of the employment of violence 


THE STRUGGLE FOR THE SUCCESSION 29 


by the church leaders in the prosecution of their purposes 
was supported by ample and picturesque testimony. The 
police, said C. B. Thompson, were publicly under the entire 
control of the Twelve, and the moment the Twelve became 
dissatisfied with anyone he was put out. There was an 
institution, testified several witnesses, known as “Aunt 
Peggy.” “Aunt Peggy” was a very severe woman, and it 
was common to say by way of threat ‘‘we will deliver you 
over to Aunt Peggy.” Brigham proposed to deliver over 
to her Doctor Charles, ‘“‘a very reputable man of whom no 
evil could be said.’’ However, he was “‘whittled” instead, 
and although he appealed to the Twelve for protection 
against the whittlers, the protection was denied by Brigham. 
Whenever any opposition was manifested toward the au- 
thority of the Twelve, Young and all the others would 
throw out “hints and innuendoes” that violent measures 
would be employed against the trouble makers. ‘“They had 
better look out,” Young and Kimball were in the habit of 
saying, “TI’ll send Aunt Peggy after them.’’ When anyone 
became disaffected “Aunt Peggy was there,” and mouths 
were sealed up. Kimball denounced one Lambert Lyman, 
announced that his priesthood should be taken from him, 
and called on the police “to put on the whole armour and 
take care of him’’; “he has not been seen since,”’ the wit- 
ness significantly concludes. 

The “whittling” and “whistling” societies were evidently 
close kin to Aunt Peggy. They were “‘set going” by the 
high council of the Twelve. At a meeting of the high priests’ 
quorum Kimball said if anyone present should reveal any- 
thing that was said he would lose his head, “even if he 
[Kimball] had to take it off himself.” It was then proposed 
that a man named Madison be put out of the way by some 
means, and one of those on the stand proposed that C. C. 


30 Tue Kincpom oF ST. JAMES 


Rich “grease and swallow” him. A motion to this effect was 
made, but for some reason it was dropped without a vote. 
A week later the witness saw Madison going out of town 
“whittled by about twenty men with long bowie knives.” 
They were kicking him down, rolling him in the mud, etc., 
for three-quarters of a mile. 

To gentile readers the rite of “greasing and swallowing” 
also seems to call for explanation. It meant “tying one end 
of the rope to one and the other to a stone and sinking it 
in the river.” The loyalty to the Twelve of C. C. Rich, 
who had been nominated to perform the rite on Madison, 
was attested by his election in December, 1847, to mem- 
bership in the Twelve on the occasion when Young formally 
assumed the First Presidency. Analogous to the custom of 
“oreasing and swallowing” was the doctrine, taught by 
mere innuendo of “making a sacrifice of stinking meat to 
the gentiles and making catfish bait.” 

In the light of such testimony Strang’s omission (with 
which the Twelve did not neglect to taunt him) to show 
his head at Nauvoo becomes easily understandable. In the 
effort to minimize any movement in his favor his spokes- 
men were “cut off from the church without opportunity for 
discussion or debate,” and any attempt to consider or in- 
vestigate his claims was sternly frowned upon. Orson Hyde 
moved that “if any man investigate Strangism or talk with 
them on the subject” he should be cut off, and without 
putting the motion to a vote the audience shouted “Amen!” 
Although Apostle Page had not heard the advice “to cut 
off the tails of the Strangites just behind the ears,”’ he was 
well satisfied that there was much danger of the ‘“Sundial’’ 
being accorded this treatment. Although no explanation of 
this term is recorded, it seems evident that it was employed 
as a euphemism for Prophet Strang. 


CHAPTER III 


Early Progress and Converts 


r MA HE success of the campaign for followers which 
Strang was waging depended upon his ability to 
convince the great body of the Saints that upon 

him, rather than upon the Twelve, the crown of the mar- 

tyred Joseph had descended. With the Twelve in complete 
possession of the field at Nauvoo, and with such forceful 
leaders as Brigham Young, Orson Hyde, and Willard Rich- 
ards among their number, the magnitude of this task is 
apparent. Yet one tremendous advantage rested with Strang 
in the combat for the headship of the orphaned flock. The 
Mormon Church owed its existence to a prophet and seer, 
who was presumed to be the mouthpiece of God to finite 
men; the entire church organization and faith was centered 
in the existence of a prophet in the flesh, and the Saints 
were accustomed to sing lustily, in their religious gatherings, 
the hymn beginning “A church without a Prophet Is not the 
church for me.” Now Joseph was gone, and no one save 
Strang even pretended to have any divine commission to 
succeed him. The Twelve under Young’s guidance boldly 
usurped the vacant headship, and defended the usurpation 
with such arguments as they were able to muster. But the 
illogic of this position was such that it could not escape the 
attention even of a zealot. Brigham Young refused to issue 

revelations at all, and Orson Hyde’s sole essay in this di- 

rection was given out with a painful lack of self-assurance. 

Strang’s taunt that the Brighamites had ceased to sing “A 

church without a Prophet,” could not be answered, and the 

difficulty was finally solved (and at the same time all the 


By Tue Kincpom oF St. JAMES 


prior arguments of the Twelve on the subject were repu- 
diated) by the election of Young as President in December, 
1847. 

Our present concern is with the early months of 1846 
when Strang’s advocacy of his prophetic pretensions began 
for the first time to win any considerable hearing. Honest 
and simple-minded zealots aside, it was obvious that only 
those Saints would enrol under Strang’s banner who were 
disgruntled, for one reason or another, with the ruling group 
at Nauvoo. Equally obvious is it that some of the conspicu- 
ous leaders must be won over if any progress was to be made 
with the more simple-minded Saints. Of disgruntled leaders 
there was no lack for during Joseph Smith’s life there had 
been an almost constant succession of apostasies and excom- 
munications, and under the ruthless rule of the Twelve in 
the months following Smith’s death these processes had 
operated with accelerated speed. 

The conversion of Reuben Miller, noted in the preceding 
chapter, proved a conquest of much significance to Strang. 
Miller seems to have been a somewhat simple soul, whose 
religious zeal was only equaled by the instability of his 
judgment. When he returned to Nauvoo following his un- 
fortunate encounter with Strang at St. Charles, he proceeded 
at once to advocate vigorously the Prophet’s claims to the 
leadership of the Church.* In particular he pressed them 
upon John E. Page of the Twelve, who, on February 1, in- 
dited a long letter to Strang wherein he recounted his valiant 
labors in the Mormon vineyard and the sad way in which 
they had been requited. To resort to modern terminology, 
Page’s mental state presented a severe case of inferiority 
complex. 


* Strang MSS, letters of Miller to Strang, February 15, 1846, and of 
John E. Page to Strang, February 1, 1846. 


EARLY PROGRESS AND CONVERTS 39 


The last day of May next [he wrote] will be ¢en years since I 
first left my family, and my little all, to Preach the Everlasting 
gospel, from that time untill about one year since I have been in- 
cessantly employed in the vineyard, and have baptized more than 
one thousand souls. I began my work in extreem Poverty, and have 
suffered every privation immaginable, my family has been drag[g]ed 
through an Earthly hell in my absence, and I am as poor and 
destitute as when I first entered into the ministry. I have served 
this people in all diligence for ten years past, thinking most im- 
plicitly, that when ever I should return to any of the stakes of Zion 
to settle I should be reciprocated by the Church and its authorities 
and be sustained in my capacity equal with my bretheren of the same 
calling . . . I find my jet black hair faided to a silver gray, my 
lungs that was once like a lions is now hoarse and faltering, my 
bodyly system that once was like iron and steel for strength, is now 


more like a child’s, soft and measureably infirm . . . Infirm, I am 
reduced to the capacity of a mere menial servant; and [to] the most 
severe hardship to keep body and spirit together . . . If ever my 


present bands of restraint are cut assunder, and I get out from under 
the influence and power of a partial despotism, I am fully resolved 
to let loos[e] my small artillery in a war of words against false 
DEMCIplEs. 2. 


Jealousy of his fellow members of the Twelve, and bitter- 
ness over the inadequacy of the writer’s financial situation, 
is reflected in every line of the long communication, which 
readily serves to explain Page’s early acceptance of Strang’s 
claims, with the prospect held out thereby of a more com- 
fortable status in the new organization than he had ever 
attained in the old. 

Although this inroad upon the ranks of the Twelve was 
a distinct advantage to Strang at the time, in the long run 
the support of such a man as Page was of more than doubt- 
ful value. So, too, was the accession about the same time of 
John C. Bennett, a far more notable character than Page, 
to whose career we must now turn our attention. 


34 Tue Kincpom oF St. JAMES 


Bennett was a native of Massachusetts by birth, a physi- 
cian by profession, and an expert politician and demagogue 
by virtue of predilection and native endowment. Possessed 
of infinite mental resources, and endowed with great physi- 
cal energy and superb self-assurance, he assumed a com- 
manding réle in whatever circle his lot was cast. He had 
been for a time President of the Medical Faculty, and Pro- 
fessor of the Principles and Practice of Midwifery and the 
Diseases of Women and Children at Willoughby University 
of Lake Erie, and he was now adorning a similar professor- 
ship at the Literary and Botanico-Medical College of Ohio 
in Cincinnati. The period of his active affiliation with Mor- 
monism had been brief but exceedingly lurid. When the 
harassed Saints, fleeing before Missouri mobs and militia, 
had found asylum in Illinois, they had found it expedient 
to seek from the state authorities certain unusual govern- 
mental privileges and powers. The Springfield politicians, 
avid of Mormon votes, had proved extraordinarily com- 
plaisant. In particular Bennett, who was one of the state 
officials, saw in the situation an opportunity to promote his 
welfare by catering to the Mormon influence. It had been 
due chiefly to his political skill that the remarkable Nauvoo 
charter was lobbied through the legislature. Shortly there- 
after Bennett himself became a Mormon, and almost at 
once became the recipient of an astonishing shower of offi- 
cial appointments and favors. He was made Mayor of Nau- 
voo and Major General of the Legion, head of the Nauvoo 
University, and acting member of the First Presidency of 
the Church. That he had possessed some tangible assurance 
of these developments at the time he was pushing the Nau- 
voo charter through the legislature seems highly probable. 
It is probable, too, that to him, rather than to Smith, was 
due the creation of the Legion and the University; he took 


EARLY PROGRESS AND CONVERTS es 


no less pride in his military than in his medical attainments, 
and his interest in institutions of learning was as active as 
the Prophet’s was quiescent. In short, Bennett was possessed 
of a fertile mind and an insatiable penchant for directing 
everything within the orbit of his observation, and to this 
propensity were ascribable alike his meteoric rise to fame 
and power, and his sudden downfall therefrom. 

A year and a half sufficed for the ‘“‘General-in-Chief” 
and ‘Prime Minister’ of Mormonism to run his course. 
In the spring of 1842, over issues that have never been 
publicly explained or avowed, Bennett ‘withdrew’ from 
all connection with the Saints. Extraordinary measures were 
resorted to to keep the quarrel from the public, and by 
especial direction of the Prophet to the General Clerk and 
Recorder of the Church, Bennett was given a statement sig- 
nifying that he had “withdrawn” his membership from the 
Church with “the best of feelings subsisting between all 
parties,”* together with a certificate of good character by 
Sidney Rigdon. 

These concessions availed nothing, however, to silence 
the tongue of the fallen Prime Minister concerning his erst- 
while brethren. Upon leaving Nauvoo he at once began the 
serial publication of an exposé of Mormonism in the col- 
umns of the Sangamon Journal, which together with much 
additional material, came out as a book at Boston the same 
year, entitled History of the Saints; or an Exposé of Joe 
Smith and Mormonism. In this astonishing volume Bennett 
claimed he had never believed in Mormonism, but, realizing 
the viciousness of the movement, had joined it as a spy with 
the set purpose of gaining confidential information to be 
used later in exposing its iniquities to the world. That Ben- 


* Strang MSS, letter of Bennett to Strang, March, 1846. The letter is 
undated, but is postmarked March 23. 


36 Tue Kincpom oF ST. JAMES 


nett was himself a demagogue and a rascal is abundantly 
clear, and no particular faith need be placed in this ex- 
planation. Yet if even a tithe of the things set forth in his 
exposé of Mormonism as practiced at Nauvoo were true, it 
should have sufficed eternally to condemn the Church in the 
eyes of all upright men; if false, as adherents of the Church 
have uniformly claimed, it should forever have destroyed 
the standing of Bennett with those to whose faith he had 
turned traitor. 

On March g, 1846, notwithstanding, Strang addressed 
to Bennett, now at Cincinnati, an overture to enrol under 
his banner. The correspondence that ensued between the 
two men should have sufficed to forewarn even one less 
omniscient than a prophet of God is presumed to be, of 
the trouble which would be certain to follow upon any alli- 
ance with such a man as Bennett. He manifested much eager- 
ness to join forces with Strang, accompanied by a like in- 
sistence upon being accorded the same rank and confidence 
which Joseph had reposed in him at Nauvoo.* “Joshua, the 
son of Nun, was the ‘General-in-Chief’ to Moses,” he wrote, 
“Joab to David—and my humble self to Joseph—and I 
hope to be so to you.” 


You have inspired me with new life and vigor [runs another 
communication] and in prospect of resuming my old station, I feel as 
tho’ I was a “tower of Strength” in the good cause. For about four 
years past “I have not been myself,” for my spirits have been de- 
pressed and gloomy; but I have felt eke a young lion let loose ever 
since I heard of your glorious movement. While you will be the 
Moses of the last days, I hope to be your Joshua, my old position. 
While you stand as the Crowned Imperial Primate, I will be as an 
upright line before the throne—your General-in-Chief. 


Yet his zeal did not suffice to prevent him laying down the 


* Strang MSS, letters between Strang and Bennett, March-June, 1846.

terms on which he would enlist under Strang’s banner. He 
wanted no temporary “‘vascillating’’ appointment; “what- 
ever place I am to occupy,” he wrote, “I wish it to be by 
revelation and commandment.” 

The writer’s former intimacy with Joseph Smith justifies 
the observation that he was not wholly ignorant of the proc- 
esses by which revelations were evoked. However this may 
be, with his very first letter to Strang, Bennett began to 
manifest his qualifications for the prime ministry by in- 
structing his chief what measures he should adopt and what 
appointments to office he should make. 


I advise you to obtain a charter for the “Voree University of 
Wisconsin,” as soon as possible [he wrote on March 20]. Do not 
fail in this. It would be better to call it the “University of Wiscon- 
sin,” (if you can get it passed by that name), to be located at Voree. 
This is very important. 


In subsequent letters this project was further enlarged 
upon. Without awaiting the granting of the charter—with- 
out, indeed, affording Strang time to receive the initial 
proposal for a university—Bennett had selected, not to say 
elected, one of its professors, in the person of Doctor Fagen, 
who, it transpired later, was his partner in Cincinnati. 


He is a splendid Surgeon and Physician, and I wish you to receive . 
him cordially and put him into business, so that he can acquire repu- 
tation and influence, preparatory to my coming on, so that he can 
take the Professorship of Anatomy and Surgery. Take him into the 
church and to your bosom, and treat him as you would me. I shall 
send him to you at Burlington. Retain him by all means, if you ever 
wish me with you. 


Between giving directions concerning various problems, 
present or anticipated, the General-in-Chief found time to 
explain to Strang the occasion of his withdrawal from the 


38 Tue Kincpom oF ST. JAMES 


Church, which now, for the first time, after the lapse of 
almost ninety years, will be given public exposition. The 
difficulty with Joseph originated in a spirit of jealousy on 
the part of certain of the apostles (Brigham Young, Willard 
Richards, and John Taylor), who were fearful that Bennett 
would eclipse them in the affections of the people; so, “by 
a system of duplicity, fraud and corruption,” they succeeded 
in souring the mind of Joseph Smith toward Bennett, and 
producing an open rupture. “This I have ever regretted,” 
Bennett asserted, “but circumstances compelled me to fight, 
and sustain myself by recrimination.” The truth of this 
explanation he called upon William Smith to attest. ‘He 
knew the circumstances, and, in the nobleness of his soul, 
he has always been my fast friend.” “All I ever said against 
any member of the church,” he wrote in a later letter, “was 
in self-defense . . . I have loved the church for many years. 
I love tt now. I shall devote all my energies to revive it.” 

We have here a calm confession that Bennett’s entire 
amazing crusade against the Church was insincere and dis- 
honest. At the same time he did not refrain from the grav- 
est charges against the three apostles whom he held re- 
sponsible for his difficulties at Nauvoo. 


I was with the church in its most prosperous days [he affirmed], 
and Joseph and I were only severed by the corruptness of Willard 
Richards, Brigham Young and John Tailor. Their course was dam- 
nable—they wished the death of Joseph that they might rule. I 
could and would at all times have saved Joseph and the church, had 
I been applied to, and I should have been applied to but for Willard 
Richards. 


That Strang should select, as he proceeded to do, a man 

with Bennett’s record for his chief adviser and ‘Prime 

Minister,” augured ill for the Garden of Peace at Voree. 
But it was not alone the General-in-Chief whose accession 


EARLY PROGRESS AND CONVERTS 29 


was of ominous import to the future peace of Voree. Among 
the Saints whom Strang was seeking to win by holding out 
the prospect of official appointments were some of the most 
conspicuous trouble makers in the history of Mormonism— 
among others, William E. McLellin, William Smith, and 
George J. Adams. McLellin was characterized as “a splen- 
did man,” whom Bennett meant to bend every effort to at- 
tach to Strang. How inept this characterization was we will 
shortly have occasion to note. William Smith was a “noble 
soul,” whom Bennett loved devotedly. With great practical 
shrewdness, he urged that he be invited to accept the patri- 
archal see at Voree; and that he be urged to bring with him 
“his mother, with the mummies and papyrus,” together with 
the bodies of Joseph and Hyrum, as all these things would 
have an astonishing effect in congregating the people at 
Voree. 

The allusion to the papyrus and the mummies recalls one 
of Joseph Smith’s most amusing impostures. While he was 
still at Kirtland, a traveling showman came along, exhibit- 
ing, among other objects, two or three Egyptian mummies. 
Smith had them purchased for the Church, and when it was 
discovered that some papyri had been acquired along with 
the mummies, the Prophet proceeded to “translate” them. 
In due time he announced that one of the writings was by 
Abraham of Old Testament fame, and thereafter until 
Smith’s death the mummies were treasured and exhibited 
(for a price) to curious visitors. Unfortunately for the 
credit of Smith’s translation, however, he published along 
with it a picture of the document he had translated; thereby 
he enabled certain future scholars who really possessed some 
knowledge of Egyptology to expose the measure of Smith’s 
pretended knowledge of the subject. 

The character of George J. Adams, whom Bennett per- 


40 Tue Krincpom oF St. JAMES 


suaded Strang to appoint as councilor,* was no less curious 
than that of Bennett himself. Of his early history, or the 
date of his conversion to Mormonism, we have found but 
little information. His letters reveal him as an uncultured, 
turbulent man, boastful, passionate, and crude. He was an 
actor by profession, and an orator of no mean ability. By 
the year 1841 he had become enough of a zealot to join 
with Orson Hyde who claimed to have a divine commission 
to carry the gospel of Mormonism to Jerusalem.+ Hyde 
persevered in his mission, and in a long prayer on the Mount 
of Olives commended the Church to God and dedicated the 
land for the gathering of Israel. Adams, however, seems to 
have fallen by the wayside, for Hyde distinctly states that 
he performed this interesting act of devotion alone. In 1843 
Hyde and Adams were again appointed upon a foreign mis- 
sion, this time to Russia. This mission seems not to have 
materialized,t and we next hear of Adams addressing a 
sixteen-page letter to “‘his excellency John Tyler . . . touch- 
ing the signs of the times and the Political Destiny of the 
world.” Like Bennett, Adams was excommunicated by the 
Saints and he was living in Cincinnati when Strang ap- 
pealed to Bennett to join his movement. Undoubtedly 
Bennett and Adams discussed the matter and the latter 
promptly wrote to the Prophet expressing a desire to receive 
his old “place, standing, and appointments” in the Church.§ 


* The two associates of the Mormon prophet in the First Presidency are 
known as councilors. Bennett urged Strang to appoint Rigdon and Smith 
to this office, with himself as “supernumary” counselor; if Rigdon should 
decline the offer (as was anticipated) Bennett requested the place for him- 
self. Strang’s First Presidency, as thus constituted, was composed of himself, 
Bennett, and Adams.
