He explained that his excommunication had been the work 
of his enemies among the Twelvites, and warned Strang 
not to credit their ‘“‘base phalshoods” concerning him. Their 
charges that he was addicted to drink and unduly fond of 
women were animated by jealousy over his success in draw- 
ing crowds “whereaver and wheneaver I lift up my voice.” 

A warier prophet than Strang might have hesitated long 
before admitting to his innermost councils such a man as 
Adams disclosed himself to be. Whether because of Ben- 
nett’s influence or of the necessities of his situation, however, 
Strang accepted Adams’ overture, and promised that he 
would be sustained in his office “according to the ordination 
of Joseph and Hyrum.”’* “As for those complaints that you 
say are made against you,” he added, “I have only to say 
now that if you are guilty you must do so no more.” Anew 
era had dawned upon the Church, and there was to be no 
“raking up of old scores.” Equipped with this easily granted 
pardon, Adams entered upon his new role of high councilor 
in the Strangite organization; long after Bennett and Mc- 
Lellin and William Smith had apostatized and been damned 
with the ban of excommunication, Adams continued stead- 
fast in his support of Strang, being chiefly engaged in prose- 
lyting work in the East. The frequent reports he sent back 
to Voree pictured his preaching as attended by vast and 
enthusiastic crowds; but reports of another sort were also 
being sent the Prophet, disclosing all too clearly that the 
“Boanerges of the Latter Day Saints” had not taken seri- 
ously Strang’s injunction to refrain from drunkenness and 
fornication. The persistent retention of such a man in the 
highest position in the gift of the Church affords a fair 
measure of the moral standards to which the Saints in this 
period subscribed. The allegiance of Adams was from the 


* Tbid., letter of Strang to Adams, May 5, 1846. 


42 Tue Kincpom oF St. JAMES 


beginning of more than doubtful benefit to the Church, and 
we shall see that his apostasy in the end was a cause of 
infinite trouble and danger to Strang. 

With the preliminary work of reorganizing the Church 
accomplished, the Prophet proceeded to plan an invasion 
of the East and of Europe to wrest from the Twelve the 
sources of Mormon support in those regions. The first ob- 
jective in this campaign was the stake at Kirtland, Ohio, the 
earliest seat of Mormonism. Thither Strang proceeded in 
early August, accompanied by a group of supporters and 
equipped with a letter of commendation addressed “to all 
the Saints scattered abroad,” in the name of the “high 
priests, elders, presidents and general authorities” of the 
Church of Latter Day Saints. Under the spell and persist- 
ence of Strang’s eloquence* the opposition of the Kirtland 
Saints completely melted. The stake was organized anew in 
the Strangite interest and the Prophet gained possession of 
the Mormon Temple. Here at Kirtland, too, a deputation 
was appointed to visit the British Isles to wrest from the 
Brighamite faction the allegiance of the English Saints. 
Included in its membership was Martin Harris, one of the 
three original witnesses of the Book of Mormon.+ Missions 
to the Pacific Islands, to Russia, and to India were also 
approved by the Kirtland conference, and Strang was given 
full power to carry them into execution. 

From Kirtland, accompanied by G. J. Adams and other 
associates, Strang proceeded eastward to Philadelphia, New 
York, and Boston, everywhere setting the local churches “‘in 
order,” which meant, of course, winning them to an accept- 
ance of Strang’s prophetic calling and a repudiation of the 
Brighamite leadership. At Philadelphia Strang encountered 


* On one occasion he preached for eight hours in his “most rapid manner.” 
Chronicles of Voree, p. 102. 


+ For the documents see Voree Herald, August, 1846.

Orson Hyde and John Taylor of the Twelve and promptly 
challenged them to a public debate on the respective validity 
of their claims and his own to the headship of the Church. 
The boast Strang was wont to make that he never refused 
an argument might almost be repeated by Mormons gen- 
erally, for from its foundation the Church has thriven on 
disputations with unbelievers. In the present instance, how- 
ever, Hyde and Taylor had no desire to close with Strang. 


After Lucifer was cut off and thrust down to hell [they replied], 
we have no knowledge that God ever condescended to investigate 
the subject or right of authority with him. Your cause has been 
disposed of by the rightful authorities of the Church. Being satis- 
fied with our own power and calling, we have no disposition to ask 
from whence yours came. 


Hyde and Taylor had gone on their present mission to the 
churches expressly to combat the claims of Strang;* the 
latter promptly turned this rencounter to advantage by pub- 
lishing the correspondence in the Voree Herald, together 


* Hyde’s present disinclination to discuss the claims of Strang seem some- 
what odd in view of the readiness he had displayed to discuss them in his 
reputedly divine revelation of March 14, preceding. One may reasonably 
wonder whether his knowledge of Strang’s readiness in debate and his 
powers of invective was not responsible for the disinclination to encounter 
him. In the summer of 1845 Strang had pronounced upon the group of 
leaders at Nauvoo this curse, with which Hyde was, of course, familiar: “as 
for those who, as gospel ministers, have assumed to teach such damning, 
soul-destroying doctrines (that deceit, fraud, lying, perjury, plundering un- 
believers, polygamy, fornication and adultery are required by the command 
of God in the upbuilding of his kingdom). In the name of God and the 
Lord Jesus Christ; may their bones rot in the living tomb of their flesh; 
may their flesh generate from its own corruptions a loathsome life for 
others; may their blood swarm with a leprous life of motelike, ghastly 
corruption feeding on flowing life, generating chilling agues and burning 
fevers. May peace and home be names forgotten to them, and the beauty 
they have betrayed to infamy; may it be to their eyes a crawling mass of 
putridity and battening corruption ; its delicate hues a sickly light that flows 
from universal corruption; its auburn tresses the posthumous growth of 
temples of crawling worms; its fragrant breath the blast of perdition. With 
desires insatiate may each gratification turn to burning bitterness and glow- 
ing shame.” Voree Herald, April, 1846. 


44 Tue Kinepom oF St. JAMES 


with a further report of how Hyde had fled from G. J. 
Adams at Cincinnati, locking himself up in a stateroom of a 
river boat to avoid a meeting with that champion of Strang.* 
In Boston, too, Strang vainly sought an argument with 
Parley P. Pratt, another of the Brighamite Twelve. The 
“Archer of Paradise,’ as he was known among his associ- 
ates, not only refused the argument, but according to Strang 
he was compelled, ‘between notes for borrowed money, 
writs for seducing wives, and warrants for adultery,” to 
flee the city in advance of the police. 

Viewing the results of this first invasion of the East, the 
Brighamite leaders at Nauvoo might well manifest a lively 
concern for the future of their cause and a corresponding 
bitterness toward their hated rival, for it seems apparent 
that Strang had made alarming progress toward capturing 
the Mormon strongholds from Illinois to the Atlantic. Quite 
different, however, was the outcome of the contest in Eng- 
land. To England proceeded Hyde, Taylor, and Parley P. 
Pratt, fresh from their encounters with the Strangite prose- 
lyters in Philadelphia and Boston.£ Hard on their heels 
followed Lester Brooks and Martin Harris of the Strangite 
deputation to the English Church, appointed at the Kirtland 
conference in August. The one really able member of this 
deputation, however, Moses Smith, had been kept at home 
by reason of a schismatic disaffection at Voree; Martin 
Harris was ignorant, vain, and foolish, a liability rather 
than an asset to any cause to which he might adhere, while 
Brooks proved totally incapable of coping with men of the 
character of Hyde and Taylor. 

Instead, therefore, of avoiding their opponents, as they 
had done in America, the Brighamite leaders sought to en- 


* Voree Herald, September, 1846. + Ibid., August, 1846. 
£ Liverpool Millennial Star, VIII, 92.

gage them in debate, heaping meanwhile, unmeasured abuse 
upon them and upon Prophet Strang, their leader, in the 
columns of the Liverpool Mé/lennéal Star, the organ of the 
Church in England.* No language however extreme, no 
comparisons however odious, were spared in the effort to 
overwhelm the Strangite delegation, and maintain intact 
the authority of the Twelve in Great Britain. “Polecats,” 
“swine,” and “sons of Satan” are but characteristic samples 
of the designations applied by these apostles of God to men 
who had been distinguished by years of faithful service in 
the selfsame Church these apostles essayed to lead. On 
November 15, 1846, the Star came out with a series of 
“Sketches of Notorious Characters,’ the subjects of the 
sketches being Strang, Brooks, and Harris. Strang was de- 
scribed as the 


successor of Sidney Rigdon, Judas Iscariot, Cain (the brother and 
murderer of Abel) & Co. Envoy Extraordinary and Minister Pleni- 
potentiary of His Most Gracious Majesty, Lucifer the I, assisted 
by his allied contemporary advisers, John C. Bennett, William 
Smith, G. J. Adams, and John E. Page, Secretaries of Legation. 


Brooks was represented as an elder in the Church of Ohio 
who had associated himself with a “notorious speculator” 
to cheat the Saints out of their farms by persuading them 
to exchange their improved Ohio farms for a larger area of 
wild land in Illinois. Only their implicit confidence in 
Brooks induced them to deal with the speculator, and when 
they got to Illinois they found he had no such lands and 
they had lost their farms “completely.” Thus Brooks had 
had the “honour of betraying the interests of his best friends 
into the hands of a consummate enemy,” and for this he was 
haled before the church authorities and ‘cursed before God 


* See editorial and other references in zd7d., VIII (October-December, 
1846), 92-175, especially pp. 93, 98, 120, 122-126, 137-138, 140, 185. 


46 Tue Kincpom oF St. JAMES 


and man.” Now he had come, as a Strangite apostle, to 
preach “purity, reformation, and good things” to the Saints 
in England. 

Of Martin Harris a still more damning account was 
given. One of the original witnesses to the Book of Mormon, 
he had yielded to the spirit and temptation of the Devil; 
“filled with the rage and madness of a demon,” he had 
flown from one thing to another as if bereft of reason. For 
this he had been expressly condemned of God through his 
mouthpiece, Prophet Smith. But there was no need to warn 
the Saints against him, ‘for his own unbridled tongue will 
soon show out specimens of folly enough to give any person 
a true index to [his] character.” In this the editor prophe- 
sied truly, for even Strang admits that all prospect of suc- 
cess of the English mission was destroyed through the folly 
of Harris.* The failure was of vast moment to the future of 
Mormonism. To the steady tide of reinforcements in men 
and money which flowed from England was largely due 
whatever measure of success was achieved by the Brigham- 
ite Saints in Utah; while Strang, almost at the beginning 
of his ministry, was completely shut out from this source of 
power and development. Two years later (May, 1848) a 
final appeal was addressed by the ‘“True Shepherd” of the 
flock “to the Saints emigrating from Great Britain and 
Ireland to America” to ignore the Godless usurpation of the 
Twelve, who would lead them to destruction, and to fore- 
gather into the fold prepared by God for them at Voree and 
Beaver Island.+ It was a powerful argument, but it fell, as 
might have been expected, upon deaf ears.

CHAPTER IV 


The Garden of Peace 


HE Prophet returned from his invasion of the East 

to the Garden of Peace at Voree to encounter a 

dangerous schismatic revolt on the part of a large 
section of those who had so recently flocked to his banner— 
the first of a long series of heresies which were to sadden 
his life and bring him to ruin in the end. One of the happiest 
characterizations ever perpetrated by Theodore Roosevelt 
was the designation of a certain type of radicals as the 
“lunatic fringe” of society. One can hardly read extensively 
in the literature of early Mormonism without becoming im- | 
pressed with the thought that he is dealing chiefly with this 
lunatic fringe. Men who had broken with the established 
order, both of government and religion; who believed im- 
plicitly in a Prophet, supposed to be in daily direct com- 
munion with God on subjects, oftentimes, of the most 
trivial character; who possessed the gift of tongues and the 
power of miraculous healing,—in a word, men who sub- 
mitted blindly their dearest interests in life to the dictates 
of a supposedly inspired autocrat, and to whom miraculous 
visions and supernatural visitations were matters of ordinary 
occurrence,—could hardly be noted for stability of judg- 
ment or conservatism of action. Added to this general tend- 
ency to bend to the latest breeze that blew was the un- 
doubted fact that numerous erratic individuals professed 
Mormonism for purely selfish reasons and were interested in 
the Church only to the extent that they hoped to make some- 
thing out of it. In the first flush of success Strang attracted 
many of this character to his standard. Most of them had 


48 Tue Kinecpom oF St. JAMES 


little to lose in any event, whether of reputation or of goods, 
and they joined the new movement in the hope of acquiring 
power or property, or both, as the case might be. When it 
speedily appeared that there was room for but one ruler in 
the Strangite organization, and that the Prophet had both 
the will and the ability to direct the activities of his fol- 
lowers, both the honest vagarists and the insincere self- 
seekers manifested their disappointment by schismatic re- 
volt. 

The first of these movements came to a head in the early 
autumn of 1846. To the gathering of the Saints at Voree 
had flocked as precious a band of visionaries and rascals as 
ever scuttled a ship or a reputation. Disappointed ambition 
finding room for expression under the guise of advocacy of 
various doctrinal vagaries was responsible for the revolt of 
the ‘‘Pseudos,” as it came to be known, in the summer and 
autumn of 1846. As early as the April conference conten- 
tion had been joined over the question whether Mormons 
who came over to the Strangite organization from the Brig- 
hamite or other factions should be required to submit to 
baptism and joining the Church anew. The commitments the 
Prophet had made to such men as Bennett, Adams, and 
Page required him to combat this proposal and by reason of 
his opposition it was defeated; but many of his followers, 
among them Aaron Smith, under whose guidance Strang 
had himself become a Mormon, opposed this decision of 
the question. The real reason for their opposition, it seems 
apparent, proceeded from their disinclination to be associ- 
ated with the type of men by whom Strang was surrounding 
himself. This distaste finds clear expression in an interesting 
letter to Strang from a correspondent at Ottawa, the scene 
of his initial proselyting triumph. Referring to Bennett and 


THE GARDEN OF PEACE AQ 


Adams she observed:* “You cannot be surprised that we 
are unwilling to fellowship men whose moral character is 
so bad that even the Twelve could not tolerate them.” 
The smoldering fires of discontent were fanned into open 
conflagration over an issue which Bennett precipitated upon 
the infant Church. “I have many things to tell you when I 
come [to Voree], that I cannot commit to paper—some very 
zmportant indeed,’ he had written the Prophet in one of 
his early letters.+ Soon after his arrival at Voree the ever 
resourceful General-in-Chief produced an apple of discord 
which shook the community to its foundation and for a time 
bade fair to bring early ruin upon Strang’s prophetic career. 
In the heyday of Bennett’s popularity at Nauvoo, Joseph 
Smith had brought forth a revelation which provided, 
among other things, for the establishment after his death 
of a “Halcyon Order,” the “pattern” of which was com- 
municated by Smith to Bennett with the charge upon the 
latter that he should proceed to establish the order after the 
anticipated death of the Prophet (with which the revelation 
dealt) should have taken place.t Bennett had apostatized a

year later, Smith was killed in June, 1844, and now, two 
years after this, Bennett returned to the Mormon fold as a 
member of the First Presidency of the Strangite Church, 
bringing with him his knowledge of Smith’s projected ‘‘Hal- 
cyon Order.” 

The secret dealings of God with his prophets it is not 
open to the historian to explore; but it seems a somewhat 
remarkable coincidence that promptly upon the arrival of 
Bennett, Strang received a lengthy revelation commanding 
the Saints, among other things, to build a temple at Voree, 
wherein, under his ministration, the “mysteries of the king- 
dom” should be revealed to them.* But the Prophet was 
enjoined to “take a great covenant” of all to whom he 
should teach these mysteries, and the wrath of God would 
fall upon all who should break the covenant they had taken. 
The work of establishing the new Order of the Illuminati, 
as it was known, began at once.f On July 6, Strang was 
created Imperial Primate of the Order and Bennett its 
General-in-Chief; while ‘“‘chevaliers,” ‘‘earls,’’ “marshals,” 
and “cardinals” following in due array.t The ceremony of 
the covenant-taking, however, seems to have been deferred 
for the present.

arrangements for the eastern tour described in the preceding 
chapter, and despite the threatening situation of affairs at 
home he felt compelled to hurry away to fulfil his engage- 
ments. Before starting he appealed to the faithful in a three- 
hour address of “pathetic and burning eloquence”* to re- 
frain from controversy and discord. Notwithstanding, the 
contention was soon renewed, the establishment of the secret 
order affording a new occasion of offense to the dissatisfied, 
and in the absence of the Prophet a plot was hatched for his 
overthrow. The leaders in this enterprise were Isaac Scott, 
Collins Pemberton, and William E. McLellin. Associated 
with them were men like Aaron Smith and Reuben Miller 
who had given ample testimony of their belief in Strang’s 
divine calling and had held important offices in his church. 
Pemberton journeyed to Chicago and there influenced the 
local stake of Mormonism to pass a vote rejecting Prophet 
Strang, “not as in transgression but as an imposter from 
the beginning.” + Miller brought out a pamphlet, entitled 
James J. Strang Weighed in the Balance and Found Want- 
ing, canvassing adversely the claims of Strang to the Presi- 
dency,¢ and charging that he and Bennett had instituted a 
grossly improper secret association at Voree. 

The church leaders retorted with sweeping denials, and, 
as usual in Mormon factional controversies, with charges of 
criminal and immoral practices against their opponents. 
Thus, Pemberton was characterized as “a lying, licentious, 
perfidious, pseudo-Mormon”’; others were “guilty of lascivi- 
ous and abominable iniquities, such as teaching and pro- 
claiming the spiritual wife doctrine.” All Pseudos were

infected with the spiritual wife doctrine and other vagaries,

Such a turmoil of schism as the one we have all too in- 
adequately sketched could hardly fail to be attended by seri- 
ous consequences to the infant Church. The more immediate 
consequence at Voree was a series of trials held during the 
closing months of 1846, which resulted in purging the 
Church of the principal offenders and the filling of the offices 


*It is difficult for one unversed in the literature of early Mormonism 
to realize the distressing frequency with which charges of most shocking 
moral delinquency were bandied about among the Saints. Perhaps no better 
illustration of this state of mind and the complacency with which it was 
viewed can be afforded than by reciting the case of Hazen Aldrich. On 
December 16, 1846, he was formally brought to trial before the Voree stake 
on charges preferred by General Bennett of “schism, heresy, mischief- 
making, diabolism, libel, and supererogating the spiritual wife doctrine.” 
The last charge, reduced to plain English, was that he had committed incest 
with his daughter. The charges were sustained by the testimony, among 
others, of Bennett and Strang himself, and by unanimous vote Aldrich was 
cut off from the Church (Chronicles of Voree, pp. 130-131). Yet two months 
later we find this man, who should ere now have been behind prison bars, 
addressing a letter, through the columns of the Voree Herald, to the Saints 
in commendation of Bennett and announcing the intention, upon recovery 
from a case of illness, of writing the “brethren a series of articles on the 
important subject of practical religion—such religion as we ought to live 
by and would wish to die by.” Voree Herald, February 11, 1847. Comment 
upon such a situation would seem to be as “supererogatory” as the crime of 
which Aldrich was convicted by his fellows.

they had held with new appointees. Abroad, the damage to 
the progress of the Strangite movement was extensive, as 
numerous reports and letters show. Two illustrations may be 
offered in this connection. Under the leading of William E. 
McLellin, the ‘“‘stormy petrel” of Mormonism, the Kirtland 
stake, which Strang had reorganized in August, 1846, within 
the short space of four months was so completely lost that 
in December following, God through his mouthpiece Strang, 
was moved to pronounce upon it a curse like unto that pro- 
nounced upon Sodom of old.* It should be ‘‘a waste and 
a desolation,” a den of wickedness which should not be 
cleansed until those who had polluted it were utterly de- 
stroyed. The substance of the apostates should waste, their 
houses fall, their land fail of its increase, and its waters 
dry up. It was to be the seat of Satan, the gathering place 
of unclean things. 

Far more serious in its ultimate effects than the loss of 
Kirtland was the wrecking of the English mission which had 
been appointed at the Kirtland conference. From that con- 
ference Aaron Smith had hurried home to play his part in 
the revolt we have just described. His brother Moses who 
was to have led the English mission followed him to Voree, 
seeking to placate him and win him from the schismatic 
course on which he was bent. In this Moses Smith failed, 
and in the resulting turmoil he was prevented by lack of 
funds and other obstacles from embarking upon the mis- 
sion.t The complete failure of Lester Brooks and Martin 
Harris to win over the English Saints, and the far-reaching 
consequences of this, both for the future of Strangism and 
of the Brighamite faction, have already been described.

By the beginning of 1847 the church leaders began to 
congratulate themselves that the fold had been purged of 
apostates and concord had been restored to the Church. But 
alas for the misnamed Garden of Peace, a renewal of the 
storm, more violent and far-reaching in its effects than the 
outbreak which had just been surmounted, was at hand. On 
New Year’s day the faithful at Voree indulged in a feast of 
food and love, in the course of which the Eucharist was 
administered and speeches were made by Strang, Bennett, 
Greenhow, James M. Adams, and U. C. H. Nickerson.* The 
next day, January 2, Strang resumed the work of establish- 
ing the Order of the Illuminati by introducing to the initi- 
ates his secret covenant. It did not remain secret very long, 
for the apostate band at Voree, promptly gaining possession 
of it, struck off two thousand copies and distributed them 
broadcast to all who might be interested.+ Further, they 
hotly pressed the charge through the columns of their tem- 
porary organ, the Elkhorn New Era, and other papers, that 
an undemocratic and improper secret order, whose members 
were banded together to carry on works of iniquity and to 
screen each other from justice, was being promoted by

Strang and Bennett at Voree. They ridiculed the sup-

The reply of the Prophet and his associates to these accu-

the debate which was waged so zealously concerning it. The 
Pseudo version of the covenant, far from being a “gross 
perversion,” erred chiefly through understatement. Had the 
oath as really administered to Strang’s followers been pub- 
lished to the world, the blow to his organization would have 
been far more crushing than was the one suffered from the 
Pseudo version of it. If, as all the officials of the Church at 
Voree solemnly claimed, it was not “repugnant to the Book 
of Doctrines and Covenants,” it was at any rate repugnant 
to the obligation of loyalty to the government of the United 
States. Beginning with a declaration of belief in God, Jesus 
Christ, the Church of Latter Day Saints, “and in James J. 
Strang, the Prophet of God,” after a pledge never to reveal 
the secrets of the Order, the oath continued: 


And I do further, in like manner, promise, covenant and swear 
that I will uphold sustain and obey the said James J. Strang and 
his lawful successors, if any he has, each in his time as the Imperial 
primate and actual sovereign Lord and King on Earth and as my 
true and lawful Sovereign wheresoever and in whatsoever kingdom 
state or dominion I may be; and in preference to the laws, com- 
mandments and persons of any other Kings, Potentates, or States 
whatsoever, and will yield obedience to the revelations he shall give; 
the Laws made by the Grand Council of Nobles of God’s Kingdom, 
with his concurrence; and the decrees he shal] make, as the Supreme 
Law, above and superceding all laws, obligations, and mandates of 
any other person, authority, or power whatsoever. 


By other clauses of the covenant the initiated renounced 
“without reserve and without regret,” “all allegiance to any 
and every other Potentate State and Nation”; and swore to 
keep and uphold order, subordination, and the rights of supe- 
riors, councilors, and nobles in the Order. Of the latter class 
(passing over Primate, Viceroys, and General-in-Chief) 
132 noblemen, ‘chosen from the various ranks of the No- 
bility” were to constitute the Grand Council, seventy of 


THE GARDEN OF PEACE 57 


whom should constitute a quorum for the transaction of 
business. The document concluded with a paragraph invok- 
ing the curse of God and man upon any who should be “‘so 
corrupt, treacherous, and abominable” as to violate the 
covenant he had taken. 

The Pseudos were correct, then, in charging that an un- 
democratic and improper secret order had been instituted at 
Voree, and the intended effect of the solemn official denials 
published by the church authorities was in plainest contra- 
vention of the truth. The leading architect of the opposition 
to Strang in the months following the affair of January 2 
(which was, in fact, but the prolongation of the schism of 
1846) was William E. McLellin, temporarily of Kirtland, 
Ohio. McLellin was a quack doctor and an expert dema- 
gogue, who in the course of a long career succeeded in ad- 
hering to and then deserting almost every faction in the 
history of Mormonism. He had wormed himself into the 
confidence of Prophet Smith in the early thirties, only to 
play the réle of an Iscariot in the hour of Smith’s direst 
danger at the hands of the Missouri mob.* When, in 1846, 
news of Strang’s movement was spread abroad, McLellin, 
who was seeking to eke out a living by the practice of medi- 
cine in Ohio, perceived a new field for exploitation. He had 
been of old a crony of John C. Bennett, and when that 
renegade Mormon floated into Voree and into his old posi- 
tion as one of the First Presidency, McLellin entertained 
visions of attaining a like success for himself. 

Although he was restored to his old apostleshipf at the

pointed and in less than three months, as we have already 
seen, he engineered the revolt at Kirtland. Prophet Strang, 
to whom he had so recently subscribed allegiance, was now 
found to be an impostor, and under heavenly guidance Mc- 
Lellin undertook to effect a new Mormon reorganization 
with David Whitmer as the divinely designated leader.* 
He started the Ensign of Liberty at Kirtland, which came 
out at infrequent intervals for one or two years. More im- 
portant, he carried the war into Voree, as Strang had carried 
it to Nauvoo in 1846, and reorganized the apostate group 
which had set up an organization some months earlier under 
the leadership of Aaron Smith. In October, 1847, McLellin 
visited Voree in person and there engaged in a joint public 
debate with Strang over the doctrines and facts at issue 
between them.f In this contest the superior intellect and 
refinement of Strang stood out in marked contrast to the 
ignorance and boorishness of his opponent. But it was a 
Pyrrhic victory at best, for all such discord, as Strang was 
statesman enough to perceive, but served to discredit Mor- 
monism generally in the eyes of the gentile world. 

Unless the mental state of the community at Voree was 
abnormal in the period under discussion, the moral state 
was deplorable. The presumption seems fair that there 
was a mixture of both of these conditions, although it is 
impossible to discover what degree of each was present. 
Throughout the summer and autumn of 1847 a large num- 


joining Strang’s organization he was, like Bennett, William Smith, Page, 
Adams, and others, given the standing he had enjoyed under Smith.

ber of church trials took place, the usual result of which 
was conviction, with the consequent excommunication of 
the culprit and his “delivery to the buffetings of Satan.”* 
In some instances the accused admitted their guilt and in-

Strangite organization. In other cases they failed to appear 
for trial, and judgment went against them by default.} In 
a few cases the charges were warmly contested, and failed 
through absence or inadequacy of evidence. 

A new feature of the situation was the trial of a consider- 
able number of women, in some instances on doctrinal 
charges, in others on those of misconduct. One or two illus- 
trations of cases of this kind may prove instructive. On 
July 4, Mary Nickerson was accused by Louisa Young 
before the High Council of the stake of Voree of slander and 
un-Christian conduct. The accused was the wife of U. C. H. 
Nickerson, one of the most active leaders of the Church, 
who at the April conference preceding had been ordered 
to go on mission to the far West to win back the Saints 
who were migrating to Utah. After testimony by numer- 
ous witnesses, both male and female, the president of the 
Council ruled that Mrs. Nickerson had been exonerated, 
whereupon the accuser took an appeal to the High Council 
of the Church. Eight weeks later Mrs. Young was herself 
brought to trial before the same council to which she had 
haled Mrs. Nickerson, on a charge of apostasy. She pled

against her, the case was dismissed for want of evidence.* 
Somewhat analogous to the foregoing was the case of 
John W. Crane, one of the minor officials of the Church. In 
October, Crane was brought to trial on charges of belief in 
spiritual wifery and of misappropriating church funds. He 
denied that he believed in the spiritual wife doctrine but 
admitted he had expressed a belief in “plurality of wives” ; 
on more recent investigation of the matter, however, he had 
discarded this belief. The Council voted unanimously its 
satisfaction with this confession, and the remaining charges 
failed through lack of evidence. Less than three months 
later, however, Crane was again summoned to appear be- 
fore the ecclesiastical bar for judgment. Instead, he sent in 
a “‘scurilous” letter, declining to attend. This time nine 
charges were laid against him—bearing false witness; pro- 
fanity; heresy, in teaching that it is right to plunder un- 
believers, and that Saints may have other women than one 
wife; attempting to seduce two young women; suing a 
brother at the law; making a hypocritical confession before

THE GARDEN OF PEACE 61 


the High Council; misrepresenting the teachings of the 
Presidency; obtaining property by false pretenses; and mis- 
appropriating church property. Each of these charges was 
proved by the testimony of from one to seven witnesses, and 
Crane was excommunicated and delivered over to the buffet- 
ings of Satan. 

Among the crowd of Saints who fell from grace (and the 
Church) during the summer and autumn of 1847 were 
William Smith, apostle and patriarch, and General-in- 
Chief John C. Bennett. Smith’s story is not entirely devoid 
of pathetic aspects. A crude, ignorant man, he had the 
courage following the death of his brothers, Joseph and 
Hyrum, to brave the wrath of the Twelve at Nauvoo, and 
despite their threats and cajolery he induced the whole 
Smith family to reject their leadership. Among many and 
grave defects of character, his devotion to his aged mother 
and other relatives under circumstances of great destitu- 
tion stands out as a redeeming feature. He had been impor- 
tuned to join the Pseudos in the summer of 1846, but had 
remained loyal to Strang, to the great delight of the official 
church organ. The occasion of his defection a few months 
later is not entirely clear. A long letter which he addressed 
to Strang on Christmas day, 1846, expounds in vigorous and 
pungent language his dissatisfaction over a proposal which 
had been made to associate ‘““Young Joseph” Smith with 
him in the patriarchal order. The office of patriarch had 
been created by Joseph, soon after the founding of the 
Church, for the benefit of his father, who for a fee would 
bestow upon the faithful a patriarchal blessing. Upon the 
death of ‘Father’ Smith, Hyrum had succeeded to the patri- 
archal duties and emoluments, and William now claimed 
them by ‘“‘lineage” and by blessing. He represented force- 
fully that a “superfuaty of Patriarchs” would destroy the 


62 Tue KINGDOM OF ST. JAMES 


benefits of his office, and warned the Prophet that he would 
not submit to treatment at his hands which he had resisted 
at the hands of the Twelve. At this time Smith was still in 
Illinois with his mother, and the removal of the latter to 
Voree was to take place the following spring. Ere the time 
arrived, however, the discovery was made (or the charge 
was trumped up) that the Patriarch had been guilty of 
adultery on the occasion of his first visit to Voree in July, 
1846. Although Smith undertook to defend himself, he was 
convicted of the offense against which God had of old 
admonished McLellin,* and at the October conference was 
banished from the Church. The disgusting testimony sub- 
mitted in support of the charge against Smith may have 
been untrue: if true, it convicts him of moral conduct com- 
parable to that of a plantation slave; if false, it illustrates 
anew the readiness of the Saints to impute such conduct to 
their most prominent leaders and officials. 

The breach between Strang and Bennett, like the earlier 
breach between Bennett and Joseph Smith, is shrouded in 
mystery. At the same October conference which witnessed 
William Smith’s downfall, the Prime Minister was for- 
mally charged with “apostasy, conspiracy to establish a stake 
by falsehood, deception, etc., and various immoralities,”’ and 
he was expelled from the Church and delivered over to the 
buffetings of Satan. 

Although no man could be more vocal on occasion than 
Bennett, for some reason now unknown he accepted his 
dismissal quietly. Withdrawing to Plymouth, Massachu- 
setts, the home of his wife’s parents, he devoted himself,

like another Diocletian, to the unmartial calling of raising 
chickens. Notwithstanding his breach with Strang, he re- 
tained a watchful interest in the progress of the Church, and 
three years later, when the Kingdom was established at 
Beaver Island, we find him making overtures to be taken 
once more into the fold, and expressing a feeling of warmest 
friendship for the Prophet. At this time he was publishing a 
book on the art of poultry raising, and here, appropriately, 
he may be allowed to disappear from our story. 

Unlike Bennett, the Patriarch, with peculiar smallness 
of soul, set forth upon an eastern lecture tour, exposing to 
the gentile world the frauds of Mormonism in general and 
of Strangism in particular.* Among other things, he stated 
that Strang had made the Voree plates from an old brass 
kettle; that the covenant illumination was a trick performed 
by the use of phosphorus; and that Strang contended that 
all biblical miracles were similar cheats. After all which, 
the erstwhile Patriarch sought a new career in Mormonism 
by attempting to set up a new reorganization with himself 
as the Prophet. 

To the end of his career Prophet Strang was compelled 
to contend with an ever fresh crop of apostasies, being assas- 
sinated at the last by a group of renegades from his own 
flock. Almost without exception, too, these apostates, re- 
gardless of how prominent a part they had themselves taken 
in the Strangite organization, on leaving it poured out un- 
measured abuse upon their former leader and associates. In 
this they incriminated themselves, of course, and the public 
might take its choice, as Strang was quick to point out,f 
whether to believe them rascals formerly or liars now. This

argument cut both ways, however, for in practically every 
instance these men had received, while in good standing 
with Strang, repeated certificates of character and good con- 
duct, while as soon as they apostatized they were found to 
be destitute of all decency and virtue. There is undeniably 
a considerable measure of truth in the orthodox explana- 
tion that the apostates were insincere or knaves from the 
beginning and that as fast as their real character was dis- 
covered it was necessary for the Prophet to cut them off.* 
But this explains only in part the steady procession of seem- 
ingly earnest men who “gathered” to Voree and Beaver 
Island, oftentimes at the cost of great personal sacrifice, 
professing to be led by direct divine revelation, and after 
a short period of time turned away anathematizing Strang 
and all his works. 

Whatever the explanation, it is abundantly clear that 
the conduct of the Saints themselves drove the Prophet to 
despair and made of the Garden of Peace a veritable temple 
of discord. In August, 1848, he issued, through the columns 
of the official church paper, an ‘appeal’ to his followers 
to which anyone who has hankerings after a prophetic 
career may well afford to devote earnest consideration.f 
Recounting his arduous labors and upright course since 
assuming the prophetic office, and reminding his hearers of

his character and good repute prior thereto, Strang pro- 
ceeded to show that he was daily subjected to intolerable 
abuse and suspicion, not by the gentile world, which had 
been kind to him thus far, but by his own followers, who 
lent a willing ear to the inventions, however vile, of the 
apostates. 


I last year spent six days [he said] in Jefferson County, New 
York, engaged three days in public preaching, and all that time, so 
that I scarcely got opportunity to sleep at all, with crowds of Saints 
and strangers, and during that time saw but three persons that I 
had ever seen before. There are certain doctrines of devils, which I 
had occasion to treat of, and which I set my face against as flint, 
publicly and privately, on all occasions. I never, by word, or by 
sign, or by silence, varied from the same unswerving testimony 
which I usually gave. And never in a single act, for a single moment 
did my example come short of my testimony. Yet I have now letters 
lying before me, written in that region, in which I am accused of 
criminal intercourse with more than forty different Saints there, in- 
cluding every sister in the church in that region of whom I have the 
slightest recollection, and several of whom I do not so much as 
know that they exist. 

Here in Voree my footsteps are dogged as with a shadow. I can- 
not step into a house but in a half hour it is known by half the 
neighborhood. And kindred spirits of Saints and apostates congregate 
together and the Saint begins, “What do you suppose Brother Strang 
has gone there for?” To which he gets the looked-for answer, “Any- 
body might know what a man goes where there is a woman for” (as 
though there was not usually a woman in every house), and pleased 
with the wit of such ribaldry, after a chuckle, or, if very sanctimoni- 
ous, a groan, replies, “O I don’t know. I don’t think Brother Strang 
is quite so bad as that. Do you really think so?—But I wish he 
would be more prudent.” And before the day is out, perhaps is urg- 
ing me to call at his house, and complaining that I don’t visit more. 
God speed the time when such wretches will go to their own place. 
. . . I am now reaping the bitter reward of their friendship in the 
blasting of that fair fame over which apostates and open enemies 
were powerless; and in the disgrace and infamy which by some 


66 Tue Kincpom oF St. JAMES 


means has been heaped on all God’s prophets, but on none more 
bitterly. 


If such was the spiritual atmosphere in which the Prophet 
lived and moved, what were the material conditions of his 
life? If one may credit his own explicit testimony, he lived 
in utmost poverty, the while he supported a burden of toil 
beyond the ability of most men to bear. In the summer of 
1847 he stated that, with no salary or income, he seldom 
had a week’s supply of food in the house; in his journeys 
by boat he traveled deck passage; he had not a single suit 
of whole clothing, and he was forced to send his letters 
unsealed, for lack of money with which to purchase wafers.* 

The home of Strang at Voree was in keeping with the 
clothing he wore. In July, 1846, God had commanded the 
faithful to build him a house, with a room in it for the 
work of translating ancient records.+ Yet two years later 
a visitor to Voree describes the Prophet’s residence as a 
“rudely made log house, about ten feet by fourteen, one 
story high, containing a chest or two, two beds, common 
low bedsteads, a small stove, [and] three or four chairs.”’£ 
Darker still is the picture of the Prophet’s circumstances 
sent out to the Saints abroad in December, 1848. They are 
asked to imagine him as coming to spend the evening: 


As you gather round the table [they are told], why does he yet 
linger at the fire? Do you know he has no overcoat? not a shred of 
flannel about him? Wears a summer vest? and both coat and pants 
are half worn? That he wears a straw hat at mid-winter? and he 
has been a prey to rheumatism for years. Well, let him warm by 
the fire. This is all the clothing he has. But do not help him to more; 
for if you do, as soon as he gets home he will divide with his brethren 
of the Order of Enoch, who are as bad off as he is. Give him nothing 


* Voree Herald, August 19, 1847. 
+ Recited in revelation of July 1, 1848. 
t Voree Herald, October 26, 1848. 


THE GARDEN OF PEACE 67 


but water to drink. It is so long since he has drunk anything else, 
he might not know what it was. Give him neither cake, pies, or 
sweetmeats. He will remember in bitterness that his children at home 
can have none.* 


Under such material conditions, he toiled incessantly to 
promote the ingathering of the Saints. Constant travel under 
the most favorable conditions of those days was in itself 
an arduous undertaking. But Strang’s poverty prevented 
him from enjoying such accommodations as there were. 
Thus, at midnight, July 4, 1849, he left Voree for a ride 
of twenty-seven miles to catch the morning boat at Racine 
en route to Beaver Island.f It was his third trip to Beaver 
this season, and for some months he had not slept in a bed 
two nights in a week. The Voree Herald was started in 
January, 1846, with less than a dozen subscribers.t It was 
kept up at the cost of infinite toils and struggles, “hoping 
often against hope”’ with every indication, oftentimes, that 
it could not be continued a day longer. 

Did you ever write [the Editor asked his readers in November, 
1847] when your mind was dark and your head seemed spinning 
round like a great wheel ? Or when all your ideas seemed lost in the 
bottom of a deep, dark well, from whence you could scarcely draw 
them up; and when, having got them marshalled in order, you would 


forget them or lose them in the darkness before you could write 
them down? 


About the same time Strang arranged a joint debate with 
a certain Professor Graham of Michigan Central College, 
on the validity of the Mormon faith. Graham postponed the 
discussion, pleading occupation with other matters. In reply 
Strang told something of his own engagements. Besides 
domestic business which usually would employ the time of 
three or four men, he had editorial charge of a paper, 


* Ibid., December 28, 1848. 7 Ibid., July 5, 1849. 
+ Ibid., November 25, 1847, November 9, 1848, and March 22, 1849. 


68 THe Kincpom oF St. JAMES 


pastoral care of a church scattered over a third of the earth, 
and an extensive private correspondence. All this without 
the aid of a clerk, and he was then waging two other public 
discussions with able opponents. 


The Unbuilding of V oree 


ROM this dark picture of the Prophet’s tribulations 

we turn to the more cheerful subject of his construc- 

tive labors and achievements at Voree. Despite the 
frequent schismatic upheavals, and the ever present poverty 
of the Saints, gradual, although slow, progress was made 
both in the upbuilding of Voree and of the Church abroad. 
In any discussion of this progress considerable attention 
must be devoted to its economic basis and aspects, since 
Strang was not merely the spiritual leader of a congregation, 
but as well the temporal ruler of a community which aspired 
to become a commonwealth. The original considerations 
which had served to commend the site of Voree as a location 
for the Saints were, indeed, economic. When Smith in the 
winter of 1844 deigned to consult Strang upon the possi- 
bility of finding a new location for the Saints, the latter 
promptly urged upon the Prophet the advantages of a loca- 
tion on White River in the vicinity of Burlington, Wis- 
consin. It would afford a healthy home for the mechanics 
and artisans from the northern and eastern states and from 
Europe, for whom the climate of Nauvoo was unsuitable; 
there would likewise be ample employment amid peace- 
able surroundings, thus relieving the economic pressure at 
Nauvoo. Smith was so impressed with the argument that 
he commissioned Strang and Aaron Smith to make a full in- 
vestigation of the situation and report their findings to him. 
That report is lost, but anyone who is familiar with the 
history and development of this region can have little doubt 


AO Tue Kinepom oF St. JAMES 


as to the arguments Strang advanced.* The tide of white 
settlement had set into this section only half a dozen years 
before. It had moved rapidly, however, and by 1844 the 
government land had practically all passed under private 
control. But prices were still low, labor was in demand, and 
the surrounding country was developing rapidly. In short, 
the foundations of a commonwealth were being laid in one 
of the fairest agricultural regions on the face of the globe. 
The immediate site of Voree, on the banks of White River, 
was beautifully rolling and contained a stone quarry from 
the development of which much was anticipated.+ Pro- 
vided with the necessary capital, there was every reason for 
expecting that the characteristic industry of the Saints 
would soon make of Voree a flourishing center of civiliza- 
tion. 

Although proselytes soon began flocking to Voree they 
came for the most part in a poverty-stricken condition, and 
the accumulation of worldly capital was slow. Lurid tales 
of the growth and wealth of the place were soon spread 
abroad through the gentile press. Strang deprecated such 
reports, and strove, through the Voree Herald, to make 
known to the world the actual facts. 


As the place began to be built last April [he said in September, 
1846], principally by a plundered and exiled people, it certainly is 


* Isaac Scott, whose career as a Pseudo we have had occasion to note, 
asserted in later life that the whole enterprise at Voree originated in a 
fraudulent scheme for real estate speculation, the promoters of which were 
Strang and three associates who owned the land on the site of Voree. The 
original plan was to have Smith build up a stake at Voree; when he was 
killed, however, the schemers decided to make Strang Smith’s successor as 
this “would make a sure thing of building up Voree.” Lamoni Saints’ 
Herald, December 29, 1888. 

} This expectation was largely disappointed as the stone proved, on trial, 
too soft to have much commercial value. Proof of this is seen in the fact 
that the quarry has never been worked, except for a short period by the 
Saints, and is now regarded as valueless. 


THE UPBUILDING OF VOREE vis 


not a very rich city. Its population dwell in plain houses; in board 
shanties, in tents, and sometimes many of them in the open air, and 
if any of those good Christians who say Voree is [nonexistent] will 
call here of a Sunday we will show them a congregation of from one 
to two thousand people* besides those who stay at home. The place 
is more prosperous than could have been expected in the present im- 
poverished state of the church. All who come here find plenty of em- 
ployment at good wages and a bountiful supply of all the necessaries 
of life at low prices. Men of all trades and occupations except idleness 
and sponging can be employed here to advantage. 


Two years later a Saint newly arrived from Philadelphia 
gave this description of the place for the benefit of his 
eastern friends: There were three hundred to four hundred 
faithful Saints, half of them living in the communistic 
Order of Enoch. Round about were a “good number” of 
apostates, 


who once belonged to the church, but who now make it their busi- 
ness to beset every newcomer with all sorts of the vilest and most 
wicked accusations against the Saints, especially the Prophet, in 
which they not unfrequently succeed in destroying their faith. Voree 
[he continued] is a very pleasant country place, hardly having the 
appearance of a town, and almost destitute of public houses. Dwell- 
ing houses and work shops stand in the midst of cultivated fields, 
some of them far distant from the roads, and the headquarters of the 
Order of Enoch are on the top of the Hill of Promise, surrounded 
by an ancient forest of oak and hickory. Stretching away to the 
north is Gardner’s Prairie, consisting of some 4,000 acres of grain 
fields with scarcely a tree in the entire space. The White River 
meadows at the south are nearly as extensive. The whole are sur- 
rounded by hills of moderate height, generally covered with timber. 
The place is healthy. Men who have a moderate amount of capital 
can successfully pursue almost any honest business. But as all kinds 
of work-shops are not established, those who are not able to set up 
in business might not find employ to their liking. 


*If this was intended as a statement of the population of Voree, it was 


much exaggerated. 
+ Voree Herald, August 3, 1848. 


72 Tue Kinepom oF St. JAMES 


About the same time (October, 1848) another visitor to 
Voree describes the home of Strang as a rude log house of 
one story, about ten by fourteen feet in size. His followers, 
“an intelligent and industrious community,” live mostly in 
unfinished stone houses. 

A full year later, however, in November, 1849, within 
a few months of the time when the headquarters of the 
Church were removed to Beaver Island, one of Strang’s 
spokesmen at Voree, writing to the “brethren abroad” con- 
cerning conditions there stated that ‘“‘quite a number” of 
houses had been finished within, and more were “‘in a state 
of progression.”* Evidently in the four years devoted by 
Strang to the upbuilding of the place, from the spring of 
1846 to 1850, no considerable material development was 
brought about, and with the hand of the leader removed, 
no long time was required to obliterate all physical evidence 
of a village having been developed here. Today Voree is 
but a memory, the only visible reminders of its former 
existence being the abandoned quarry site and mill pond, 
and a few unfilled cellar excavations. 

Two wise measures instituted by Strang in the upbuild- 
ing of Voree are worthy of especial note. Legal provision 
was made against saloons ever being conducted there by the 
insertion of a condition to this effect in all real estate titles.+ 
Speculation in land was also effectively discouraged by the 
device of the Prophet assuming control of the town site and 
offering lots to all comers for actual improvement at the 
uniform price of $50. Lots varied in size from one-fourth 
to a full acre, so that a building site four rods by ten could 
be had for $12.50. 

Much of the economic life of Voree centered about 
the fiscal and communistic enterprises of the Church. The

founder of Mormonism had instituted the prudent course 
of having himself declared trustee in trust for the Church 
of all property belonging to it. Very early in the career of 
the Strangite organization the question of legal ownership 
of the temples at Nauvoo and Kirtland arose. Strang, as 
successor of Smith, put forward a vigorous claim to the 
control of both, on behalf of his church organization. His 
claim was ignored at Nauvoo, but at Kirtland, after a 
brief contest with agents of the Brighamite faction, he was 
successful in gaining control of the temple property. At 
the April, 1847, conference of the Church, Strang recom- 
mended the adoption of suitable rules for the administration 
of church property. A committee was appointed on the sub- 
ject, and its report was unanimously adopted by the con- 
ference.* The First President was made trustee in trust 
for the Church, and all lands were to be held in his name. 
The Bishop was directed to keep a full account of all money 
and property received by him, and of all disbursements 
made. Tithing was enjoined upon the Saints, and provision 
was made for the publication of annual reports of all col- 
lections and disbursements. 

The requirement upon the faithful to tithe constituted 
the fiscal foundation of the church organization. Provision 
to this effect had been made very early in the ministry of 
Strang by revelation received from God on January 17, 
1845, the command being accompanied by the promise, if 
obeyed, ‘‘of the plates of the book that was sealed’ to 
Strang, who would translate them for his followers. In 
the revelation of July 1, 1846, the Saints were urged to 
build a house for the Prophet, in which a room should be 
set apart for translations. At the same time they were com- 
manded to build a temple at Voree after plans which God

glowing promises of good to accrue to the Saints as a reward 
for their fulfilment. 

Notwithstanding, the tithes were not paid, and the work 
on the Temple and Tower lagged accordingly. The former 
building was to be a massive structure of stone, covering, 
with its several courts, two and one-half acres of ground.* 
The central feature was a great hall two hundred feet 
square, with walls eight feet thick. The floor and roof were 
to be of marble, and according to George J. Adams, who 
had seen many of the great cities of America, it would be, 
when finished, “the grandest building in the world.’’+ The 
corner stone of the Temple was laid on October 6, 1847.£ 
Slight progress was made, however, during the next year

These several community enterprises were closely con- 
nected with the communistic project which Strang imposed 
upon his followers under the name of the Order of Enoch. 
This Order, which was intended to embrace all the faithful, 
was formally instituted by Strang and a few of his followers 
in January, 1848. At the same time the lengthy annual 
pastoral letter of the Prophet to his Saints was wholly 
devoted to arguing the necessity of the communistic state 
for those who would win salvation. Already sixty members 
(counting children) had come into the Order. It had 560 
acres of excellent land, several good stone buildings, a 
valuable water power, and a property in all worth $11,000, 
with an indebtedness of about $3,000. This was offered 
the Saints as “the home of the poor and the asylum of the 
oppressed”; all were urged to come, “‘without money and 
without price,” to share in common each other’s joys and 
sorrows, toils and possessions.f

+ The letter is printed in zbzd., February 3, 1848. There seems to be

of hands to do the chores and there is a scarcity of meet and also grain 
for the teams.”

But the Saints came slowly into the Order of Enoch, not- 
withstanding this alluring invitation. At the close of 1848, 
we learn from an editorial,* about 150 persons were in the 
Order. They were supporting the Herald, and in general 
bearing the burden of church maintenance. On principle 
they eschewed the use of tea, coffee, and tobacco; to live 
within their income they were abstaining, also, from sugar, 
spices, green and dried fruit, and luxuries of every kind. 
In dress they were practicing a plainness from which even 
a Quaker might learn much. 

Such a prospect did not impel any great rush of recruits 
to join the Order. On January 7, 1849, therefore, God 
strenuously denounced his Saints, through the mouth of 
Strang, for their failure to heed his commands. 

Ye have broken my commandments and despised mine authority 

[they are told], and my precepts have been to you a vain thing. Ye 
are a stiff-necked and rebellious people who delight in your own 
vanities and prefer your lusts to the chief of the things which God 
giveth. 
To those who would pay their tithes and live in the Order 
of Enoch the prospect of flattering rewards was held out; 
those who would not do these things were to be cut off from 
God’s favor. Other things were included in the revelation, 
particularly a strong injunction of obedience to Strang, who 
was “‘meeker than Moses and more patient than Job.”’+ 

The faithful still hung back, however, and in August, 
1849, a new bait was held out to them. Among the theo- 
logical doctrines which the founder of Mormonism had

would give his saints the riches of the earth?” The reading of this letter 
should have given the Prophet a bad quarter of an hour, although we have 
no reason to suppose that it did. It illustrates admirably the guileless sim- 
plicity of many of his deluded followers.

spun from an obscure scriptural text was that of baptism 
for the dead.* Briefly stated, the doctrine held out to the 
Saints the privilege of securing for their departed relatives 
the same spiritual advantages accruing from baptism that 
the departed might have gained for themselves had they 
undergone baptism in life.t The baptisms, along with the 
other church, “‘mysteries,’’ must take place in the Temple, 
secure from the gaze of the ungodly. Proper records must be 
kept against the day of judgment, and all must be done 
under the supervision of the ecclesiastical hierarchy. The 
Saints at Voree had no Temple as yet, and they were, there- 
fore, unable to begin the work of qualifying their loved 
ones for salvation. In this dilemma God sent the Prophet 
Elijah to Strang to inform the latter that He had deter- 
mined to reward those Saints who had been faithful to Him 
by giving them a present dispensation to conduct baptisms 
for the dead at Voree “during their poverty” and until they 
should have time to build the Temple. To this end White 
River, “from the bathing-pool unto the water wheel” was 
sanctified and made holy. A recorder of baptisms was to be 
ordained, whose records should also be “recorded in the 
archives of Heaven.”

Mormons, p. 118. But Smith claimed, and deserves to bear, the responsibility 
of foisting it upon the Church.

But to whom should be accorded the privilege of re- 
deeming their loved ones from purgatory? The message 
brought from God to Prophet Strang by Elijah gave this 
answer: 


All the faithful Saints who keep and abide in the order of Enoch; 
all who have paid tithing according to my law, for the building of 
a house unto my name in Voree, and for the upbuilding of my 
church; all who from this time forward will, and do, come up and 
do these things . . . and all who, standing in the priesthood, have 
labored faithfully in their several ministries from the time the 
commandment went forth to build a house unto my name in Voree. 


Thus the whole matter was under the control of the priest- 
hood, which meant, in thé last analysis, of Prophet Strang. 
In his hands had been placed a leverage upon the action of 
his followers as powerful as any the wit of man ever devised. 
The measures necessary for putting the new ordinance in 
process of execution were taken at the church conference 
held at Voree two weeks after the revelation establishing it 
had been given.* Appointments of officials to administer 
and to record baptisms were made, and the whole was ac- 
cepted by the congregation with entire unanimity. All being 
thus made ready, adjournment was taken to the river, when 
Prophet Strang proceeded to claim “by revelation of God” 
the redemption of the bodies of James Knox Polk, Oliver 
Cromwell, and Lord Byron. He was baptized severally for 
these worthies at the hands of President George J. Adams; 
after which Adams himself proceeded by revelation to re- 
deem the bodies of Napoleon Bonaparte, Samuel Adams, 
John Adams, and John Quincy Adams. The ordinance, 
having been initiated by the redemption of these distin- 
guished characters, “large numbers’ of the Saints proceeded 
to undergo baptism in behalf of their deceased relatives.

CHAPTER VI 


The Kingdom Is Established 


ARDLY had the work of establishing the true 
H Church of God at Voree been begun when the 

restless brain of the Prophet began to dream of 
other fields of conquest. There were solid economic reasons 
for this, to which we shall shortly direct attention. From 
the Mormon point of view, however, such reasons would 
have no weight unless reinforced by the direct command of 
God. At Elizabeth, on the Monongahela River, in August, 
1846, Strang received such a command.* In a vision he 
beheld ‘“‘a land amidst wide waters, and covered with large 
timber, with a deep, broad bay on one side of it.” There 
were hills and rich valleys where the air was pure and serene 
and the foliage fragrant; and Indians in canoes glided about 
and caught fish and sat down to eat, and gathered in assem- 
blies where men taught them the ways of truth and holiness. 
Inquiring the meaning of all this, Strang learned that the 
land he was beholding should be the seat of God’s people, 
whence the gospel should go out to the red men. The people 
who taught them would be exalted and would find here a 
certain refuge from gentile oppression. A stake of Zion was 
to be established, and a Temple built. And to those who 
should remove there the riches of forest, earth, and sea were 
promised in abundance. 

All this was but the Mormon way of announcing an im- 
portant decision of policy. In the survey of economic ad- 
vantages which the location at Voree held forth to the 

* Revelation of August 25, 1846, in Chronicles of Voree, pp. 114-117. The 


probable connection between this revelation and the newly imported Order 
of the Illuminati has already been suggested. 


So Tue KincpoMm oF St. JAMES 


Saints in 1844 one factor of vital importance had been 
overlooked. The land was already in private possession, 
and the owners were quite naturally disposed to get all they 
could for their property. The majority of the Saints who 
flocked to Voree were too poor to buy these lands, and those 
who had means were disinclined to devote them to buying 
property which, under Strang’s communistic program, would 
become the common possession of all. Men of means, there- 
fore, refused, for the most part, to respond to the call to 
Voree, and the church enterprises there progressed, as we 
have seen, at snail’s pace. Another consideration doubtless 
had weight with Strang: the gentile population round about 
Voree never persecuted the Saints, but the Prophet could 
hardly fail to perceive that he could not hope to build up 
a populous community there, wholly subservient to his rule, 
without provoking such hostility as the Saints had formerly 
encountered in Ohio and Missouri and were even now the 
objects of in Illinois. Removal to a wilderness region would 
solve the land problem, and would, at the same time, make 
possible the development of a theocratic commonwealth 
free from gentile interference. 

The place to which the Saints were miraculously directed 
was the group of Beaver Islands near the northern end of 
Lake Michigan. The whole northern half of the lower 
peninsula of Michigan was then a wilderness, inhabited by 
only a few hundred Indians and white men. The principal 
center of the latter was Mackinac Island, of old the center of 
the trade in fur for almost half a continent. The fur trade 
had dwindled to insignificance, but Mackinac had found a 
new and somewhat precarious source of support in the fish- 
ing industry of the region adjoining in Lakes Michigan 
and Huron. For this industry Mackinac afforded at best an 
illogical capital, having no harbor and being remote from 


THE Kincpom Is EstTaBLisHED 81 


the best fishing grounds; moreover it had no wood supply, 
then a vital item in the conduct of the shipping trade of the 
lakes. It had, however, the advantages of entrenched posi- 
tion as a center of trade. Considerable capital was invested 
in the business houses and other property of the place, all of 
which would be lost should the trade go to another center. 
Consequently, as long as a rival was lacking, Mackinac con- 
tinued to dominate the trade of the surrounding region. 

At Big Beaver Island were to be found all the natural 
advantages as a center of trade which Mackinac lacked. It 
possessed a seemingly inexhaustible supply of timber, it 
was on the main steamboat route from Buffalo to Chicago, 
it was in the very center of the richest fishing grounds of 
Lake Michigan, and it possessed a splendid, landlocked 
harbor. Although the Indian title to the land had been ex- 
tinguished, the government had not yet thrown it open for 
settlement. Some twenty or thirty Indian families were still 
living on the island; white occupation was confined to a 
trading establishment on the north side of the harbor. Its 
owners were some New York men, who, under the name 
of the North West Company, were engaged in trading with 
the Indians and the fishermen of the adjoining region, and 
who entertained somewhat nebulous schemes for extensive 
business developments. Chiefly, however, they were cultivat- 
ing their preémption right to their location adjoining the 
harbor, in the expectation that the further settlement of the 
western country would soon render it valuable. 

At the opening of navigation in the spring of 1847 Strang 
journeyed to the islands with four companions, intent on 
making a first-hand survey of the situation. During the 
winter preceding he had been corresponding with John Fisk 
of Rochester, New York, who was a partner in the trading 
establishment on Big Beaver, and who, on behalf of his 


82 Tue KincpomM oF St. JAMES 


company, cordially welcomed the Mormons to the islands 
and expressed the desire to codperate harmoniously with 
their new neighbors in the further development of the 
place.* The Prophet’s own account of the visit, however, 
represents the traders as treating them with anything but 
cordiality.t Had they foreseen the discord that was pres- 
ently to develop, their reception of the newcomers would 
have been still more frigid. 

So destitute of means were the Saints that they sold their 
blankets to pay their steamer passage, and on reaching Big 
Beaver they went ashore with less than two days’ supply 
of provisions and without a cent of money. Building them- 
selves a shelter of hemlock boughs, they began a survey of 
the island, scantily clad and still more scantily fed.t But 
labor was in demand, and they soon procured in exchange 
for their toil a supply of provisions and the use of a boat for 
their further explorations. 

The result appeared in the Voree Herald two months 
later§ in the form of a topographical and economic survey 
of the islands, especial emphasis being placed upon the eco- 
nomic advantages they held out to the Saints who would 
heed God’s call to “gather” there. The report emphasized, 
too, the opportunity presented of carrying the gospel to 
the “Lamanites,” or Indians. For Prophet Strang, as for the 
founder of Mormonism, these brothers of a fallen race 
possessed a peculiar interest, and in all the discussions over 
the removal to the Beavers the motive of proselyting among 
the Indians held prominent place. Indeed, the first notice

dian Mission” a place of gathering: first, to give the Indians 
the arts of civilization; and second, to get lands at such price 
that a farm could be supplied to every man according to 
his need, however poor he might be.f 

The beginning toward entering into the possession of 
the new “inheritance” was humble enough. It had been 
expected that a general migration of Saints to the islands 
would set in in the spring of 1847, and in January the 
Herald announced some general directions for their guid- 
ance, and the appointment of seven men to have charge of 
the enterprise.t These men were commended to the Saints 
abroad as “‘tried, substantial, and faithful men—servants 
of the Most High God—in whom the churches may repose 
the most implicit confidence.” They had not yet been tried 
enough, however, to justify this commendation, for before 
long over half of them had apostatized and been delivered 
over to the buffetings of Satan in the flesh.§ The expected 
migration did not materialize for the present, but in July 
a handful of Saints assembled at the trading house of the 
North West Company and began the organization of the 
local stake.|| It was resolved at this conference to begin work

at once on building a mission house and on Mormonizing

At the conference Ebenezer Page was placed in charge 
of the local stake until further organization should take 
place. In all, four families of Saints located on Big Beaver 
during the season of 1847. Their isolation and privations 
in this island wilderness during the dreary months of winter 
when they were completely shut off from all contact with 
the outside world can more easily be imagined than de- 
scribed. Fortunately the winter proved unusually mild, and 
with the return of spring glowing reports were sent out to 
the Saints concerning the situation as they had found it.* 

These reports were shortly reinforced by an address from 
the Prophet himself, penned at the “city of James” on Big 
Beaver Island, June 26, 1848.+ In this address the slow 
progress made hitherto at the Beavers is explained as due 
to apostasies in the Church and the spreading abroad of 
untrue statements about the economic prospects at the 
islands. In opposition to these misrepresentations the 
Prophet drew this picture of the situation: 


Here are extensive tracts of land of superior quality ready and 
waiting to be occupied, covered with valuable timber, which, both 
in lumber and cordwood finds a ready cash market at high prices. 
Both soil, climate, and location conspire to make this place a first 
rate agricultural region. Around the islands are the most extensive 
and valuable inland fisheries of North America. Business of several 
kinds is well established here and prospering greatly. Common labor 
is worth one dollar per day. Mechanical trades are good, especially 
coopering and boat building. Provisions are abundant and generally 
at lower prices than in the middle states. 


The Indian mission house was reported nearly ready for 
use, and a school for both white and Indian children was

about to be opened. Friendly relations had been established 
with the red men who were reported to be “‘a noble and in-

A letter* from Big Beaver written near the close of 
October, 1848, further sets forth the progress of the new 
settlement. Thus far the only crop raised was potatoes which 
had turned out excellently. The land could be cleared for 
nothing, since there was a steady demand for cordwood, 
which could be sold at fifty cents a cord. A dock had been 
built on the west side of the harbor, and another was ex- 
pected to be built on the south side next season. Sixty lake 
boats had called during the shipping season, and the number 
would have been larger but for the uncertainty of obtaining 
fuel. The writer concluded with a denial of reports of a 
lack of tillable soil on the island. 

The winter of 1848—49 closed down upon twelve families 
on the desolate island, where four had been a year before. 
But even this tiny group of Saints, buried in the northern 
wilderness, could not long subsist in amity. At the beginning 
of January, Samuel Shaw and Levi Parmeter “broke out 
in revelations.’ + Parmeter had a revelation from God ap- 
pointing Gurdon Brown as Prophet; while Parmeter him- 
self was “the lion to come out of the thicket and destroy 
the gentiles.” Shaw prophesied in the name of God that 
the Indians were about to “go from house to house in the 
dead of the night and butcher the people.’’ Only by enter- 
ing into a covenant, which the two fanatics proceeded to 
present to the settlers, could this destruction be averted. 
The presidents of the local stake were not greatly im-

pressed by these revelations, however; they proceeded to 
bring the new prophets to trial and cut them off from the 
Church with the usual formula of delivery to the buffetings 
of Satan. This affair, trivial in its dimensions, is yet of 
importance as illustrating the state of hysteria which over- 
much brooding on the subject of religion seems too prone 
to develop. A tiny band of Saints had. obeyed the call of 
God, as they believed, to exile themselves in the wilderness. 
Thrown wholly upon their own resources for society, they 
spent most of their time, apparently, in religious devotion 
and disputation.* As in a more noted settlement around 
Boston Harbor two centuries earlier, they soon began to 
see spirits. “There are many spirits here, and some very 
strange ones too,” wrote President Aldrich to Prophet 
Strang, in reporting the affair of Shaw and Parmeter. 
Puritan Massachusetts hanged her witches in the seven- 
teenth century. Mormon Beaver Island could not hang its 
rebels in the nineteenth, but the Prophet published to the 
world this opinion of them: “Rebellion is as the sin of 
witchcraft. Witchcraft is sectarianism. And every rebel 
organization that ever sprung out of Mormonism is simply 
that and nothing more.’’} 

The season of 1849 witnessed the first considerable migra- 
tion of Saints to Big Beaver.+ Most of them were poor, but 
a few men of means came. A steam sawmill was begun, a 
small schooner built, and a beginning was made of con- 
structing a highway into the interior of the island. The

number of Mormon families increased fourfold—from 
twelve to almost fifty. More important developments than 
these, however, affecting the future of the Mormon estab- 
lishment, were transpiring at a distance from the Beavers. 
To the spring conference of 1849 at the city of Saint 
James* came a number of the leading men of the Church, 
and from it twelve elders went out on mission with instruc- 
tions to return with their converts to Beaver the following 
spring. 

Meanwhile the busy brain of Prophet Strang was evolv- 
ing a more aggressive program of action than any which 
had marked his ministry this far. From numerous sources 
come indications that the summer of 1849 marked a turning 
point in the development of the Church. Until then the 
Prophet had faced an unceasing round of conflict with 
biting poverty and apostasies still more bitter. Now the tide 
seemed at last about to turn. The procession of apostasies 
never ceased, but the menace of poverty gradually lessened 
and with increasing numbers and wealth the desertion of 
apostates from time to time was less seriously felt. With 
affairs thus looking up, as soon as the autumn conferences 
of 1849 at Voree and Big Beaver were over, the Prophet and 
George J. Adams set out upon an invasion of the East 
which was fairly comparable in its significance to the first 
one undertaken by Strang in the summer of 1846. Their 
mission lasted through the fall and winter and was prin- 
cipally devoted to the great eastern cities, Boston, New 
York, Philadelphia, Baltimore, and Washington. It had, 
apparently, a threefold object: to reinforce the work of the

elders in “rolling up” recruits for the islands; to improve 
the standing in general of the Church throughout the East; 
and to appeal to the authorities at Washington for certain 
legal concessions with respect to the occupancy of the islands 
by the Saints. 

If the reports given out by the Prophet be credited,* 
in all these matters a gratifying measure of success was 
achieved. Never before had the elders encountered such 
violent opposition, reported Strang, as did those who went 
east in the fall of 1849.¢ Yet all who stood to their task 
prospered and a “rich harvest” was the result, and all oppo- 
sition in that section was silenced. At Washingtont the 
Prophet was invited to accept free lodgings with one of 
the “most worthy citizens’ and by public officials was 
treated like the ambassador of a foreign nation. Exactly 
what he sought from the authorities is somewhat obscure, 
but enough is in evidence to show that it had chiefly to do 
with securing free lands for the penniless Saints. The 
Prophet seems also to have sought, but without success, to 
have the Beaver Islands withheld from public entry and 
reserved solely for the Saints. To his followers Strang re- 
ported somewhat vaguely that he had enlisted several dis- 
tinguished men “in favor of a just and free distribution of 
vacant lands among all the landless, for a perpetual inherit- 
ance, according to the principles of the law of God.” Daniel 
Webster was mentioned as fairly committed to this policy; 
but it seems safe to assume that in the mind of the great 
statesman and lawyer the “policy” in question was quite a 
different thing than it was in the minds of the readers of 
the Voree Herald.
