With the return of the Prophet from his Washington 
sojourn in the spring of 1850 a curious and significant 
change became evident in the Strangite organization. The 
chief outward manifestation of this lay in the removal from 
Voree to the Beaver Islands. But this change of physical 
environment was not more marked than the changed atti- 
tude of the Saints themselves toward the gentile world that 
surrounded them. In Ohio, in Missouri, and at Nauvoo, 
there had been constant friction between the Saints and 
their gentile neighbors, accompanied by mobbing and even 
civil war. At Voree, while the Saints quarreled endlessly 
among themselves, between them and the gentiles round 
about there was peace and amity. At the Beaver Islands 
there was almost constant turmoil and discord between 
Saint and gentile, rising at times to a state of open warfare 
and culminating at last in the wholesale expulsion of the 
Saints from the islands. Looking forward for a moment, 
the Utah Saints played for decades the rdle of Ishmaelites, 
while the Reorganized Church, with its many thousands of 
followers, has always lived at peace with its gentile neigh- 
bors. 

Why has the hand of the gentile been so frequently 
raised against the Saint, and how are we to account for the 
two exceptions noted? A complete answer to these questions 
would necessarily take account of many factors, but it is 
perfectly easy to suggest the more important ones. The 
Saints have always protested that they were law-abiding 
citizens, who desired merely justice at the hands of the 
authorities. Yet their fundamental attitude toward the 
government was quite incompatible with good citizenship. 
While the nation was engaged in the Mexican War, Strang 
publicly advised his followers to have nothing to do with 
it. The idea was continually reiterated that the country 


go Tue Kincpom or St. JAMES 


was a sink of iniquity, upon which the vengeance of God 
was about to be visited. G. J. Adams, who stood next to 
Strang in the administration of the Church, publicly prayed 
for the destruction of the United States: 


This nation is becoming weaker every day and every hour. It is on 
the very brink of ruin. There is not virtue enough left in the entire 
country to save it from crumbling into a mass of ruins, and then 
will come its desolation and final overthrow. O God! Hasten that 
time that such a cruel, blood-thirsty nation may be blotted out from 
under heaven, and be no more a people !* 


With the very highest officials of the Church giving public 
expression to sentiments such as these, it is not remarkable 
if the gentile world attributed to the Saints darker designs 
than they actually harbored. As long as they were insignifi- 
cant in numbers, and their membership diffused through- 
out the general population, public opinion could afford to 
ignore them. But when, as at Nauvoo and at the Beaver 
Islands, a large concentration of Saints in a single com- 
munity took place, a twofold process of irritation became 
operative; the Saints became a compact political power, and 
the consciousness of their strength led them to assume an 
ever more belligerent attitude; viewing which, the gentiles 
perceived that an interest alien to their own had come into 
their midst, and that they must make their choice between 
driving it out, or themselves abandoning the community. In 
short, wherever the peculiar governmental and social ideas 
of the Saints found opportunity for expression, the founda- 
tion was laid for an irrepressible conflict between them and 
their gentile neighbors. The Saints were absolutely correct 
in their recognition of the fact that they could not abide 
peaceably in the United States. Unfortunately, from the 
point of view of peace and neighborly concord, none of 


* Voree Herald, March 28, 1850. 


THE Krncpom Is EstasiisHEep gl 


their several removals—whether to Missouri, to Utah, or 
to the Beaver Islands, took them outside its boundaries. 

It should be noted, finally, that at the Beaver Islands, 
as at Nauvoo and Salt Lake City, the gentile world came to 
believe that monstrous crimes against morals and liberty 
alike, were characteristic of the Mormon régime. Mormon 
apologists, when addressing the outside world, strenuously 
denied the validity of this belief, and confining our re- 
mark to the Beaver Islands, we think with justice in the 
main. But the Mormon leaders were themselves chiefly 
responsible for its widespread currency. The most damning 
picture of Mormonism which the limitations of the English 
language permit one to paint is supplied by the testimony 
of the leaders of the Church themselves. That this testimony 
was often false, may readily be granted, yet the Mormons 
themselves are fairly estopped from pleading such a defense. 
If the gentiles believed a tissue of falsehood about the lives 
and practices of the Saints, the Saints themselves supplied 
the material from which the fabric was woven. 

On the eve of the removal from Voree the Prophet en 
route home from the East, penned at Buffalo a moving 
“testimony to the nation,’ addressed to the President, Con- 
gress, and all the people.* It recited the unrequited wrongs 
of the Mormons, from the organization of the Church twenty 
years earlier, and concluded with an appeal to Congress to 
pass a law authorizing the Saints to “settle upon and for- 
ever occupy all the uninhabited lands of the islands in Lake 
Michigan,” and to cease to sell the same to other persons. 
It also asked that the “martyrdom” of the Saints and their 


* Ibid., June 6, 1850. A copy of the proclamation as originally issued at 
Buffalo is preserved among the Strang MSS. On June 15, 1850, it was pre- 
sented as a memorial to the U.S. Senate. It was referred by that body to 
the Committee on Public Lands, which reported that the government did 
not own the lands desired by the Saints. 


g2 Tue Kinepom oF St. JAMES 


exile from some of the states be publicly condemned, and 
that measures be taken to redress their wrongs. ““The govern- 
ment which cannot or will not prevent such crimes,” the 
document concluded, ‘does not deserve to continue; and it 
would be distrusting the justice of the Most High to believe 
he would not destroy it. As you do good or evil, God reward 
you.” 

Although this pronouncement was the handiwork of the 
Prophet it was facilely adopted by the Church, and with 
its commendation issued to the world. In reality the “‘testi- 
mony to the nation” was a proclamation of defiance, and 
its issuance at this particular moment augured ill for the 
peace of the Beaver Islands and the adjoining region. 
Although Strang continually referred to the islands as an 
uninhabited land which had been set apart for the Saints 
by God Himself, others had preceded them into this region. 
An extensive fishing industry had long flourished in the 
waters adjoining the islands, and traders were already 
established on the shores of Paradise Bay. The fishermen 
were a roistering crew with the free and easy habits of a 
population which was at once frontier, seafaring, and Irish. 
Traders and fishermen alike soon perceived in the advent 
of the newcomers a menace to their own interests and ways 
of life, and on the Beaver Islands, as in Missouri and else- 
where, as soon as the numbers of the Saints became con- 
siderable, hostility developed between the two factions. 

In anticipation of his return from the East, Strang had 
appointed a conference of the Church at Big Beaver, to 
convene early in July, 1850. It culminated on July 8 in 
the most remarkable act of the Prophet’s surprising career. 
From the beginning of that career the promise had been held 
out to his followers that God would reveal to them through 
his agency hidden mysteries, and would intrust ancient 


Tue Kincpom Is EsTaBLisHEeD 93 


records to him for translation. To Joseph Smith had been 
revealed the golden plates of the Book of Mormon which he 
had translated with the aid of the mysterious Urim and 
Thummim. But it was reserved to Strang to bring forward, 
if possible, a still more remarkable record. He professed to 
have received by miraculous intervention certain metallic 
“plates of Laban,”’* on which had been copied at a date 
prior to the Babylonian captivity a certain scriptural record 
known as the Book of the Law of the Lord. This record 
was so important that the Jews had kept it in the Ark of the 
Covenant itself, and jealously guarded it from profane eyes. 
Eventually it was lost to the Jews themselves, and hence 
the Old Testament as it has come down to modern times 
contains but a traditional and incomplete record even of 
such vital matters as the Ten Commandments. The Prophet 
had translated-the plates of Laban, and the announcement 
of their contents, which he was now ready to make known 
to the world, proved startling enough.f The Kingdom of 
God was to be reéstablished on earth, and “his servant 
James’ was called to occupy the throne. For the rest, a 
surprisingly comprehensive framework of theocratic govern- 
ment was set forth, ample in scope to serve the needs of a 
kingdom of whatever size. To the contents of this book and 
their consequences as exemplified in the further history of 
the Strangite organization, we shall have frequent occasion 
to return. For the present, however, we proceed with the 
establishment of the Kingdom. 

How many of Strang’s associates had been acquainted in 
advance with this impending transformation is not a matter 
of record. There is no hint of it in the columns of the Voree

Herald, publication of which was continued until June Oo 
1850, and it is a perfectly safe presumption that in his 
numerous conferences with public men at Washington dur- 
ing the winter of 1849-50 Strang said nothing of his fast- 
ripening project to set up a kingdom within the borders of 
the United States. But G. J. Adams was cognizant of the 
plan at least as early as July, 1849,* and from various 
allusions in the Strang manuscripts it is evident that at 
least several others were informed of it before the time 
actually arrived for putting it into execution. On July 8, 
in the uncompleted tabernacle, the coronation ceremony was 
carried out.t A portion of the newly revealed Book of the 
Law of the Lord was read, and the four hundred spectators 
assembled to witness the setting up of the Kingdom of God 
on earth, with uplifted hands entered into a solemn cove- 
nant henceforth to be God’s people and live in accordance 
with His law as revealed to them by His Prophet in the 
flesh. The Prophet, clad in a robe of bright red, was escorted 
to a platform and around him were grouped the apostles, 
the Quorum or Seventy, and the minor church officials, while 
the space that remained was occupied by the common 
people. George J. Adams, who performed with equal facility 
in the pulpit and on the stage, acted as master of ceremonies. 
These concluded with placing on the brow of Strang a 
crown of metal with a cluster of stars in front. On the self- 
same day the new-made King received a divine revelation 
appointing “the Islands of the Great Lakes” as an inherit- 
ance for the Saints and delegating to the King the power

of apportioning the lands among them.* Henceforth for- 
ever the return of the day that “James the Prophet was 
established King” was to be celebrated with sacrifices and 
thank offerings.¢ At sunrise the King offered a heifer, while 
all the men presented a beast or a fowl in like fashion. The 
victims were slain by the priests, cooked by the deacons, 
and consumed by the congregation in a great public feast, 
the several stages in the performance being attended by 
appropriate declarations of sentiment on the part of the 
worshipers. In one respect, at least, Strang had demon- 
strated his ability to prophesy. As a youth of nineteen he 
had recorded in his diary the making of a great discovery: 
“That is that I am eager and mankind are frail,’ followed 
by the announcement, “JI shall act upon it for time to come 
for my own benefit.”” Did these words recur to the mind of 
the red-robed vicegerent of God on earth, sitting with a 
tinsel crown upon his head, as he gazed into the awe-struck 
faces of his four hundred credulous worshipers?

CHAPTER Vil 


The Saints Become Polygamous 


OINCIDENT with the removal from Voree to 
- Beaver Island went another change in the Strangite 

organization scarcely less astonishing than the crea- 
tion of the Kingdom. From the early days of Mormonism 
ugly reports and accusations had been whispered concerning 
the moral ideals and practices of the Saints. With the defec- 
tion of Dr. John C. Bennett at Nauvoo and the publication 
of his sweeping charges of shocking immorality against the 
leaders of the Church, the whispers swelled to a tumult, and 
the attention of the gentile world in general was for the first 
time centered upon this aspect of Mormonism. Although 
Bennett was himself an unprincipled knave, the further 
course of events at Nauvoo, culminating in the death of 
Prophet Smith and the dispersion of the Saints, was such 
as to leave no room for doubt that the doctrines of spiritual 
wifery and polygamy were being secretly taught and ex- 
emplified by the highest leaders of the Church. Although 
several years passed before these things were acknowledged, 
polygamy and the spiritual wife doctrine were ultimately 
made the corner stone of the Brighamite brand of Mor- 
monism, and today its 500,000 adherents proclaim and 
devoutly believe that both the doctrine and the practice 
of polygamy were foisted upon the Church during the life- 
time of Smith and by the Prophet himself. 

By Prophet Strang, however, these things were vigor- 
ously denounced, from the beginning of his career. Against 
the “abominations” of the Brighamites at Nauvoo he had 
poured out the vials of God’s wrath, and the Saints at 


THE Saints BEcoME PoLtyGamous Q7 


Voree who became infected with spiritual and plural wife 
leanings were cut off from the Church with unfailing regu- 
larity and dispatch. As early as the summer of 1846 a sig- 
nificant illustration of the Prophet’s attitude was afforded 
in connection with the opposition to Bennett’s Order of the 
Illuminati. One of the revolters, Willard Griffith, subse- 
quently authorized a formal retraction of his conduct. In 
it he stated his conviction that Bennett had not taught 
polygamy or spiritual wifery, and his belief that the church 
leaders at Voree were set “‘like flint” against spiritual wifery 
in every form.* A few months later, in February, 1847, the 
editor of the Voree Herald published an address to the 
Saints in which spiritual wifery was described as an “‘abomi- 
nation” which was trampled into the dust at Voree as soon 
as it made its appearance.} In July following, Strang him- 
self printed an editorial with the title “Polygamy not Pos- 
sible in a Free Government.’’t Although short, it contained 
a masterly argument in support of the thesis indicated, and 
closed with a warning to the Brighamite Saints to beware 
of the pit they were digging for themselves. Two weeks 
later, John E. Page, then one of the presidents of the 
Church, printed a signed communication to the Saints de- 
voted wholly to refuting the doctrine of polygamy and to 
proving that Strang had no responsibility for it; “We have 
talked hours, yea, even days with President Strang,” he 
stated, ‘“‘and we find to our utmost satisfaction that he does 
not believe in or cherish the doctrine of polygamy in any 
manner, shape, or form imaginable whatever.” This article

elicited a signed statement from Strang himself in the fol-

which Page had attributed to him, he proceeded: 


I am only astonished that it should be necessary to state them at 
all. Within three years I have, in the work of the ministry, traveled 
over 16,000 miles, visited all the states north of the Carolinas but 
three, most of them several times—preached to large congregations 
in all the principal cities and in most of the large branches in the 
country. And I have uniformly and most distinctly discarded and 
declared heretical the so-called “spiritual wife system” and every- 
thing connected therewith . . . I now say distinctly, and I defy 
contradiction, that the man or woman does not exist on earth, or 
under the earth who ever heard me say one word, or saw me do one 
act, savoring in the least of spiritual wifery, or any of the attend- 
ing abominations. My opinions on this subject are unchanged, and 
I regard them as unchangeable. They are established on a full con- 
sideration of all the scriptures, both ancient and modern, and the 
discipline of the church shall conform thereto. 


A more emphatic avowal of the Prophet’s attitude on the 
subject than this could not be penned; it continued to ex- 
press Strang’s position at least until September, 1848, for 
at that time Page, as President of the twelve apostles, ad- 
dressed a formal order to the Saints to withdraw their fel- 
lowship from advocates of polygamy or spiritual wifery, 
characterizing them as “unfruitful works of darkness, born 
of hell and begotten of the Devil.” 

It is a familiar axiom that the leopard may not change 
his spots, yet within less than a year from the time the 
Prophet publicly recorded his “unchanged” and ‘“‘unchange- 
able” opposition to polygamy he had himself become a 
secret practitioner of plural marriage, and the remainder 
of his life was devoted to the endeavor to foist this relic 
of primitive society upon the world as an institution di-

AN ISSUE OF THE VOREE HERALD 


THE Sarnts BecoME PoLycamous 99 


vinely ordained. How are we to account for this surprising 
reversal, from which were to proceed consequences of mo- 
mentous importance not only to the Strangite community 
but to the entire future development of Mormonism? A 
son of the Prophet has advanced the theory* that the change 
was made when Strang became convinced that Joseph 
Smith, before his death, had become a proponent of polyg- 
amy. To the author this explanation is not convincing. The 
facts concerning Joseph Smith and conditions at Nauvoo 
were as accessible to Strang at the beginning of his prophetic 
career as they were at any later time, and there is in exist- 
ence the best of documentary authority for the belief that 
Strang was by no means ignorant of Joseph Smith’s doings.+ 
Strang was a man of erratic brilliancy, and the surmise 
seems reasonable that his head was turned by his success in 
gathering and dominating over his followers, so that he 
came to believe he could do anything he chose with them. 
But it may be asked why he should choose to do this thing, 
which ran counter to his entire career hitherto. One answer 
has already been suggested. Another may lie in the fact 
that Strang was a man of powerful sexual impulsest and 
that he had made the acquaintance of a clever and charm- 
ing young woman whom he, being long since married, could 
possess only through the avenue of polygamy. 

Returning from the realm of surmise to that of demon- 
strable fact, let us take note of a performance of Strang

in connection with his eastern mission of 1849 which is 
difficult to reconcile with the accepted standards either of 
common morality or of common sense. On July 8, 1830, 
about the same time as the birth of the Mormon Church, 
Elvira Eliza Field was born at Streetsborough, Ohio.* Her 
parents became converts to Mormonism and joined the 
Church at Kirtland. In 1844 they removed to Michigan, 
and here the girl blossomed into a charming womanhood. 
For two years she lived with an uncle in Washtenaw 
County, by the name of Israel Smith, and at the age of 
sixteen she began teaching school. She taught at Eaton 
Rapids in 1847 and near Charlotte the following year. 
When Strang’s prophetic claims came to the attention of 
Elvira’s parents they accepted them. Elvira likewise be- 
came a devotee of Strang, whom as yet she had never seen, 
and planned to attend the approaching conference of the 
Church at Voree in April, 1848. “If you want a good school 
mistress,’ wrote Samuel Graham to the Prophet, “‘she has 
the name. She wants to join the associated order of Enick 
[ Enoch ].”+ 

But destiny had in store for Elvira a far different réle 
than that of schoolmistress to the children of the Saints. 
Presumably she came to the April conference, and there 
for the first time encountered the Prophet in the flesh. 
Certain it is that a few months after this the Prophet com- 
municated to her, through the agency of George J. Adams, 
as strange a proposal as ever came in sober earnest from the 
lips of mortal man. The Prophet, he said, had received from 
God, by an angelic messenger, a holy and priceless record, 
containing the law of God for the governance of his people 


* The facts concerning her are taken from a memorial booklet, prepared 
and published by her son, Charles J. Strang, after her death in 1910. 

7 Strang MSS, letter of Samuel Graham to Strang, Leroy, Michigan,

in his Kingdom, which was shortly to be established on 
earth. The new revelation required the Saints, among other 
things, to institute a polygamous organization of society; 
Prophet Strang, as King and vicegerent of God on earth, 
would himself set the example for his followers; and Adams 
had come, like another John the Baptist, to offer to Elvira 
Field the unique distinction of becoming his first plural 
wife, and his queen in the Kingdom soon to be established. 
But since conditions were not yet ripe for the public an- 
nunciation of this program, the union was to be kept secret 
for the present, even from the girl’s own parents. 

It affords a striking example of the limits of human 
credulity that Elvira accepted this offer in all sincerity, and 
vanishing from the ken of parents and loved ones, blindly 
staked her entire future upon her faith in the Prophet and 
in his lecherous Prime Minister, George J. Adams. On July 
13, 1849, a few days after the close of the church con- 
ference at Beaver Island, she was married to Strang, on 
the eve of his departure for the East.* The young bride’s 
hair was shorn, and clad in masculine garments she ac- 
companied her husband upon his mission, posing as his 
nephew and private secretary under the alias of “Charles J. 
Douglass.”’ So effective was her concealment of her move- 
ments from her family that more than six months after the 
marriage her distracted brother was still traveling over

But the secret, if kept from Elvira’s parents, was shared 
by certain of the Saints who stood high in the Prophet’s 
confidence, and it was of such a character that it could not 
be wholly confined to this select circle. Even at the July 
conference on Big Beaver, there had been gossiping among 
the Saints, which the Prophet, as usual, had sternly re- 
pressed. After his departure from the island, the gossip was 
renewed, and by the time Strang reached New York it had 
developed to dangerous dimensions. Its chief fomenter was 
Mrs. Lorenzo D. Hickey. Apparently she lacked positive 
knowledge as to what was transpiring, but there were indica- 
tions of scandalous doings at the island amply sufficient to 
give exercise to her tongue, and Saints do not differ from 
gentiles in the propensity to give ear and circulation to 
stories of scandalous conduct. 

Not only did Mrs. Hickey talk to her neighbors on 
Beaver Island, but of more immediate import to our story, 
she wrote letters to her husband, who was absent in the 
East. In the high tide of the Prophet’s meetings in New 
York City, Hickey and Increase Van Deusen precipitated. 
a disgraceful uproar. Hickey charged the Prophet with 
adultery, fornication, spiritual wifery, ‘‘and all the abomi- 
nations that were ever practiced at Nauvoo,” and professed 
to have letters from Beaver Island which would support 
these charges. Wan Deusen, screaming like a madman, so 
grossly insulted Strang, both by words and by physical 
manifestations, that a negro Saint who was present testified 
that although it was not expected of “‘persons of color’ that 
they should behave with equal decorum with white men, 


* Strang MSS, statements, among others, in letters of James M. Greig to 
Strang, November 16, 1849; A. J. Graham to Strang, January 22, 1850; 
and C. J. Douglass to Strang, February 4, 1850. 


THE Saints BECOME PotyGaMous 103 


he had never known a colored man to behave in so shameful 
a manner, or to employ language so abusive and insinuat- 
ing.* Although Strang was temporarily driven from the 
meeting by the fanatics, he the next day denied the truth 
of the charges and asked for an investigation, the result of 
which was the excommunication of Wan Deusen from the 
Church and the suspension of Hickey “for most gross lying 
and slander” upon Strang and others. 

There can be but little doubt that both Hickey and Van 
Deusen were so unbalanced as to be practically insane. 
According to George J. Adams they were possessed of 
devils. He cast them out of Hickey, and bound them up so 
tight in Van Deusen that he could not speak, but ‘trembled 
like a reed shaken in the wind.” It is certain that the gossip 
conveyed to Hickey by his wife did not include any knowl- 
edge of the real identity of “Charley Douglass,” else he 
would have pointed to her, present in the meeting, as afford- 
ing conclusive material evidence of his charges against the 
Prophet. He shortly humbly confessed his fault, was re- 
stored to the good graces of the Saints, became himself an 
ardent polygamist, and died forty years later, a zealous 
advocate of the claims of Strangite Mormonism. 

Although Strang had successfully browbeaten his ac- 
cusers at New York, at Philadelphia and Baltimore the 
tongue of scandal wagged anew over the identity of his 
private secretary. In truth, it passes ordinary comprehension

how Elvira could have been expected to play successfully,

not, for within the circle of the Saints themselves her 
“physiological” peculiarities began to be discussed in a 
manner that can hardly be presented in print. The Prophet’s 
attitude in the face of this scandal is instructive to observe. 
The absolute truth of the story in circulation he, of course, 
knew better than anyone else; he knew, too, that the knowl- 
edge of “Charley’s” identity was shared by a number of 
his chief satellites in the faith. Yet he assumed an air of 
honest indignation, and boldly demanded that those Saints 
responsible for the story present proof that “Charley” was 
a girl, and gravely descanted upon their iniquity in present- 
ing his conduct to the world in an infamous light.* Of 
course they could present no absolute proof of their sus- 
picion, and equally of course Strang (had it been un- 
founded) could have silenced the gossip with perfect ease 
by procuring from any reputable physician a certificate 
establishing beyond cavil “Charley’s”’ sex identity. Not 
daring to resort to such a measure, however, nor willing to 
confess the fraud he was practicing, he strove to silence his 
accusers by a show of anger and wounded dignity. To such 
a strait was the vicegerent of God on earth reduced, and 
to such a depth of ignominy did the young bride who had 
been “honored” by being selected as the first to exemplify 
the divine institution of polygamy, find herself exposed. 

It is pertinent to inquire at this point concerning the 
attitude of Mary Perce, Strang’s legal wife, with respect to 
the institution of polygamy. It has commonly been asserted 
that upon learning of her husband’s advocacy of the insti-

tution, and of his exemplification of it with Elvira Field, 
she indignantly left him and went to live with her own

but it is clear, from various allusions in the Strang manu-

Mrs. Strang went east either with or shortly following her 
husband, and spent at least a portion of the winter with 
his relatives in western New York. In an affectionate letter 
to her on January 25, 1850, Strang alludes to ‘‘Charley’s” 
presence with him, and it does not seem probable, however 
it may have been with others, that Mrs. Strang could have 
been deceived by her husband’s invention of a sixteen-year- 
old “nephew.” Regardless of this point, there was no 
secrecy about Strang’s relations with Elvira from the time 
of the public unmasking of polygamy in July, 1850, yet 
Mary continued to live with him for many months there- 
after, and in April, 1851, he made her a member of his 
Council. The following month Mary left the island and 
from subsequent correspondence between the Prophet and 
Samuel Graham, who had succeeded to the place in his 
councils vacated by George J. Adams, it appears that Strang 
himself commanded her to depart, having been led to 
believe that she was stirring up opposition to his policies. 
Probably this departure marks the date of the final separa- 
tion between the two, although as late as October 11, 1855, 
Strang asserted in the [s/ander that Mary had not separated

The subject of polygamy and spiritual wifery affords as 
good a test as any of the sincerity and the prophetic pre- 
tensions of Strang. Either God approves of these things or 
he does not; and Strang’s prophetic pretensions were either 
valid or the opposite. If the Book of the Law of the Lord, 
which justifies polygamy, was in fact God’s law, written 
down on metallic plates prior to the Babylonian captivity, 
God must have been cognizant of it all along; and we face 
the puzzling fact that for half a dozen years prior to 1850 
God kept his earthly vicegerent in ignorance of this law, 
the while the Prophet in his name cast numbers of men and 
women out of the Church and into the torments of hell not 
for breaking, but for attempting to live up to this divine 
precept. But leaving God out of the question we find the 
man who, in 1847, held “unchangeable” opinions against 
polygamy, founded “on a full consideration of a// the scrip- 
tures, both ancient and modern,” a few years later vigor- 
ously contending that the practice offers the sole hope for 
the elevation of the race, and proving its divine character 
from both the New and the Old Testament alike.* 

Those of Strang’s followers who assented to the establish- 
ment of the Kingdom seem not to have objected to the po- 
lygamous dispensation. When it came, however, to the point 
of personally exemplifying it, their response was not very 
enthusiastic. There were never more than eighteen or twenty 
polygamous families at the Beaver Islands.¢ The Prophet 
had four wives (in addition to his first) and L. D. Hickey 


*Strang’s secret support of polygamy began in 1849, and his open ad- 
vocacy of it dates from the establishment of the Kingdom with the Book of 
the Law of the Lord as its corner stone in July, 1850. For extensive argu- 
ments by Strang justifying polygamy, see the Northern Islander, June 14, 
1855, and Book of the Law of the Lord, pp. 318-328. 

+ Statement of Wingfield Watson to the author. Watson, as a young 
married man, lived on the island from 1852 until the dispersion in 1856.

had three. All the other polygamous families contained but 
two wives. At the time of the dispersion in 1856 the Mor- 
mon population of the islands and the adjoining mainland 
was in excess of 2,500, and it seems reasonable to estimate 
that there were at least 500 heads of families. It follows 
that in only about one family in twenty-five did plural 
marriage prevail. The causes of this limited response to 
the Prophet’s teaching seem reasonably evident. Probably 
simple poverty, coupled with a scarcity of eligible females, 
were the chief factors in the premises. The Saints were highly 
industrious, but they had scant store of accumulated capital 
and they were engaged in the task, old in American history, 
of hewing homes out of the wilderness. Aside from the 
causes noted, the shortness of time permitted Strang’s King- 
dom to exist naturally operated to hinder any widespread 
development of polygamy. There is no room to doubt that 
the community believed thoroughly Strang’s teaching; and 
its sentiment was such that young women deemed it an 
honor to be considered eligible to become plural wives of 
the Prophet. 

As Prophet Strang was by far the most notable exemplar 
of polygamy in his Kingdom, particular attention may be 
accorded his marital career, concerning which more fiction 
has been put afloat than about any other aspect of Strangite 
Mormonism. It has been stated, among other things, that 
he provided different houses for his several wives. A letter 
received from Strang’s last surviving widow* a few years 
before her death sheds light on this and other points per- 
taining to the Prophet’s household: 

You ask if we all lived in the same house—we did but had 


separate rooms—and all met in prayer and ate at the same table— 
he [Strang] was a very mild-spoken, kind man to his family, 


* Mrs. Sarah A. Wing of Springville, Utah. The author interviewed her 
in the summer of 1920, and for a considerable period corresponded with her. 


108 Tue Kincpom oF St. JAMES 

although his word was law—we were all honest in our religion and 
made things as pleasant as possible—there were four of us living in 
one house. 


This testimony from one who had long lost all motive 
or desire to shield the Prophet or his established order, 
affords a rather pleasant picture of the practical operation 
of polygamy, contrasting sharply with some of the fiction 
which has been written about Beaver Island Mormonism. 
Human nature is a variable quality, however, and there are 
reasons for supposing that Strang’s own household exem- 
plified polygamy at its best. That all the polygamous hus- 
bands were judicious and mild, or that all the wives were 
free from personal jealousies and were loyal to the teachings 
of their religion, it would be hazardous to affirm. In another 
respect, also, the testimony of Strang’s widow contrasts 
sharply with popular tradition and recent fiction: she was 
not harried into marriage; rather, she married the Prophet 
in opposition to the wish of her father (one of the twelve 
apostles). When she told him of her intention to marry 
Strang, he answered that he would almost as soon see her 
buried as married in polygamy. “But I took the chance,” 
she relates. “I thought he was the Lord’s chosen Prophet and 
all would be right.” Strong indeed must have been the 
religious and social urge which would impel a beautiful 
and clever girl of seventeen to become, in opposition to her 
father’s judgment and wish, the fourth wife of a man of 
forty-two years of age. 

Strang’s first wife, Mary Perce, whom he married in 
western New York in 1836, bore him four children. The 
eldest of these, a daughter named Mary, died in babyhood. 
A son, named William J. Strang, spent his life as a rail- 
road man, rising to the position of conductor and living for 
many years at Terre Haute, Indiana. With him his mother 


Tue Satnts BECOME PoLyGaAMous 109 


and her second daughter, Myraette Strang, made their home. 
The latter, who was a teacher, remained unmarried. It has 
been more than once stated that when Strang was shot he 
ignored his plural wives, expressing a desire to be reconciled 
to Mary; and that on being carried to Voree she attended 
him until his death. However, contemporary evidence re- 
futes this story, which can only be regarded as a bit of 
amiable fiction. 

Strang married Elvira Field, his first polygamous wife, 
on July 13, 1849. Two and one-half years later, on January 
19, 1852, he took to wife Betsy McNutt. In the summer 
of 1855 he contracted his final marriage alliances with 
Sarah and Phoebe Wright. The two latest brides were 
cousins, aged respectively seventeen and eighteen years. 
Zenas Wright (brother of Sarah and cousin of Phoebe) 
has informed me that the ceremony was a double one, both 
girls being married on the same day. Mrs. Wing (formerly 
Sarah Wright) pronounces this incorrect: ‘“There was some 
rumor,” she adds, “that we were married at the same time, 
but there was a few months’ difference.”” Why this rumor 
should have arisen remains unexplained. Charles J. Strang, 
deriving his information from his mother, Elvira Field, 
records the date of his father’s marriage to Sarah Wright 
as July 15, 1855; and to Phoebe, as occurring on October 
27.* In the absence of other or more conclusive information 
we are constrained to accept these dates as correct. 

The Prophet was shot in June, 1856, dying on July 9. 
Thus, he lived with Elvira Field seven years, with Betsy 
McNutt four and one-half, with Sarah Wright almost a

year, and with Phoebe Wright about eight months. Children 
were born to him posthumously by all these women. In 
all Elvira Field bore the Prophet four children, Betsy 
McNutt four, and Sarah and Phoebe Wright one each. The 
essential facts concerning the careers of all these children 
are known to the author. Several of them are still living, 
and from two of these much valuable information has 
been received. They are cultured, high-minded citizens, 
have never shared their father’s religious faith, and are 
averse to having any notoriety imposed upon them in con- 
nection therewith. It would ill requite their kindness to the 
author if he should disappoint this desire by inflicting un- 
welcome publicity upon them. For this reason, while pre- 
senting data concerning the members of Strang’s family 
now dead, those still living will be left to the obscurity 
from public gaze which they covet. 

With the shooting of Strang and the mobbing of his 
followers in the summer of 1856 the members of his nu- 
‘merous family were scattered far and wide, never, in many 
cases, to meet again on earth. Only by a strong effort can the 
imagination visualize the deplorable plight of his polyga- 
mous wives, deprived of their protector, plundered of their 
property, the religious community which sheltered them 
blotted out, two of them with little, and all with unborn, 
children to care for—thus were they suddenly cast forth 
as wanderers in a cold and unsympathetic world. Not more 
pathetic, certainly, was the plight of the exiled Acadians 
than that of these forlorn women. Face to face with grim 
necessity they all made shift to exist, however, and all suc- 
ceeded in rearing their families, respectably if not in com- 
fort. 

Of Elvira Field it is recorded* that she lived as a widow

at Voree until 1860, when she returned to her girlhood home 
in Eaton County, Michigan. She had a desperate struggle 
to maintain herself and children, and in the year noted, 
believing herself fatally ill, she gave them to the care of 
friends. Recovering, in 1864 she took back all but the 
youngest, who had been adopted by David Grier and whose 
name had been changed to Charles J. Grier. In 1865 she 
married a widower, John Baker, and bore him two children. 
From 1873 until old age she lived in Lake County, Michi- 
gan; she died at Courtland in 1910. Her eldest son, Charles 
J. Strang, who died about a decade later, was for many 
years a printer, residing at Lansing. Although more than 
once importuned to assume his father’s prophetic mantle, 
he always resisted the appeal and held aloof from his 
father’s followers. 

Betsy McNutt was an “old maid,” thirty-one years of 
age, when she attained the earthly glory of handmaiden 
to a prophet in the flesh. Concerning this match Wingfield 
Watson has related* that Betsy, who was present at a social 
gathering at Saint James, was being teased about her single 
state. She responded to her tormentors that there was only 
one man on the island she would think of marrying. It be- 
came evident that she had in mind the Prophet, and some- 
one carried the story to him; whereupon, out of a sense 
of supposed gallantry he offered himself to her in marriage. 
Mr. Watson intimated that Betsy was not noted for her 
beauty, and further, that the Prophet was none too keen 
in driving a matrimonial bargain. However the case may 
have been with respect to Betsy, this remark seems out of 
place when applied to the four other wives he selected. 
Betsy never remarried after Strang’s death. A few years 
after the dispersion at Beaver Island she made her way to 


*In interview with the author.

the woods of Jackson County, Wisconsin, whither a con- 
siderable number of Strang’s followers had preceded her, 
and by picking berries and other shifts managed to eke out 
an existence for herself and children. When Evangeline, her 
eldest child, married John Denio of Lamoni, Iowa, Betsy 
went there to live; there she became a Reorganized Saint, 
and there died. Abigail, her youngest child and one of the 
Prophet’s posthumous children, passed her later years at 
St. Joseph, Missouri, a member of the Reorganized Church 
of Latter Day Saints, where she died in the month of May, 
1021. 

Sarah and Phoebe Wright, girl wives of Strang’s last 
year on earth, found their way, like Betsy McNutt, to 
the wilds of Jackson County, Wisconsin. To Phoebe was 
born in October, 1856, a daughter, Eugenia (the ‘‘Princess 
Eugenia” of A. N. Somers’ narrative ““An American King” 
in the National Magazine for May, 1901), who grew to 
womanhood in Jackson County and there became the wife 
of a banker of Black River Falls. She is still living (1929) 
in another state. Her mother, Phoebe Wright, made her 
home with her until her death, November 9, 1914; she 
never remarried after the death of the Prophet. 

Sarah Wright followed her parents to Racine, Wisconsin, 
upon the dispersion at Beaver Island. A few miles away, 
at Voree, her husband lay dying, but, destitute, she was un- 
able to be with him in his last days. She soon went with her 
parents to Jackson County, Wisconsin, where in November, 
1856, her child, James J. Strang, was born. She here mar- 
ried a Saint, Doctor Wing, and removed with him to Utah, 
where under his tutelage she learned the medical art. Doctor 
Wing developed polygamous inclinations, which Sarah had 
renounced after the death of Strang. Accordingly a separa- 
tion ensued, and Mrs. Wing for many years successfully 


Tue Satnts BEcoME PoLycaMous 113 


practiced medicine at her home in Springville. Here on the 
shore of Utah Lake, beneath the towering peaks of the 
Wasatch Range, she passed her later years, in an environ- 
ment as different from that of the Beaver Islands as the 
mind can readily conceive. The author interviewed her here 
in the summer of 1920, a few years before her death. 

It is a striking commentary upon the futility of human 
aspirations and strivings that of all Strang’s numerous 
family, not a single member remained loyal to his religious 
teachings and pretensions. Of his wives, two at least— 
Elvira Field and Sarah Wright—possessed more than ordi- 
nary capacity and talent. Both renounced Mormonism 
altogether, and Sarah even reared her son in ignorance of 
the very name of his father.* Betsy McNutt went over to 
the Reorganized Church, but before her death had become 
an adherent of still another of the “isms” which have 
afflicted the Saints since the demise of Joseph Smith. 

It remains to set forth the results for the future of Mor- 
monism of that dramatic proclamation of the Kingdom of 
God beside the pleasant harbor of Saint James on July 8, 
1850. The new development, with its embracement of plural 
marriage, proved too much for some of the Saints who had 
followed Strang’s teachings hitherto, and out of their revolt 
proceeded consequences of far-reaching importance. At Zara- 
hemla (near modern Blanchardville), Wisconsin, was a 
Mormon stake which had been organized but a short time 
before by Zenas H. Gurley, who had been for several years 
a zealous advocate of the Strangite faith. Living in the 
same community was Henry H. Deam, another follower of 
Strang, while at Beloit lived Jason W. Briggs, one of the 
most active of Strangite ministers. These men repudiated 
the doctrine of polygamy, and they were largely instru- 


* Statement in letter of Mrs. Wing to the author. 


114 Tue Kincpom oF St. JAMES 


mental in establishing the Reorganized Church of Latter 
Day Saints.* Their first measure, taken under Deam’s lead- 
ership, was to publish in the Mineral Point Tribune a pro- 
test against polygamy “‘and other abominations” practiced 
by Strang and his followers, and an announcement of their 
secession from them.+ Gurley, who had founded the local 
stake, was absent from home when this notice was published. 
On returning he asked the signers what they proposed to 
do next. The answer was they did not know; they believed 
the first principles of the gospel he had taught them, but 
they refused to accept polygamy and its attendant doctrines 
as taught by Strang and others. In their dilemma it was 
resolved to “‘seek to the Lord for wisdom” on the question 
who was the true head of the Church. They were answered 
repeatedly “through gifts of tongues, prophecy, and vision” 
that all pretenders to the prophetic office were impostors, 
and in due time “‘young Joseph” Smith would be called to 
the office, of which he was the rightful heir. During the next 
two or three years the Reorganized Church was established, 
with the usual accompaniment of visions, revelations, and 
angelic visitations, on the twin corner stones of monogamy 
and the leadership of young Joseph Smith. Today this 
church numbers 100,000 adherents and is a vigorous rival 
of the Utah or Brighamite faction of Mormonism. It claims

to be the true Church of Latter Day Saints, and on at least 
one occasion, wherein the issue of title to church property 
was involved, has enjoyed the satisfaction of having this 
claim formally validated by a decision of the United States 
Court. 


CHAPTER VIII 


Mormon Misdeeds and Gentile 


Persecution 


N an earlier chapter we have noted the fact that while 

at Voree Strang’s followers lived in peace and harmony 

with their gentile neighbors, the years of the Kingdom 
at Beaver Island were characterized by one prolonged period 
of turmoil and discord. Difficulties began with almost the 
first appearance of the Saints on the island, although for one 
or two seasons the Mormons, insignificant in numbers, bore 
the aggressions of the fishermen with comparative meekness. 
The first season there was no post office on the island, but 
one was shortly established at the store of the gentile trader 
on Whiskey Point.* The fishermen who made this their 
rendezvous, and whose habits are doubtless responsible for 
the name attached to the place, adopted the somewhat try- 
ing practice of abusing and beating up Mormons who visited 
the Point to inquire for mail.f In 1848 the public lands 
were thrown on the market. In accordance with a practice 
general on the frontier, individuals had made squatter 
claims, and had begun to make improvements before the 
land was opened for entry. The fishermen learned of the 
approaching land sale in advance of the Mormons and pro- 
ceeded to take advantage of their knowledge by marking 


* Whiskey Point lay across the harbor from the Mormon settlement, 
where today the lighthouse and life-saving station are located. 

+ The principal source of information for these events is Strang’s narra- 
tive, Ancient and Modern Michilimackinac. Although a partisan document, 
it can be relied upon as presenting the Mormon point of view of the events 
described. It is not improbable, however, that Strang’s sense of the dramatic 
led him to improve the narration by drawing upon his imagination for 
certain of its details. 


MispDEEDS AND GENTILE PERSECUTION 117 


out the most desirable tracts of land. It was then proposed 
that at the sale all should respect these squatter claims, 
whether occupied or not, and no one should buy in the land 
to which another had laid claim. The Mormons respected 
this agreement; but two of the fishermen, Thomas and 
Samuel Bennett, proceeded to enter several lots on which 
Mormons had located and built their homes, and the latter 
saw themselves deprived of their property. By this initial 
act of aggression the Bennetts laid the groundwork for a 
quarrel of large dimensions, eventuating finally in a tragedy 
which we shall shortly have occasion to notice. 

The first considerable migration of Saints to the islands 
began in 1849, and therewith rascally steamboat captains 
began the practice of opposing settlement here by persuad- 
ing their passengers to go on to Wisconsin or other points. 
When persuasion failed, they sometimes resorted to open 
breach of contract, refusing on one pretext or another to 
land passengers who had bought tickets for the islands. In 
one instance over fifty emigrants from Otsego County, New 
York, had taken passage on the Empire State under especial 
agreement to land them at Big Beaver. The boat passed the 
island in the daytime, running closely along beside it, but 
the captain refused to land his passengers. During the winter 
of 1849-50 a party from Whiskey Point descended upon 
a group of Saints who were getting out timber for the 
Temple. They drove the men from their work, severely 
beating one of them who refused to heed their commands. 
Others visited the lyceum which the Mormons started that 
winter, and by “rude and filthy” conduct broke it up; while 
still others called at the Mormon homes in the absence of 
the men and insulted and terrorized the women and chil- 
dren. 

Matters came to a crisis with the summer of 1850, when 


118 Ture Kincpom oF St. JAMES 


the Prophet and the principal leaders of the Church, ac- 
companied by a large number of followers, moved up to 
occupy the land God had set apart for them. The Mormons 
now abandoned their nonresistant attitude. They let it be 
known that violence would meet with proper resentment, 
and rowdies who sought to disturb their meetings found 
themselves thrown out with resolution and dispatch. The 
Prophet himself went to a fisherman’s home, with a body- 
guard of Mormons armed with clubs, and seized a cow, the 
ownership of which was disputed, overawing by this show 
of force a group of Irishmen who gathered with shilalas to 
beat him up and rescue the cow. 

Temporarily, at least, the day of beating Mormons on 
Beaver Island was over, and from their despised estate of 
previous seasons the Saints now found themselves in the 
ascendant. The fishermen, on their side, were spurred to new 
violence by this situation and by the spectacle of the in- 
creasing tide of Mormon immigration. The invitation was 
sent out to all the fishing grounds to gather at Whiskey 
Point in readiness for the Fourth of July, and the climax 
of the celebration of national independence was to be the 
expulsion of the Mormons from the island. Aid was en- 
listed also from Mackinac, and several small vessels from 
there were engaged to bring supplies, liquid and martial, to 
the Point and be on hand for the fray. To keep such a design 
secret was impossible, particularly from so shrewd and 
enterprising an opponent as Strang. The latter had reasons 
of his own for calling in his followers from far and wide, 
and accordingly notice had been sent out for a church con- 
ference at Saint James in early July. The men were or- 
ganized as a guard, drills were held, and arms and ammuni- 
tion procured. The latter even included a cannon, while to 
offset the gentile navy a schooner, procured from Chicago, 


MisDEEDS AND GENTILE PERSECUTION 119 


was anchored in the harbor and in the nighttime secretly 
manned with a force of armed men. 

In the night of July 3, while the fishermen were carousing 
on Whiskey Point, a few men from the Mormon camp went 
among them as spies and learned their numbers and plans; 
they also dumped some powder into the Lake, and doped a 
barrel of whiskey with tobacco, which rendered those who 
used it excessively drunk. The gentile plan was to attend 
the Mormon meeting, armed with concealed weapons, and 
at the proper moment start a disturbance, and overpower 
or kill the Mormon men; after which the women and prop- 
erty of the Saints would be at their mercy. 

Their disillusionment began at daybreak, when the Mor- 
mons began firing a salute with their cannon, affording to 
the fishermen their first inkling of the presence of such a 
weapon; their interest mounted when the cannon balls from 
across the harbor began skipping past Whiskey Point 
unpleasantly close to the trading house where they had 
gathered, and they perceived that the artillerymen were 
getting the range of the buildings there. Before their interest 
had time to cool, a delegation of Saints called upon Mc- 
Kinley, the trader,* to notify him they would hold him re- 
sponsible for any damage done by the mob he was harboring, 
and that the first sign of an attack would be the signal for 
destroying his establishment. In readiness for such an attack 
the Saints garrisoned their tabernacle, and in front the ar- 
tillerymen waited, with the cannon unlimbered and a fire 
wherein a supply of heated balls was in readiness for instant 
use. The fishermen had no stomach for such a contest as im- 
pended, and after finishing their carouse went their several 

* Peter McKinley from Ohio had bought Cable’s trading house at the 


Point. One writer states that he was a cousin of William McKinley, later 
President of the United States. 


120 Tue KINGDOM OF ST. JAMES 


ways, resolving “‘to pay off the damned Mormons” on a 
more propitious occasion.* 

Hardly had the fishermen been routed and the coronation 
festivities concluded at Saint James when the Prophet be- 
came involved in a dispute of far-reaching consequences 
with his own Prime Minister, George J. Adams. For four 
years Adams had been, next to the Prophet himself, the 
most conspicuous figure in the Strangite organization. Much 
of his time had been devoted to proselyting work in the 
East, and from his various centers of activity he had sent 
back to the Prophet a flood of reports of great successes 
won, yet his character was such that, although he enjoyed 
the confidence of the Prophet, in the eyes of many other 
Saints his support was of more than doubtful value to the 
Church. In the winter of 1849 the Advent Herald of Boston 
made an attack upon Adams, stating among other things 
that he had been excommunicated by Joseph Smith for an 
unnamable offense.f About this time Strang received a 
revelation promising that if Adams would reside near him 
and assist him in presiding over the Church the Lord would 
“make him a host, and the head of nations.’ Defending 
Adams against the Advent Herald, therefore, Strang argued 
that his moral lapse was no worse than that of Judah, while 
his acting was no lewder than the spectacle of “King David 
dancing naked before the maids of Israel.”’ In any event, 
it was God who had called Adams, and Strang, his mouth- 
piece, had no responsibility in the matter. 


* Minutes of addresses by Strang and others at the conference preceding 
the coronation ceremony, preserved among the Strang MSS, show that the 
Saints were fearful of an attack by a gentile mob. Strang and George J. 
Adams were outspoken in their defiance of the gentiles. Strang affirmed 
that he would with his own hand execute the death sentence upon the 
mobbers, while Adams called not merely for their defeat, but for a gener- 
ous letting of gentile blood.

Before many months, however, Adams was to outrage 
decency in a fashion which could not be thus complacently 
overlooked. While preaching to “‘vast throngs” at Boston 
in the winter of 1849—50,* he became acquainted with a 
woman of “indifferent fame” whom he took to Baltimore 
and there introduced to his associates as a wealthy widow 
from Charleston, South Carolina.t On returning to Beaver 
Island in the spring of 1850, Adams brought this woman 
with him in the réle of Mrs. Adams, giving out that his 
former wife, who was in an advanced state of consumption, 
had died. It presently transpired, however, that the real 
Mrs. Adams was still living in New Jersey, where her hus- 
band had abandoned her. For this offense against decency 
and humanity, the Prophet subjected Adams to discipline, 
depriving him of his authority as a member of the First 
Presidency of the Church.t 

Enraged over his humiliation, Adams proceeded to Macki- 
nac and there joined forces with the gentile element whose 
slaughter he had been demanding only three months earlier. 
On October 20 he returned to Beaver Island accompanied 
by Tully O’Malley, Sheriff of Mackinac County, armed 
with a writ for the arrest of the King on the charge of 
threatening the life of the pretended Mrs. Adams. Evading 
the Sheriff, Strang proceeded to Mackinac on a schooner and 
there took quarters at a hotel, where a few days later he 
was arrested and brought to trial before Charles O’ Malley, 
a justice of the peace. The ensuing proceedings were a mere

travesty on the name of justice. The magistrate, as grossly 
ignorant of legal procedure as he was prejudiced against 
the accused, repeatedly committed Strang to prison, from 
which he as often secured release on habeas corpus pro- 
cedure. The testimony of Adams and the other witnesses 
for the prosecution, is of more interest than these details. 
They swore that they believed Strang to be a dangerous 
person, and that he had forty men on Beaver Island sworn 
to do his will, even to the extent of committing murder; 
while one witness claimed Strang had said in his presence 
that if Mrs. Adams stayed on Beaver Island until Christ- 
mas “she should ride around the Bay on a black-horned 
buck, with her face backwards and the boys to hoot at her, 
and whoever kept her company should share her fate.” 
Whatever truth there may have been in these statements, 
they are entitled to no credit on the strength of the testi- 
mony of Adams. He was responsible, next to Strang him- 
self, for whatever iniquity there might be in the Strangite 
organization. He had given too many glowing testimonials 
of Strang’s divine calling and upright character* to gain 
credence now for his accusations of Mormon iniquity. Never- 
theless, the apostasy of Adams wrought serious injury to the 
Kingdom. His sensational charges were spread by the press 
throughout a wide region, and thus an impression was dis- 
seminated that the Mormon Kingdom was a seat of unparal- 
leled villainy.t The practical consequence of this belief

was the development of a readiness to believe any accusa- 
tion, however preposterous, that might be made against 
the Mormons, and a corresponding feeling of sympathy with 
their opponents, without troubling to inquire into the justice 
of their contention. 

In vain did the Prophet, who wielded a facile pen, com- 
bat the flood of accusations directed against conditions on 
Beaver Island. The popular judgment had been rendered, 
and all efforts to modify it proved futile. An editorial in 
the Northern Islander for April 3, 1851, began, ‘“‘No fact 
can be more certain than that throughout the United States 
there is no legal protection to a Mormon.” Proceeding, it 
stated that Strang and all the leading Mormons lived in 
continual consciousness that there were men around them 
who were seeking their lives; and that should they be killed 
the murderers would never be brought to justice by the au- 
thorities. It was stated that one Praiseworthy Bower had 
offered to join a band, if one could be gotten up, to burn 
the houses of some twenty Mormons on the island, while 
Thomas Bennett was threatening to have Strang’s life before 
another harvest time. The editor gave warning that the Mor- 
mon leaders would not permit themselves to be picked off 
one by one, but would unitedly resist their enemies. The 
Mormons had made their last retreat from persecution, and 
would now “be crushed as a people and swallowed up in 
one common ruin” rather than purchase temporary respite 
by sacrificing one another. While making light of the threats 
of Bower and Bennett, their confidence was not placed in 
the sanctity of the law, “for we well know it has none. It is 
in the certainty that should such an aggression be com- 
mitted, the aggressors would be wiped off the face of the 
earth before they found rest for the soles of their feet.” 

Such language, which found frequent repetition in the 


A Tue Kinepom oF St. JAMES 


Northern Islander from this time forward, marks a great 
change from the pacifist attitude of Strang in the earlier 
days of his ministry at Voree. Then he had thundered elo- 
quently against the policy of political and military arro- 
gance at Nauvoo, which he rightly pointed out as the factor 
chiefly responsible for rousing the gentile mobs against the 
city. Now he was leading his people along a similar course, 
which could only end in the expulsion of the Saints from 
the city of Saint James, even as they had already been 
expelled from Far West and Nauvoo. 

A new assault upon the Mormon Kingdom was made by 
the authorities at Mackinac with the opening of navigation 
in the spring of 1851. Somewhat earlier than this, a Mor- 
mon constable from Saint James had endeavored to arrest 
Eri Moore, a fisherman, who was selling whiskey to the 
Indians on Garden Island.* Anticipating opposition, the 
Constable took with him a posse of thirteen men, who were, 
of course, Mormons. With the aid of his friends Moore 
defied the posse, and after some shooting which did no 
harm, the Mormon party retired from the field. In this 
affair the Mormons seem to have been clearly in the right. 
Yet Moore proceeded to Mackinac and persuaded Justice 
O’Malley to issue warrants for the arrest of thirty-eight 
men of Saint James who were charged with being concerned 
in the transaction against Moore. 

The Mormon story of what followed represents that 
the Sheriff descended upon Saint James with a posse of 
“thirty intoxicated Indians and eight or ten drunken Irish- 
men” and without having encountered any resistance or 
attempted to execute his warrants began hunting the Mor- 
mons down like wild beasts. 


* Northern Islander, May 1, 1851; Ancient and Modern Michilimackinac, 
pp. 29-31. 


MIsDEEDS AND GENTILE PERSECUTION 125 


Let the citizens of the United States [wrote the Prophet in the 
Islander] imagine a tribe of wild savages, first made drunk, then 
provided with arms and ammunition, and then let loose upon the 
peaceable inhabitants of a town, with authority to make prisoners 
of all the town officers and principal public men, have them whoop- 
ing about your houses and swearing death against the men they are 
ostensibly sent to arrest, and you have but a faint picture of the 
scene that Mr. Sheriff Granger has created for his malignant pleasure 
upon Beaver Island. 


About the time of Granger’s visitation, Strang had gone 
with a company of men to Hog Island to try to save a 
yawl which had been lost from the wreck of a steamer some 
time previous. Granger led his posse to the island, and first 
taking precaution against the Mormons’ escaping by steal- 
ing their boat and chopping up the yawl, fell at midnight 
upon their sleeping camp. The alarm was raised too soon, 
however; the Mormons escaped to the woods, and all but 
one soon found their way to a leaky fishing boat on the 
opposite side of the island. It was early April, and the lake 
was covered with fields of floating ice. The men were but 
half-clothed, had no food, and the boat was without sails 
or oarlocks. In this desperate plight they put to sea and after 
twenty-four hours succeeded in making Gull Island. Here 
were some unoccupied fishermen’s shanties, where they re- 
mained five days, burning the huts for fuel and subsisting on 
fragments of provisions left behind in the shanties. During 
this time they contrived, without ax or other tools, to put 
their boat into such a state of repair as to render possible 
their return to Saint James. Twelve days elapsed in all be- 
fore they could return to rescue the man left behind on Hog 
Island. He was found still alive, having subsisted on fish 
heads and other scraps of refuse, including in his diet some 
rawhide oarlocks. The conclusion of all this disturbance 
was the taking of fifteen or twenty men to Mackinac, where 


126 Tue KincpoM OF ST. JAMES 


two or three were released on bail and the charges against 
the remainder were dropped. Those let to bail were dis- 
missed at the next term of court. 

A few weeks after the Granger raid a tragedy took place 
at Big Beaver from which the Mormons derived additional 
unwelcome notoriety. Toward the end of May, Samuel 
Graham, one of the Mormon leaders, went to Whiskey 
Point to have an interview with Sheriff Granger. Before he 
reached the house where Granger was, Richard O’Donnel 
and James Hay assaulted him with a cane, fracturing his 
skull and inflicting other serious injuries.* For this offense 
warrants were issued successively, first by County Judge 
Greig, and then by M. M. Aldrich, justice of the peace. 
Both these magistrates were Mormons, however, and the 
Sheriff refused to execute the warrants. 

The Mormons then appealed to William N. McLeod, 
Prosecuting Attorney for the county of Michilimackinac, 
who proceeded to Saint James and assumed direction of 
the matter. A warrant for the arrest of O’Donnel and Hay, 
issued by Justice Aldrich, was put into the hands of William 
Chambers, a Mormon constable. Repairing to the fishing 
ground armed with this warrant, Chambers was stopped 
by Thomas and Samuel Bennett, armed with guns, who told 
him the fishermen had banded together to resist all attempts 
to arrest any of their number. On reporting his rebuff to the 
Justice, that official issued a new warrant for the arrest of 
the Bennetts on the charge of obstructing an officer in the 
discharge of his duty, and Chambers summoned a posse to 
assist him in executing both warrants. Those summoned at 
first demurred, but on the threat of the Prosecuting Attorney 


*For this affair see Northern Islander, August 14, 1851; Ancient and 
Modern Michilimackinac, pp. 33-35. 


MIsDEEDS AND GENTILE PERSECUTION 127 


to take legal action against any who ignored the Constable’s 
summons, a posse of thirty or forty men was raised. With 
these Chambers returned to the house of the Bennetts and 
summoned them to surrender. They responded by opening 
fire on him and he fell, but the posse immediately returned 
the fire, killing Thomas Bennett and wounding Samuel. 
Hay made his escape, and O’Donnel fled in an open boat, 
but was pursued and placed under arrest. Within close 
range were some threescore fishermen, pledged to resist 
arrest, but they made no move to come to the relief of the 
Bennetts, being overawed, probably, by the suddenness of 
the affair and the evident determination of the posse. 

A coroner’s jury, composed of six Mormons and six 
gentiles, convened over Thomas Bennett’s corpse, brought 
in a verdict in accordance with the facts, although three of 
the gentile members declined to sign it on the ground that 
the Constable had not been shown to be a citizen of the 
United States. The Mormon procedure in this affair would 
seem to have been entirely justified, yet far and wide in 
the press the killing of Thomas Bennett was represented 
as an act of cold-blooded murder, and Bennett as an inno- 
cent citizen who had merely sought to uphold his rights in 
the face of “Mormon law.” Even the post-mortem surgical 
examination was pictured as a barbaric mutilation of the 
body. It is a sad commentary on the popular voice, which 
has been frequently supposed to be the voice of God, that 
it uniformly condemned whatever the Mormons might do, 
misrepresenting their acts, however innocent, to their dis- 
advantage. A large number of Mormons were arrested for 
complicity in the killing of Bennett, and held in durance 
at Mackinac for many weeks. At length they were dis- 
charged, no bills having been found against them. Since 


128 Tue Kincpom oF ST. JAMES 


the administration of justice was at this time wholly in 
gentile hands this outcome of the affair tends strongly to 
establish their innocence of wrongdoing.* 

A much more celebrated assault upon the Mormons, 
wherein the accused again came off scatheless, was being 
made contemporaneously with the Bennett affair. A some- 
what lurid account of the case, written in after years by 
George C. Bates, the United States District Attorney who 
prosecuted it,} represents that before the petty Kingdom of 
Prophet Strang the highest authorities of the United States 
government stood appalled. The nation was still agitated 
by the bitter struggle over the compromise measures of 1850, 
and the sudden death of President Taylor followed by the 
accession of Millard Fillmore to the presidency, had thrown 
the Whig party into a state of confusion. The electorate of 
Michigan was almost evenly divided between the two chief 
political parties, and the Mormon vote of Beaver Island, 
wholly controlled by Strang, might easily determine the 
outcome of the state election. With this situation to face, 
the Democratic state administration of Michigan was not 
eager to antagonize the Mormon ruler. The Governor solved 
the difficulty by “‘passing the buck” to the Whig federal ad- 
ministration, appealing to President Fillmore to bring to an 
end the “disorder, riots, and crimes of the Mormon people 
at the Beaver Islands.’ The President, however, was ‘‘cau- 
tious, cold, and calculating’; he was even now laying his 
plans to secure the Whig nomination to succeed himself at

the Baltimore convention of 1852, and he had a lively con- 
cern over the possibility that the Beaver Island Mormons 
might cast the deciding vote in the coming election against 
him and in favor of the Democratic candidate. Webster, 
his “godlike” Secretary of State and the dominant figure 
in the Administration, had, like his chief, an interest in the 
approaching presidential convention and a tender regard 
for the Mormon vote, and he felt that the reduction of the 
Mormons to order might well be left to the Democratic 
Michigan state government. After many notes had been 
passed and much red tape cut, however, Senator Stephen 
A. Douglas, “a gallant, true, and straightforward Demo- 
crat,”’ who had had much experience with the Mormons in 
Illinois, was called into consultation, and ‘‘then, as always, 
his voice was for war on anybody and everybody who 
resisted the constituted authorities of the nation.” The 
Administration, its backbone thus strengthened, now issued 
orders through the Attorney-General to the District At- 
torney at Detroit to institute legal proceedings against 
Strang and his confederates. Simultaneously, through the 
Secretary of the Navy, the command went forth to Captain 
Bullis of the U.S. Steamship Mzchzgan to proceed to Detroit, 
equipped for action, and place himself under the orders of 
the District Attorney. 

The course of human affairs presents to the observer, 
oftentimes, a strange intermingling of tragedy and comedy. 
In the matter before us the Mormons supply the tragedy and 
the United States government as represented by District 
Attorney Bates the comedy. It was a case of Don Quixote 
going out in real life, in the middle of the nineteenth 
century, to make war upon a windmill. The fevered imagi- 
nation of the District Attorney, fed upon the exaggerated 
or mendacious tales emanating from Mackinac and caught 


1320 THE Kincpom oF St. JAMES 


up and embellished by the press of a wide region, perceived 
in the tiny island community which, at least at this particu- 
lar time, desired above all else to be let alone, a distinct and 
deadly menace to the peace and security of the great Re- 
public. Warrants were obtained charging Strang and his 
confederates with treason, robbery of the mails, counter- 
feiting, and trespassing upon United States lands. Equipped 
with these and supported by forty well-armed men under 
the command of “General’’ Schwartz, Bates embarked on 
the Michigan for the distant Beavers. 

The District Attorney’s story of what followed would 
not withstand a very severe cross-examination, but it is 
interesting, nevertheless, for the light it sheds upon the 
hysterical state of mind of the local gentile public upon 
the Mormon question. At Mackinac, Judge Greig, the sole 
Mormon official of the county, was holding court. Before 
reaching this place the District Attorney devised, ‘‘with the 
aid of Captain Bullis’s United States Navy,” a highly in- 
genious plan for the capture of those against whom the 
warrants he carried had been issued. At half after three 
o’clock the steamer anchored in the Strait half a musket 
shot away from the front door of the courthouse, and her 
cannon was trained upon the building. The captain’s gig 
was now lowered “with all the pomp and ceremony of war,” 
and in it the brave Attorney, accompanied by an officer or 
two, pulled ashore. Proceeding to the court room he ordered 
the amazed Judge to consider himself under arrest and 
adjourn the court and come on board. The Judge was direct- 
ing his aides to arrest the intruders for contempt of court, 
when the District Attorney apprised him “that by raising 
his eye he would see the guns of the Michigan trained upon 
him and his courthouse,” and that any hesitancy or resist- 
ance “‘would result in the destruction of the building and 


MIsDEEDS AND GENTILE PERSECUTION 131 


his own death.” The clerk was directed to “enter the ad- 
journment” and the Attorney’s aides to seize the Judge and 
ce 


conduct him on board the ship, where Captain Bullis “in 
full naval uniform’ received him and escorted him below. 


In less than half an hour from the time the Michigan let go her 
anchor it was tied up again, and she was steaming gently away 
toward the Beaver Island with the Hon. J. M. Greig as prisoner, 
confined below decks, in utter amazement at the coolness and impu- 
dence of those authorities by whom he had been taken by violence, as 
he thought, from the judgment seat of the kingdom of James the 
First. 


Well might he be amazed, for surely such procedure was 
never elsewhere heard of outside the pages of Gilbert and 
Sullivan, and Judge Greig was born too early to enjoy the 
privilege of perusing these. Mackinac was not a Mormon 
town, but instead the chief stronghold of the gentile oppo- 
sition; and whatever the case might have been at Saint 
James, at Mackinac Judge Greig had none but gentile 
associates. The performance of the District Attorney in 
arresting him was, therefore, as needless as it was ridiculous. 

But worse treatment was yet in store for the abducted 
Judge. From Mackinac to Saint James the distance is about 
fifty miles. Orders were issued for the Mzchigan to proceed 
slowly enough to arrive in the harbor about two o’clock in 
the morning. E” route the District Attorney subjected the 
Judge to a third degree performance calculated to extort 
from him a letter to Strang and directions where the King 
could be found. That his residence was but a few yards 
from the water’s edge was known, of course, to every fisher- 
man at Mackinac, but the Attorney was intent, apparently, 
on learning this from other sources. When the Judge proved 
recalcitrant, he pointed to the yardarm over the cabin and 
“in the most solemn manner possible” told the prisoner that 


132 Tue Kincpom oF St. JAMES 


unless he gave such a letter within one hour he should swing 
from it “as certain as the stars twinkled above it.’’ Captain 
Bullis was now called in, of whom the Judge inquired, 
pardonably enough, whether “the District Attorney was 
drunk or crazy”; whereupon the naval officer produced his 
orders placing him at the command of the District Attorney 
and confirmed that dignitary’s representations by saying 
that if ordered to do so he should be bound to “‘swing” the 
prisoner from the yardarm. The unhappy Judge now 
yielded, and not only wrote the desired letter but drew a 
chart to show how the King might be reached in the night. 
Armed with this, and followed by a suitable body of men, 
each ‘“‘well armed with navy revolver, cutlass, and a covered 
ship’s globe lantern,” the Attorney “pulled with muffled 
oars to the beach,” and leaving a sailor to guard the boat, 
set out by a circuitous pathway for the home of the King, 
who was slumbering peacefully in his bed, as unconscious 
of the impending naval visitation as Judge Greig had been 
in his court room a few hours earlier. Stationing the “‘big 
boatswain” at one end of the house, and the ‘“‘young boat- 
swain” at the other, the Attorney and his “faithful deputy” 
entered; creeping quietly up the stairs they found them- 
selves in “a long, low room, where wide berths, heavily 
draped with stunning calico, shielded beds like the berths 
and staterooms of steamers, which proved to be occupied 
by Mormon women four in a bed.” 

We forbear, from considerations of space, to describe in 
detail the further adventures of the bold intruders upon 
this enchanting scene. Suffice it to say, that with the aid of 
Judge Greig’s letter, representing that but for the Prophet’s 
presence he would be hanged at dawn, that worthy was 
enticed on board the Michigan. Here, after a “hearty break- 
fast,” the King was induced, by the same threat of hanging

which had already intimidated Judge Greig, to send out

ere the sun went down in that beautiful bay the steamer Michigan,
