and devils. 


We turn for a moment to the more prosaic, but not for 
this reason less truthful, account of the arrest given by its 
principal victim, Prophet Strang. He relates that President 
Fillmore, being in Detroit on a visit to his brother, there 
heard complaints of Mormon offenses against the United 
States laws, such as treason, counterfeiting, mail robbery, 
and trespassing upon public lands. The President ordered 
a Sweeping investigation to be made and placed the U.S. 
steamer Michigan at the disposal of the District Attorney 
in carrying it out. The first intimation the Mormons had 
of this measure was afforded when the ship arrived at 
Mackinac just after Judge Greig had adjourned court. That 
official went on board and with great difficulty persuaded 
the District Attorney and the Marshal to go about the 
matter peaceably, avoiding any hostile demonstration. In 
pursuance of this advice, on arriving in the harbor of Saint 
James about midnight, the Attorney went ashore alone and 
inquired for Strang. An interview was soon arranged, which 
Bates began by seeking to convince Strang of his helplessness 
and the wisdom of surrendering peaceably. On asking to 
see the papers, the Prophet was told they were on the ship. 
It was agreed that Strang should go aboard to inspect them, 


134 Tur Kincpom or Sr. JAMES 


after which he might surrender himself to the officer charged 
with his arrest or at his option be put ashore with the pledge 
that he should not be pursued or molested for a space of 
fifteen minutes. 

On the Michigan, Strang was shown a list of thirty-eight 
names (besides his own) of men charged with trespass, 
while some of them were also charged with the other crimes 
we have noted. He sent the list ashore with the request that 
the persons concerned come to the wharf to be arrested, and 
within two hours thirty-one men, all of those sought who 
were on the island, did so. An awning was spread on the 
deck of the vessel and preparations were made to hold a 
commissioner’s court, when McKinley, one of the com- 
plainants, expressed his inability to produce his witnesses, 
since some of them were nearer Detroit than Saint James. 
Court was thereupon dismissed and an agreement entered 
into that Strang and three others should go under surveil- 
lance to Detroit, there to be the subjects of further judicial 
proceedings; Strang became responsible, also, for the appear- 
ance of four others at Detroit should bills be found against 
them; the rest of the defendants were discharged. 

The trial of Strang and his associates in the United 
States District Court at Detroit on the charge of robbing 
the mail went on from the latter part of May until the ninth 
of July, the case being tried and decided adversely to the 
accused in the press long before the conclusion of its passage 
through the court. It is significant of the character of the 
government’s evidence against these men that during the 
entire trial, although accused of the gravest of crimes, they 
were permitted to go unconfined and free of bail, their 
presence being secured by the mere personal pledge of Strang. 
Although the defendants were charged with attacking a 
mail train in broad day in close proximity to an Indian 


MIsDEEDS AND GENTILE PERSECUTION 135 


village and in the presence of several white men, the U.S. 
commissioner sent to take testimony on the spot could 
obtain, after the examination of scores of witnesses, no sup- 
porting evidence. The jury, composed of ten Whigs and 
two Democrats, acquitted the defendants, and the remain- 
ing indictments against them were therefore dismissed.* 

The District Attorney attributed his failure to the judge’s 
charge to the jury against prosecutions for religion’s sake. 
“Although the evidence was quite clear against them,” the 
jury ‘“‘seemed to have imbibed the idea that these men were 
on trial for religion’s sake,’ hence the acquittal. Strang 
made a dramatic and powerful speech to the jury, com- 
paring himself to Christ, and the prosecution to the lawyers 
and Pharisees who persecuted him. 

From the vantage point of eighty years’ detachment from 
the scene of conflict, we are disposed to agree, in the main, 
with the jury. The Mormon régime which Prophet Strang 
had set up at the Beaver Islands was necessarily productive 
of consequences of the gravest import for the peace and 
happiness of this section of the state of Michigan. But the 
charges made by District Attorney Bates were the product 
of excited gentile imaginations, and the utter collapse of 
the prosecution affords, as Strang himself shortly pointed 
out, the best evidence of the innocence of the accused. That 
timber had been cut by the Mormons on public land is, 
indeed, undeniable; but men did this everywhere on the 
frontier without any sense of wrongdoing; and in the par-

ticular case before us Strang produced an official letter from 
Bates giving him permission to cut government timber. As 
for counterfeiting, Strang explained that George J. Adams 
had persuaded a young fellow at Mackinac to tell a tale of 
a counterfeiting rendezvous in a cave in the sand heap which 
the Mormons dignified by the name of Mount Pisgah. Bates’ 
party had taken the youth aboard at Mackinac, and being 
unexpectedly called upon to verify the story in a legal pro- 
ceeding he had gone blindly on until the moment arrived 
to point out the place. Then he broke down and “in shame 
and confusion” confessed the deception. 


CHAPTER IX 


The King as Lawgiver 


HIS establishment of a theocratic kingdom within 

the borders of the United States was attended by 

numerous complexities, some of which are still 
difficult to explain. Strang was a shrewd and able man, and 
he could not fail to realize the folly of engaging in rebellion 
against the government of the United States.* He therefore 
adopted a course designed to reconcile as skilfully as might 
be the anomalies of the situation in which he had placed 
himself. Far from opposing the federal and state authority, 
he made use of the votes at his command to gain control 
of the agencies of local government, and so well did he 
succeed that within a few years he was entertaining visions 
of controlling not merely the Michigan state election but 
the choice of President of the United States itself. To his 
own followers he was the ruler of an actual kingdom to 
which they owed a more real and intimate allegiance than 
to the government of the United States. But he had the 
sense to realize that this rule could not be imposed upon 
unbelievers, and that its practical application even to his 
own followers was subject to numerous limitations.+ In 
short, the Prophet had in theory made himself the head 
of a theocratic monarchy; but in practice, pending the de- 


* The language he employed during the period of the Kingdom seems 
often to belie this conclusion, but his conduct until the end of his career 
bears it out. 

+ For example, the church law prescribed the death penalty for adultery. 
Such an ordinance obviously could not be enforced at Beaver Island. 
Strang did not hesitate to state publicly in his paper, however, that only 
the necessity of deferring to the civil law prevented the enforcement of 
the church law on this subject. 


138 Tue Kincpom oF St. JAMES 


struction of “Babylon the Great,” which all Mormons con- 
fidently anticipated, he ruled as best he might through a 
combined process of dictation to his followers, and manipu- 
lation of the ordinary agencies of local government. 

The character of the rule Strang aspired to establish may 
be clearly seen in the pages of the Book of the Law of the 
Lord. Here, in forty-seven chapters, a complete framework 
of government is provided, applicable to any population, 
however great, and laying down regulations for the most 
important relations of human society. A more centralized 
autocracy, it is safe to say, the mind of man never devised. 
Strang himself was King of the Earth by direct appointment 
of God.* For the support of his government a thorough- 
going system of tithing was provided,+ with sweeping pro- 
visions for its enforcement. All lands were in the hands of 
the King, to apportion to his subjects at his discretion.£ The 
King had complete control of highways, including the au- 
thority to lay them out, to provide for their administration, 
and to levy tolls upon those who used them.§ The King was 
the fountainhead of justice, having power ‘‘over the Princes 
and Rulers, and over all that sit in judgment.’’ || He declared 
the laws and commandments, and it was his duty to destroy 
traitors and overthrow rebellion. Needless to add, perhaps, 
he controlled the army and navy.{| He might appoint vice- 
roys, but these officials were limited in all respects by the 
power that created them. He appointed councilors and 
judges, apostles and priests. He ordained deacons, “men of 
stout heart, strong arm, and swift foot,” in every city to 
be keepers of the prisons and execute the judgments of the 
courts. 

Whatever may be thought as to the divine origin alleged 


* Book of the Law of the Lord, chap. xx.  +/Jbid., chap. xlvi. 
+ Ibzd., chap. xli. § Ibzd., chap. xlv. 
|| Zbzd., chap. xxi. ( Ldcd., chap. xxii. 


Tue Kine as Lawciver 139 


for the Book of the Law of the Lord, many of its provisions 
were well in advance of the age. Thus, isolation, the chief 
drawback to American rural life, was obviated by the device 
of granting a town lot as a part of each “inheritance” and 
requiring the Saints to live together in villages and cities.* 
Half a century in advance of the modern movement for the 
conservation of natural resources, far-sighted regulations 
were provided concerning the planting of trees and the 
preservation of the timber supply. Trees were to be planted 
by the roadside both in town and country, while in the 
cities there was generous provision for “abundant publick 
parks and watering places,” to which old and young might 
resort for rest and recreation. 

Along with these beneficial regulations, two surprising 
changes in the Strangite organization accompanied the 
establishment of the Kingdom: the practice of polygamy 
was adopted and the institution of communism was aban- 
doned. The first of these changes has been discussed in an 
earlier chapter. As for the latter, despite its divinely or- 
dained basis, the communistic Order of Enoch had evidently 
proved a sad failure. Although the revelation of January 
7, 1849, had expressly commanded its establishment at the 
Beaver Islands, to be observed by all the Saints, it was now 
quietly abandoned in favor of the device of individual 
inheritances. A shadow of communism was sought to be 
preserved, however, in the institution of “princes” or 
patrons. Groups of Mormons were encouraged to associate 
themselves under the patronage of a chosen leader, sharing 
his table and house.+ Even this faint approach to com- 


* An “inheritance” was an allotment of land by the Prophet to a Saint, 
in proportion to his need. The design at Beaver Island contemplated that 
each inheritance should consist of a village lot and about 160 acres of farm 
land. 

+ Allusions in Book of the Law of the Lord, and oral information of 
Wingfield Watson, who attached himself for a time to one of these groups.

munism, however, seems never to have become firmly rooted 
among the Saints of the islands. | 

The fortunes of the Mormon Kingdom seemed at their 
nadir during the trial of Strang and his associates at Detroit. 
In the season when the growing crops demanded the labor 
of all, the island was for weeks deprived of the services of 
the great majority of its adult male population. Strang 
records that between April 11 and June 23, 1851, ninety- 
nine Mormons were arrested on Beaver Island and carried 
to distant places for trial on criminal charges. In addition, 
many were taken as witnesses, so that at one time but 
twenty-four men were left on the island.* In this emergency 
the women went into the fields and cultivated the crops 
which were essential for the subsistence of the settlement 
through the winter. 

To add to the trials of the Saints, hostile individuals 
clothed with official authority went through the settlement 
in the absence of its protectors, and plundered at will the 
property of the Mormons. They even apportioned among 
themselves the farms of the Saints who were undergoing 
trial at Mackinac or Detroit, awaiting only the expected 
news of their conviction to enter into possession of the 
property.+ But none were convicted, and upon their return 
to their homes the injured Saints served notice of their in- 
tention to exact legal reparation of their despoilers. Doctor 
McCulloch, from whose dock a large quantity of wood had 
been taken, began a prosecution of one of the offenders, in 
the course of which the details of the peculations were laid 
bare. In consequence the plunderers hastened to make resti- 
tution to those they had wronged, their zeal in this being 
quickened by the prompt conviction, with exemplary dam- 


* Ancient and Modern Michilimackinac, p. 35. 
} Northern Islander, August 14, 1851. 


Tue Kino as LAWGIVER 141 


ages awarded, of such as rejected the Mormon overtures 
for a fair settlement. It is perhaps needless to add that these 
trials were held in a Mormon court, the magistrate of which 
was King Strang himself, sitting in the capacity of justice 
of the peace at Saint James. 

The revulsion of the Saints from the despair of early 
summer, when all but a few of the faithful believed the 
settlement was about to be broken up, was rapid. In a 
mid-August editorial appeal to the Saints abroad to hasten 
their “gathering” to the islands, the Islander states* that 
Strang, who three months before was being hunted by sixty 
armed men with the avowed intention of shooting him at 
sight, now “‘sits quietly in his own house, without keeping 
one of his trusted friends by to assist him, and dispenses 
the law to these same men as a Justice of the Peace; and 
when business requires, goes alone and unarmed in the 
midst of them, without molestation or danger.’ Never, since 
the first Mormon set foot on the island, had they been as 
secure from danger as at the present time. “A strong hand, 
never departing from the right, will secure respect any- 
where,” the editorial concluded; “but vacillation makes us 
despised.” 

The Beaver Islands had, in 1847, been erected by the 
State Legislature into the township of Peaine; although a 
part of Emmet County, the township was attached to 
Mackinac for judicial purposes, to Newaygo for the elec- 
tion of state representative, and to Lapeer for the election 
of state senator. These political circumstances, combined 
with the geographical features, gave the Mormons com- 
plete control upon the islands. They proceeded, in the 
spring of 1852, to enact in township meetings numerous 
ordinances suitable to the government of the community. 


* Thid. 


142 Tue Kincepom oF St. JAMES 


A portion of Paradise Bay was designated as a quarantine 
station; a hospital for the care of persons afflicted with 
contagious diseases was established; elaborate provisions 
governing the keeping and control of dogs, domestic ani- 
mals, and gunpowder were enacted; a board of health was 
charged with a periodical inspection of the fishing stations.* 
That all authority on the islands centered in the hands of the 
Prophet is amply evident. He was the township supervisor, 
and one of the township justices of the peace. As supervisor 
he was ex officto moderator of the township meetings, and 
member and chairman of the board of health. Thus, to his 
complete control of the Mormon population by virtue of 
his position as head of the Church,} he added control of the 
gentile element by reason of centering the civil government 
of the township in his hands. A serious drawback from the 
Mormon point of view, however, was the fact that the ad- 
ministration of Mackinac County, to which Peaine Town- 
ship was attached for judicial purposes, was bitterly hostile 
to the Mormon régzme. 

From this measure of gentile control Strang shortly 
emancipated his Kingdom by turning to his advantage one 
of those wholly unusual situations which are more likely 
to arise on the frontier than in older and better organized 
communities. The legislative apportionment law passed in 
1851 attached the unorganized counties of Emmet and

for canvassing them. Unless this situation was due to some 
oversight on the part of the framers of the law, it would 
seem to lend color to the Mormon charge that it was de- 
liberately planned to disfranchise the Mormon settlement. 

At the election of November 2, 1852, Strang put him- 
self forward as candidate for the State Assembly from the 
Newaygo district, his candidacy being kept secret until the 
day of election, and received every one of the 165 votes 
cast at the Beaver Islands.* The avowed candidates for the 
office remained blissfully ignorant of their dark horse rival, 
or even of the fact that the Beaver Islands were included in 
the Newaygo district, until the election canvassers at distant 
Newaygo were suddenly confronted by one of the publishers 
of the Northern Islander, who announced himself as can- 
vasser for all the unorganized counties attached to Michili- 
mackinac, and convinced them that the Beaver Islands were 
in their district. The solid Mormon vote which he carried 
sufficed to elect the Prophet to the State Assembly. The 
Islander had strongly predicted this result on November 11, 
long before news of the canvass could be returned to Saint 
James. Thus by a clever coup the ruler of the island king- 
dom had attained the vantage point of the State Legisla-

ture, from which to prosecute his further designs for the

Never, perhaps, in legislative history did a member- 
elect enter upon his duties under more discouraging auspices, 
or conclude them with more substantial credit, than fell 
to the lot of the new member from Newaygo district. His 
opponents at Mackinac, intent on preventing him from 
taking his seat at all, disinterred an old indictment against 
Strang, the existence of which the court officials had for a 
long time ignored, and proposed to throw him into prison 
as he passed through Mackinac en route to the state capital. 
This design the Prophet forestalled by journeying by way 
of Green Bay. The District Attorney for the Upper Penin- 
sula thereupon engaged an officer to arrest Strang at Lansing 
immediately before the call of the House.* For the rest, 
his certificate of election was abstracted from the files of 
the Secretary of State, and his nearest opponent in the elec- 
tion was on hand prepared to take possession, unopposed, 
of the contested seat. 

Through the ingenuity and determination of Strang the 
plot totally miscarried. He resisted the attempt to remove 
him from the chamber, and laid before the House a dupli- 
cate certificate of election. A committee appointed by that 
body to inquire into the matter rendered a report declaring 
the arrest had been attempted not with a view to the fur- 
therance of justice, but with an intent to accomplish some 
unworthy purpose, and to deprive the House and Strang’s 
constituency of his services. By unanimous vote of the House 
this report was adopted and Strang was discharged from

arrest. The petition of his Newaygo opponent for leave to 
contest his seat was then brought up and referred to the 
committee on elections, which returned two reports, one 
favorable and the other adverse to Strang’s title. Both 
parties were thereupon given leave to be heard at the bar 
of the House. An advocate spoke for Strang’s opponent, 
while the Prophet himself undertook to plead his own cause. 
According to the Lansing State Journal, he spoke ‘“‘with a 
force of reasoning, energy, and eloquence which, whatever 
may be the result, will leave a most favorable opinion of his 
personal qualifications for the -position he claims.’’ The 
“result”? upheld Strang by a vote of forty-nine to eleven, 
three-fifths of the Whig votes being cast, significantly 
enough, in his favor, in addition to practically nine-tenths 
of the Democratic votes. 

The effort to deprive Strang of the office to which he 
had been elected had resulted in making him the best ad- 
vertised member of the House; and the tact and ability 
which he displayed in defending his title to the seat won 
general sympathy and respect. ‘He is a talented man, equal 
to any other man in debate and general information,” said 
the Jackson Citizen. “Mr. Strang is confessedly the most 
talented and ready debater in the House,” wrote the Tem- 
perance Advocate; while a little later the Detroit Pree Press 
succinctly summed up his legislative career as on the whole 
“an eloquent and honorable one.” 

The Newaygo district as then constituted comprised over 
twenty counties, and the Mormon Prophet was the legisla- 
tive representative of one-fourth the area of the state. For 
the government of this sparsely settled region there had 
been passed at different times a motley conglomeration of 
legislative acts which had never been harmonized into one 
consistent whole, and which stood in sad need of compre- 


146 THE Kinepom oF ST. JAMES 


hensive revision. Thus, Oceana and Newaygo were or- 
ganized counties, detached from all others, yet they had no 
judiciary other than justices of the peace and judges of 
probate,* and no crime could be legally punished. The 
Grand Traverse settlements had no legal county organiza- 
tion, nor were they attached to any existing county; they 
were, therefore, “utterly without law.” Approximately one- 
fourth of the state had but a single organized township, 
and it was a matter of doubt or dispute as to what county 
a number of the largest settlements in the district belonged.t 
To his credit, Strang made himself the guardian of the 
varied interests of the whole Newaygo district. During his 
term of office the organization of Grand Traverse County 
was perfected, two new counties and eight new townships 
were organized, and a judiciary and a municipal govern- 
ment were provided for all parts of the district. Although 
“its local wants were more numerous than those of any other 
district,” boasted the Northern Islander of May 5, 1853, 
“not a local measure was committed to Mr. Strang’s charge 
(except a petition to change the name of a township sent 
the last week of the session) but it was carried.” 

But such service did not suffice to win for Strang surcease 
from the sort of opposition which had marked the beginning 
of his legislative career. Along with the other constructive 
work of his first term, Strang had secured the complete sepa- 
ration of the Beaver Islands from Mackinac County by the 
organization of the county of Emmet. It comprised, in addi- 
tion to the islands, a considerable district of the adjoining 
mainland, then inhabited chiefly by Indians, along with 
some gentile whites, who were chiefly fishermen congregated

practically the unanimous vote of his locality, while one 
Lay of Grand Traverse County obtained all but six of the

a powerful argument demonstrating the superiority of 
Strang’s title from whatever point of view the matter was 
examined.} The schemers now passed a resolution in caucus, 
according to the Is/ander, that no bill introduced by Strang 
should be allowed to pass. Notwithstanding, he succeeded 
in playing again a prominent role in the legislature. Even 
in those cases where his measures were defeated “‘the justice 
of God did not slumber,’ for they included measures of 
no concern to the Mormons but of much importance to 
Grand Traverse and Mackinac counties, whose spokesmen 
had been chiefly instrumental in placing difficulties in the 
path of Strang’s legislative efforts. 

Of particular importance from the point of view of the 
present narrative was the action taken changing the bounds 
of Emmet County and creating the new county of Manitue. 
From the county of Emmet as organized by the act of 
January 29, 1853, secured during Strang’s first legislative 
session, the Beaver Island group, North and South Fox, and 
North and South Manitue Islands were removed and or-

mainland portion of Emmet County as originally created 
was reorganized as the county of Emmet. The Mormons 
claimed that the original act of 1853 creating the county 
was regarded by all parties in the light of a compromise 
measure; the territory thus organized was to be regarded as 
a Mormon reserve, which they might develop and govern 
as they should see fit, thus putting an end to the unhappy 
clashes which had so long recurred while the Beavers were 
attached to the county of Mackinac. The avowed motive 
of the new legislation was to restrict the Mormons to the 
islands.* But once again the “justice of God’ operated to 
the confounding of the gentiles; for when the first election 
was held in the reorganized county of Emmet, the entire 
Mormon ticket was chosen without opposition. 

Thus, instead of one county, the Saints were now in con- 
trol of two, with the prospect of establishing a settlement 
in Antrim County on Grand Traverse Bay; and their official 
organ, the Northern Islander, exultingly inquired whether 
the settlers of Grand Traverse County, who had sent a peti- 
tion to the State Legislature for the dismemberment of 
Emmet County, were not gratified over the fact that by this 
means they had “brought the Mormons twenty-four miles 
nearer home.’ Some thirty Mormons had settled in Emmet 
County just before the election, too late to acquire the right 
to vote. Although the majority of the population were 
Mormons, the majority of the legal voters were gentiles. 
The result of the vote seemed to refute the representations 
made at Lansing the winter before as to the dislike of the 
gentile element of the county for the Mormon rule. John S. 
Dixon had spent the winter at Lansing capitalizing the anti-

Mormon prejudices in an effort to have the township

A notable indirect consequence of the organization of 
Emmet County was the renewal of open hostilities between 
Mackinac and Beaver Island, culminating in the battle of 
Pine River in July, 1853. The legislature of 1851 had 
passed an act to restrict the sale of intoxicating liquors, 
which was followed by still more stringent measures in 
1853 and 1855. The act of 1851 penalized the sale or gift 
of intoxicating liquor to Indians, and further provided that 
no one might vend in less quantity than twenty-eight gallons 
unless he should first give bond with two or more responsible 
sureties to answer for any damages that might result from 
their use. As long as Mackinac County had jurisdiction over 
the fishing grounds this law remained, outside of Peaine 
Township, a dead letter, but on the organization of Emmet 
County the Mormons promptly initiated measures looking 
to its enforcement within their jurisdiction. The act of the 
same session organizing Emmet County gave to it jurisdic- 
tion, concurrently with other counties, over the waters of 
Lake Michigan. Within the limits of Emmet County were 
several hundred Indian families, who made their living 
chiefly by fishing. Left to themselves they were at least 
reasonably industrious and prosperous,f but, as ever with 


* Ibid., June 14, 1855. 
+Strang stated that the average annual family catch of fish “when 


150 Tue Kincpom oF St. JAMES 


the red man, they were unable to resist the fatal lure of 
alcohol. The traders found in the debauchery of these 
Indians an exceedingly profitable source of revenue. Strang 
estimated that the five hundred Indian fishermen of Emmet 
County, even when debauched by liquor, took $100,000 
worth of fish in a year. Three-fourths of their pay was 
received in “Indian whiskey,’’* which cost the trader five 
or six cents a gallon, and the Indian fifty cents to one dollar; 
the remainder, in goods of poor quality at such prices as an 
intoxicated Indian could be induced to pay for them. 

There was nothing peculiarly heinous in the conduct of 
the Mackinac traders, for we have here but the ancient 
tale, as old as the Indian trade, of the debauchery of the 
red man by the white. The peculiar element of the particular 
situation before us was supplied by the Mormons. Commonly 
the only opposition to the traders’ supplying the Indian with 
whiskey proceeded from distant governmental edicts or 
from missionaries to the red man. As a rule the trader 
ignored or defied these authorities with equal impunity. 
But the Beaver Island Mormons had a peculiar interest in 
the welfare of their Indian neighbors. One of their primary 
motives for settling on the islands had been that of con- 
ducting there missionary work with the Indians who con- 
gregated from a wide region at the upper end of Lake Michi- 
gan; in addition, the Saints held in peculiar abhorrence 
intemperance and the chain of vicious practices which fol-

The Mormon ruler initiated his program of law enforce- 
ment by assembling all the men of the county who could 
qualify as sureties,* and on discussion all pledged them- 
selves not to become bondsmen for any person to sell 
liquor. That the traders might be forewarned of the in- 
tended enforcement of the law in Emmet County, a notice 
was published in the Northern Islander of May 5, 1853, 
warning them that authority to sell liquor in Mackinac did 
not carry with it the right to sell on Lake Michigan. From 
Old Mackinac§ to the west line of the state, and south to 
Grand Traverse Light, the Lake and all the bays were now 
under the jurisdiction of Emmet County. 


If the trade is persisted in [the notice concluded] the sheriff will 
go out with sufficient force and make arrests in all such cases. The 
law will be enforced whatever the cost. He is a fool who at this day 
thinks he can defeat it by crying pirates, robbers. That cry has been 
raised once too much.

The response of Mackinac to this notice was both prompt 
and plain-spoken. A notice issued May 16, over the names 
of the county supervisor, the district attorney, and four 
justices of the peace, requested the citizens to meet at the 
courthouse the following day ‘at the ringing of the bell,” 
to devise ways and means of protecting themselves against 
the “felonious depredations’”’ of the Mormons. The meeting 
adopted a series of resolutions inveighing against the “high- 
handed pretensions and immoral course of the people called 
Mormons.” Coming to the immediate point in question, it 
resolved that the organization of Emmet County and Drum- 
mond Township,* “taken in consideration with the avowed 
determination of the Mormons to make forcible arrests 
under color of law’ was highly dangerous to the rights and 
interests of the citizens of Mackinac. A Committee of Safety 
was therefore created, charged with the duty of memorializ- 
ing the President and the Governor of Michigan, besides 
taking such other steps as might be conducive to the ‘‘public 
safety.” Some resolutions sent down by the fishermen of 
Pine River and Gull Island, expressing the determination to 
take the law into their own hands were read, and it was 
voted to publish the proceedings in the Detroit papers. 
Although no express approval was given the utterance of 
the fishermen, they were described as “seventy resolute men 
who know the importance of the step they have taken,” and 
the whole purport of the proceedings was to encourage them 
in their course. 

It would be charitable to suppose that the district attor- 
ney who presided over this gathering, and the four magis- 
trates who lent their influence to its proceedings, failed to

realize the true character of their course. In all the turbu- 
lent history of the liquor business in America it would be 
difficult to find a more unblushing defiance of law and order, 
or a bolder commendation of an announced intention to 
defy it, than the one offered to the world by these magis-

Pine River which followed a few weeks later, they cannot

Emmet County, Strang answered the ebullition from Macki- 
nac with a dignified and crushing rejoinder, which appeared 
in the Islander of June 30. 


Our reply to Mackinac and her allies [it concluded] is, “let words 
cease and deeds begin.” We prefer peace; but if war must come, let 
it be upon us and not upon our children. We shall not yield a step 
to the threats, and are ready today for the blow. 


One of the most attractive spots on the Great Lakes is 
the beautiful resort region which centers at the modern 
town of Charlevoix, on the Michigan mainland opposite 
the Beaver Islands. Here, at the present time, is a fine in- 
land harbor, through which the waters of Pine Lake find 
their way into Lake Michigan. Pine Lake is a beautiful 
sheet of water, running many miles inland; its shores are 
dotted with summer cottages, and into it empty some of 
the most noted trout streams of northern Michigan. In 
Strang’s day, Pine Lake emptied by a shallow stream into 
Round Lake, a small sheet of water, and a short outlet, 
known as Pine River, connected the latter with the waters 
of Lake Michigan, perhaps a third of a mile away. In their 
natural state, this outlet and the one connecting Pine and 
Round lakes could be traversed only by small boats. Govern-

may be entered with ease by the largest lake steamers, and 
Round Lake has become the harbor for the port of Charle-

At Pine River had assembled, as early as the summer 
of 1852, a considerable group of fishermen, who, at least 
in the eyes of the Mormons, constituted a settlement of out- 
laws. In the autumn of 1852 a Mormon officer from Beaver 
Island called at Pine River in search of some men whom he 
had warrants to arrest on the charge of stealing. “Almost 
the entire population” fled to the woods upon his approach. 
“The Pine River Settlement,’ commented the Northern 
Islander,* “is little else than a band of vagabonds and 
thieves, and the sight of an officer of the law sets them all 
in a twitter.’ It drew consolation from the fact that lack 
of provisions would compel the fishermen to scatter on the 
approach of winter, and the improbability that they would 
ever reassemble. 

Contrary to this pious hope, the summer of 1853 found 
the fishermen assembled at Pine River in greater numbers 
than before, and animated by a common determination to 
resist the authorities of Emmet County. The issue came on 
July 12, when the Sheriff of the county set out from Saint

trouble, fourteen men, even though well armed, were all too 
few, since the settlement contained five times this number, 
and the only possiblility of retreat was across the open lake, 
a distance of many miles.

As they approached the landing, armed men could be seen

way of signals. The Mormons landed on a narrow beach, 
under a bluff which was covered with a growth of timber 
and bushes. The Sheriff quickly explained his mission, and 
in less than half an hour his party was preparing to embark 
for the return voyage to Saint James. Meanwhile, armed 
men had been gathering on the bluff, and a party of thirty 
or more had filed down a narrow path to the beach and 
stationed themselves close to the boats. A considerable surf 
was breaking on the shore, and it was difficult to get the 
boats off. They had just been launched, and the last man 
was climbing in, when a murderous fire from both beach 
and bluff was poured upon them. In view of the closeness 
of the assailants to their victims, one must either suppose 
the fishermen to have been marvelously poor marksmen, 
or accept the Mormon theory that the escape alive of any 
of the Saints afforded “an extraordinary instance of the 
care of God for his creatures.” As it was, six of the fourteen 
Mormons were shot, all of them at the beginning of the 
attack, while the boats were pushing off. 

Before these men lay twenty-five miles of open water. 
Behind them, in hot pursuit, came three boatloads of fisher- 
men, the largest manned by twenty-five men. For ten miles 
the Mormons, aided by a light wind, kept beyond musket 
range of their pursuers. Then, the wind dying down, the 
latter drew nearer and opened fire on the laboring boats. 
These were riddled with bullets and it seemed likely the 
occupants would all be slain, when the bark Morgan, bound 
from Buffalo to Chicago, came in sight, and in response to

A large number of the attacking party were recognized [said the

party from here who would make Pine River settlement as bare as 
the palm of a man’s hand; but the moral effect of sending a half 
dozen to State Prison is worth more than the death of them all. 
Legal remedies are better than violent ones. 


Whether or no a copy of the Northern Islander extra was

later, a posse came from Saint James to make arrests, not 
a soul was to be found. The “‘seventy resolute men” who but 
a few weeks before had published to the world their deter- 
mination to take the law into their own hands had vanished. 
Two years later the Saints held a conference on Holy

a gallows on which they hung in effigy the men who had 
made the assault upon their compatriots on that July after-

The permanent settlement of the Pine Lake region dates 
from the year following the dispersal of the fishermen. 
Before the actual settlement it had been erroneously sup- 
posed that Pine River afforded a valuable site for water 
power development. The busy brain of Prophet Strang had 
already marked out this region as a suitable one for Mormon 
exploitation, and while at Lansing attending his first legis- 
lative session he had procured a plat from the land office 
and marked on it the more desirable tracts of land to 
preémpt. In this he was outwitted by some speculators, 
however, who abstracted the plat from his private papers, 
and hurrying to the land office, entered for their own benefit 
the tracts he had marked on it.f They then proposed to sell 
to Strang one-third of the land entered, at five times the 
cost of the whole. He ignored this proposition, and before 
long John S. Dixon, buying of these speculators, located 
at the mouth of Pine River. About the same time a few 
Mormons settled in the vicinity, adjoining Pine Lake, and 
between the members of this tiny group of Saints and 
gentiles promptly developed a state of antipathy and dis- 
cord similar to that existing on a larger scale between 
Mackinac and the Beaver Islands. In the gentile press of 
the state appeared lurid stories of Mormon outrages com- 
mitted, or about to be committed, upon Dixon. These Strang 
answered with circumstantial denials in the Islander, and 
added to the controversy the interesting suggestion that the

speculators who had originally sought to outwit and hold

peace.* 

Mormon plans for the settlement of the Pine Lake terri- 
tory were largely held in abeyance during 1853 by reason of 
the hostilities with the fishermen and the citizens of Macki- 
nac. With the spring of 1854, however, the gentile menace 
in this quarter having been dissipated, the Islanders again 
turned their attention to the exploitation of the country 
surrounding ‘‘Lake Mormon.” Strang had long urged his 
people to render themselves economically self-sufficing by 
developing farms enough to raise on the Beaver Islands 
sufficient food to support the population. He now pointed 
out} that the chief value of the Pine River region was agri- 
cultural, and that it offered the best location for farming 
to be found in this section of the state. The proper situations 
for settlement were several miles inland, but there was good 
connection by water with the outside world. If “the breth- 
ren” would go in early this season they would find the 
country unoccupied, and would encounter no danger of 
gentile interruption. How many actually responded to this 
appeal is not now in evidence. A census of Emmet County 
taken near the close of 1854 showed 1,706 settlers in Charle- 
voix Township.t Evidently the great majority of these 
were Indians; but of the white population, the Mormons 
apparently constituted the majority. Mormon optimism 
foresaw a much larger population settled here in the near 
future. In November, 1854, the Is/ander,§ denouncing the

CHAPTER X 


The Downfall of the Kingdom 


' , y ITH the developments recounted at the close of 
the preceding chapter we reach the zenith of 
Strang’s prophetic and royal régime. For ten 

years as a Prophet and more than five years as Prophet and 
King he had successfully outridden every storm which had 
risen to blast his designs and cut short his career. Politically, 
he had gained a strategic position in the state of Michigan, 
and was now dreaming of entering Congress* and even of 
dictating the issue of the coming presidential election. 
Economically, the Mormon King felt strong enough to de- 
clare trade boycotts against Mackinac and Detroit and to 
threaten Buffalo with similar treatment unless the residents 
of that city should manifest a becoming attitude in their 
treatment of the Mormons.} Apparently he could at last bid 
defiance to all gentile opposition. 

Notwithstanding the seeming success which had been 
achieved, the Kingdom contained within itself the seeds of 
dissolution. The autocratic rule which Strang had estab- 
lished at length bred a disaffection which eventuated in 
his assassination at the hands of a group of his own fol- 
lowers; and these same men took advantage of the heritage 
of gentile hatred which the years had developed to bring 
down upon Strang’s bewildered followers a destroying mob 
which completed the destruction of the Kingdom of God 
which had been so hopefully initiated on that July day of 
the year 1850. 


* Northern Islander, July 19, 1855. 
{+ Ibzd., August 24 and September 13, 1855. 


THe DownFaLt OF THE KiIncpom 163 


One of the leading Mormons at Saint James was Dr. 
Hezekiah D. McCulloch, formerly of Baltimore, who had 
been converted to the Strangite doctrine and had “gathered” 
to the islands about the time of the establishment of the 
Kingdom. His education, ability, and possession of property 
enabled him speedily to attain a leading position in the 
councils of the King, and for several years he bore a promi- 
nent share in the activities which centered at the Beaver 
Islands.* He was made a judge of the highest tribunal of 
the Church, and a member of the King’s confidential council. 
While Peaine Township was attached to Mackinac County 
he was elected to the office of coroner, and on the organiza- 
tion of Emmet County he became clerk and register of 
deeds. But he was addicted to drink, and persisted in scan- 
dalizing the Mormon cause by publicly drinking at the bars 
of vessels which touched at Saint James. For this he was 
rebuked, and the project of sending him to the State Legis- 
lature at the expiration of Strang’s first term was aban- 
doned. Another set of complaints charged him with dis- 
honest business practices. Gradually the breach between 
him and the Saints widened, until from a leading councilor 
of the Church he became its avowed enemy. If Strang’s 
story of the trouble may be credited, McCulloch developed 
at this juncture a decidedly small, not to say contemptible, 
spirit. While taking advantage of his church membership 
to collect his debts in the ecclesiastical courts, he refused to 
submit to their jurisdiction in cases where injured Saints 
sought to prosecute him. He also refused to turn over the 
records of his county office to his successor, and by such 
acts as suppressing the public records of land titles, strove 


* A long and somewhat pathetic account of McCulloch’s relations with 
the Mormons was published by Strang in the /slander for June 19, 1856. 


164. Tue Kincpom oF St. JAMES 


to create as much trouble for his former compatriots as 
possible. 

We have dwelt somewhat at length upon the case of 
McCulloch, for he was the real assassin of Strang. Although 
others’ hands fired the guns which slew him, it was the 
vengeful brain of Doctor McCulloch which conceived the 
plot and inspired the instruments who executed it. Those 
instruments had, of course, grievances of their own, which 
found their common origin in the character of the Mormon 
administration of affairs on the islands. Strang’s authority 
over his followers proceeded, as we have already seen, from 
two distinct sources: for the governance of the faithful in 
their relations with one another, his position as head of 
the ecclesiastical theocracy to which they belonged was all- 
sufficient; for their government in such matters as they 
found themselves involved with gentiles, his control over 
the local organs of the civil government could be invoked 
whenever the occasion should arise for doing so. The church 
law differed from the civil law in numerous respects. This 
occasioned no difficulty as long as the followers of Strang 
submitted with cheerfulness to the arbitrament of the for- 
mer; but when they opposed its administration a situation 
pregnant with potential discord presented itself, and the 
church authorities (which, practically speaking, meant the 
King) must either forego the application of the church law 
or, persisting, commit acts unwarranted from the point of 
view of the civil law. If the recalcitrant Saint chose to with- 
draw from the Kingdom and resume his life in the outside 
world he might, indeed, do so, but only at the penalty of 
forfeiting any real estate he might have acquired, since the 
Law of the Lord gave to his Prophet James complete control 
over this class of property. 

Loyal adherents, no less than gentile enemies, agree that 


THe DownFALL OF THE Kincpom 165 


Strang was an inflexible ruler. At Voree he did not essay the 
role of temporal ruler, and no occasion arose, therefore, for 
discord with respect to such matters; but in the ecclesiastical 
realm there was one unceasing round of excommunications, 
and these were explained by the church organ itself on the 
ground that “Mordecai now sits in the king’s gate’—in 
other words, the Prophet was inflexible in enforcing the 
church law upon those who might transgress it. With the 
establishment at Beaver Island of a theocratic kingdom 
Strang assumed the role of a temporal ruler, and coinci- 
dent therewith bitter criticism arose over the enforcement 
of the authority he had assumed. 

The unsavory case of George J. Adams, affords as good 
an illustration as any of the nature of the difficulty thus 
encountered. Strang charged that the woman Adams had 
brought to the islands was a prostitute. To Mormons, sexual 
irregularity is peculiarly heinous, and the authorities under- 
took to drive “Mrs. Adams” from the community. One may 
sympathize with their desire, without conceding the legal 
authority of the King to have her ridden “around the Bay 
on a black-horned buck, with her face backwards and the 
boys to hoot at her.” The charge that he threatened to do 
this may have been an invention of Adams,* and in the 
absence of any specific admission by Strang, we are unable 
to determine the truth of this particular matter. It is freely 
admitted, however, that the King resorted to the infliction 
of far more severe punishments on other occasions, unsanc- 
tioned by state law. As early as January, 1851, a correspond- 
ent of the Cleveland Plain Dealer, who had roused the 
wrath of the Prophet for sending to that paper an article 
about alleged Mormon misdeeds, was publicly promised, 


* See the Northern Islander, December 12, 1850, for the testimony on 
this subject. 


166 Tuer Kincpom oF St. JAMES 


if he would ‘‘walk up within the dominion of the ‘Mormon 
king,’ a good horsewhipping, administered according to 
Mormon law, in the presence of a multitude of witnesses.’”’* 

Presumably the correspondent did not make haste to ac- 
cept this invitation, but the giving of it, publicly, is indica- 
tive of the existence thus early in the history of the Kingdom, 
of a system of enforcing church decrees which was unknown 
to the law of the land. How often the practice was invoked 
we have no means of knowing, since ordinarily it would be 
kept out of the church paper, and no record be made of it. 
The crime of adultery was, to the Mormons, one of the 
gravest of human offenses, and their law made it punishable 
with death; “and except for the civil authority [we] would 
not hesitate a moment in inflicting the penalty,” declared 
Strang’s official organ.f Two cases of adultery had oc- 
curred on the islands up to this time (March, 1856) and 
in both the offending man was “subjected to the most ter- 
rible personal chastisement, thankful to escape with life.” 
What degree of severity this punishment involved may be 
inferred to some extent from the report in the spring of 
1856 of another offender who for “lying, tale-bearing, and 
endeavoring to incite to mischief and crime,” was given, 
after a hearing, thirty-nine lashes with birch rods.t 

The old proverb, cherchez la femme, finds fresh illustra- 
tion in this general connection. The Law of the Lord devotes 
a chapter, among other things, to the subject of “apparel 
and ornaments.” “Ye shall not clothe yourselves after the 
manner of the follies of other men,” it begins, “but after 
the manner that is seemly and convenient, shall ye clothe 
yourselves.” There follows a disquisition by the Prophet 
upon the follies perpetrated in the name of fashion. What 


* Northern Islander, January 9, 1851. } Ibid., March 13, 1856. 
tIbid., April 3, 1856. 


THE DowNFALL OF THE Kincpom 167 


might constitute “seemly and convenient” was left to the 
Prophet to expound. In this, as in many other matters, his 
action was well in advance of the age. He proceeded to 
prohibit “every form of dress which pinches or compresses 
the body or limbs’; aside from such styles, ‘“objection- 
able because they injure health, shorten life, and produce 
hereditary diseases and weakness,” he inveighed against a 
“numerous class of fashions” which were less gravely objec- 
tionable; this included bell-crowned hats, enormous sleeves, 
hoop skirts, and other monstrosities of apparel with which 
our grandmothers were wont to afflict themselves. On the 
constructive side, he prescribed for women a short skirt with 
pantalet trousers. A capable observer who lived on Beaver 
Island from 1852 to 1856 testifies that the Mormon women 
genuinely liked this style of dress and discarded it “with 
many a regret’? when compelled thereto in order to avoid 
impertinent observations among gentiles.* But a few, at 
least, of Strang’s female followers objected to the uniform 
thus imposed upon them, and in the Islander of May 1, 
1856, one such woman is held up to public scorn for her 
wilful course.t Whether this woman was Mrs. Thomas 
Bedford is not clear, but we have the information from 
several sources that Bedford encouraged his wife in her 
refusal to wear the dress prescribed by the Prophet, thereby 
incurring the condemnation of the latter. Another backslid- 
ing Mormon, between whom and King Strang a feeling of 
bitterness developed, was Alexander Wentworth. One of

Bedford and Wentworth seem to have lacked the enter- 
prise and courage unaided to compass the King’s murder, 
but the estrangement of Doctor McCulloch brought to the 
little group of malcontents the requisite brain and resolu- 
tion to bring the thing to pass. To these conspirators was 
added, in the spring of 1856, another precious character, 
in the person of “Doctor” Atkyn. What he was ‘‘doctor’’ of 
is not now in evidence, but he had paid various visits to 
Saint James from 1850 on, coming finally, in the autumn 
of 1855, to open a daguerreotype gallery. In the last weekly 
issue of the Islander ever published, occurs a two-column 
account of Atkyn’s career, which presents him as a cheap 
adventurer who sought, whether by fair means or foul, to 
sponge a living off the simple-minded Saints. Having run 
his course at the islands, and being hopelessly in debt, he

for it. 


Thomas Bedford had been a fisherman, who married a Mormon woman 
and joined the Church, but never became a “strong professor.” Mrs. Wing 
stated to the writer that he and David Brown were partners in the fishing 
industry. One day Brown came home and surprised Bedford and his wife 
in the act of adultery. For this, Brown and some others gave Bedford a 
whipping. Alexander Wentworth was a young man and something of a 
dandy. He married a daughter of Frank Johnson, partner of Dr. H. D. 
McCulloch. Thus Wentworth, aside from any independent grievance of 
his own against Strang, was closely connected with the firm which pro- 
vided the forefront of the opposition to the Prophet. Interview with Zenas 
Wright, May 25, 1920. 


THE DowNnFALL OF THE KiNGpom 169 


coolly proposed, unless they should pay his bills, to resort 
to a program of blackmail, and as evidence of his ability 
along this line, exhibited a specimen of lampooning hand- 
bill he had gotten out against certain reputable citizens of 
Council Bluffs. They did not comply, and Atkyn proceeded 
to execute his threat by visiting Detroit, Lansing, and other 
points, “peregrinating the country, endeavoring to slander 
the Mormons and sponge his living.” 

There were other malcontents, apparently, but these four 
men seem to have been the ringleaders in the enterprise to 
overthrow Strang. Never was an assassination plot gone 
about more publicly, and the failure of Strang to take 
effective measures to counteract it seems particularly strange 
in view of his resourceful character and his oft-repeated 
public defiance of his enemies. The last few issues of the 
Islander teem with notices of the quarrel and of some im- 
pending mischief. ‘“I'wo doctors* left here yesterday,” notes 
the official organ of May 22, “and today two or three 
ignorant persons say they are on an errand of mischief.” 
Although the [s/ander made light of this suggestion, ridi- 
culing the idea that a new persecution could be gotten up, 
the issue of four weeks later carried a seven and one-half 
column ‘‘Apology for the Mormons,” in which the Prophet 
bade defiance anew to all his enemies. 

We laugh in bitter scorn at all these threats [he concluded] and 
we tell these wolf hounds, marshal your myrmidons and send them 
along to make a spoil of beauty and booty as soon as you please. 
We bid them a bloody welcome to hospitable graves; over which, 
each year, we will pile stones, with a muttered curse, against the day 
of the resurrection of damnation. 

We will neither purchase temporary peace and future calamities 


by dishonorable trafficing with political jugglers, nor will we yield 
our homes to enemies. If we live, here will we live. If we die, here 


* The allusion is to McCulloch and Atkyn. 


170 Tue Kincpom oF St. JAMES 


will we die, and here shall our bones be buried, expecting in the 
resurrection of the just to possess the land forever, and dwell with 
the righteous during the lifetime of the eternal. 


These were brave words, but the sequel soon showed that 
the Mormons were not prepared to back them up with deeds. 
Doctor McCulloch, on his mission of stirring up vengeance, 
had succeeded in persuading the captain of the U.S. steamer 
Michigan to visit Saint James in furtherance of his plot.* 
Returning to Saint James on the Michigan, McCulloch 
made over his property to one of her officers, to prevent the 
Saints from enforcing their claims against him.f 


McCulloch brought home several pistols [records Warren Post, 
church historian and one of the twelve apostles] which he gave to 
his clique that they might become expert marksmen. It so happened 
“that after several days practice in shooting at targets the iron 
steamert came again into the Bay. Then was the time for their

hellish plot to be executed; the King must be slain, and some of

The Méchigan put in at the dock of Johnson and Mc- 
Culloch, lying directly in front of their store, and about 
seven o’clock in the evening one of the ship’s officers was 
sent to summon Strang to come on board as the captain 
wished to see him.* The Prophet accordingly started for the 
boat, ‘“‘somewhat cast down in mind in relation to what was 
transpiring around him.” As he stepped upon the bridge 
leading to the pier, Bedford and Wentworth emerged from 
the store, the former armed with a horse pistol and the latter 
with a revolver. Coming up behind the Prophet, Wentworth 
shot him in the head, bringing him to the ground. As he 
raised his head to see the assassins, who were but an arm’s 
length away, Wentworth fired again, shooting him in the 
face below the right eye. He rolled partly over, apparently 
stunned by the second shot, when Bedford discharged the 
horse pistol into his back. The murderer concluded his share 
of the assault by beating the helpless man over the head and 
face with the pistol. The two men then rushed on board 
the steamer and begged protection of her officers, which was 
readily granted. The assault took place in full view of 
several men at Johnson and McCulloch’s store, and of a 
number of the officers and crew on the boat, none of whom 
made any effort to interfere. Loyal Saints quickly gathered, 


and the city of Erie obtained permission from the government to preserve 
the vessel as a historic memorial. At last report, the Wolverine was lying 
in Erie harbor. For a comprehensive account of the vessel’s career, see “The 
Iron Ship,” by the present writer, in Burton Historical Collection Leaflet, 
VII, 17-32.

and carried the unconscious King to the house of the Prindle 
brothers, where the surgeon of the boat came and rendered 
him some medical attention. Meanwhile, some of the lead- 
ing Saints held a hasty conference, as a result of which 
Sheriff Miller that night sent a letter to Captain McBlair 
requesting him to join the group of Mormon leaders in the 
printing office to discuss the course to be pursued with refer- 
ence to Wentworth and Bedford. This the commander of 
the Michigan refused to do, saying the men were in safe 
custody and would be turned over by him to the civil au- 
thorities of Mackinac County. There was, of course, no legal 
justification for this, since the crime had been committed in 
the county seat of Emmet County, and the Sheriff of that 
county was the proper official to assume custody of the 
criminals. He had, however, neither the physical power nor 
the legal authority to compel the captain of the Michigan 
to surrender them.* About ten o’clock the following morn- 
ing the ship departed for Mackinac, having first taken on 
board all those who were supposed to be implicated in the 
plot against the King. At Mackinac, Wentworth and Bed- 
ford were turned over to Sheriff Granger, who kept them 
in prison for the space of ‘‘about five minutes,” after which 
they were set at liberty and treated as heroes by the towns- 
men. 

With the guiding hand of the Prophet removed, the 
Mormons at Beaver Island seemed to lose all capacity for 
intelligent or concerted action. Had Saint James contained 
a single leader possessed of the capacity of any one of half 
a dozen or more of those at Nauvoo after the killing of 
Joseph Smith, the day might still have been saved; but

and as often challenged by them, was at hand they cowered 
like a flock of terror-stricken sheep, submitting without 
show of opposition to the outrages which quickly followed.* 
About ten days after the shooting of Strang, the Mzchigan 
again made the harbor of Saint James, with McCulloch, 
Wentworth, Bedford, and others of their crowd on board. 
They immediately precipitated an uproar by undertaking 
to “arrest’’ such of the Mormons as were in sight, firing 
upon Samuel C. Field in this connection, and threatening 
to take the Prophet, dead or alive; but upon a considerable 
number of the Saints running together to learn what the 
confusion was about, the assailants desisted and the steamer 
set off for Mackinac. 

Fearing for the Prophet’s safety, after this visitation it 
was decided to remove him to Voree} and on June 28 he 
left Saint James on board the propeller Lowisvélle. Two 
days later a number of families departed for Voree and other 
points on board the Iowa. On July 3 came another visitation 
from Mackinac, led this time by the Sheriff of that county, 
intent on arresting some of the Mormon leaders; in this he 
failed, however, for, “directed by the spirit,” they had left 
the island “without the help of mobs.” Notice was now 
given the Saints by the mob that they must leave the island 
forthwith. There ensued, during the next two or three days, 
a scene of despoliation and forced deportation such as the 


* Zenas H. Wright, son of Phineas Wright, one of the twelve apostles, 
was a youth of seventeen at the time of the mobbing. In an interview with 
the writer (May 25, 1920) he explained the submissiveness of the Mormons 
to the mobbers as due to the fact that the principal leaders of the former had 
all fled the islands in the interval between the shooting of Strang and 
the visitation of the mob. However correct this explanation may be, it 
does not suffice to obviate the writer’s criticism of the quality of leadership 
displayed at the Beaver Islands. At Nauvoo when the Smiths were killed and 
the city threatened with destruction Young and the other apostles made 
haste to return to the post of danger and responsibility. 

+ Information on these points is drawn chiefly from Warren Post’s

174 Tue Kinepom oF St. JAMES 


world became, unhappily, all too familiar with in the course 
of the recent World War. Bands of swearing, drunken 
ruffians went about the island commanding the Mormons 
to assemble at the harbor for immediate departure, on peril 
of having their homes burned over their heads. There they 
were crowded on board steamers, as many as the vessels 
could carry, and started into exile; up the Lake or down the 
Lake, it was all the same to the mobbers, who, even before 
the Saints had left the harbor began the appropriation of 
their property. 

Almost exactly half a century [writes a modern historian of 
Michigan]* has gone by since that July day—just after the “glori- 
ous Fourth’—when helpless women and innocent children were 


driven into exile, and it still remains—and we trust it may always 
remain—the most disgraceful day in Michigan history. 


Precise figures as to the number of American citizens who 
were thus driven into exile are lacking, but it is not difficult 
to make an approximate estimate. A census taken in the 
autumn of 1854 disclosed 2,608 residents on Big Beaver 
Island.f There was undoubtedly a considerable increase 
during the year 1855, and the gentile population of the 
island was but a handful. If it be assumed that the latter 
equaled the increase in population between the autumn of 
1854 and the spring of 1856, we arrive at the conclusion 
that approximately 2,600 people were driven from the 
island following the shooting of Strang. Confidence that 
this figure does not err through overstatement is strength- 
ened by the record in a contemporary letter that 490 were 


* Byron M. Cutcheon, Michigan as a Province, Territory, and State, 
III, 308-309. 

+ Northern Islander, November 2, 1854. The population today is but a 
fraction of what it was before the Mormon dispersion. The census of 1920 
showed a total of 779 residents in the two townships into which the island 
is divided.

taken to Chicago on a single boat ;* 300 more had previously 
departed on the Iowa, while the Lowisvélle carried “all she 
could stow” to Detroit, one of the wives of Strang being 
included in this shipload. 

For the most part the anguish and hardships endured by 
these exiles will forever go unrecorded. But the imagination 
will be assisted in grasping the real significance of the gen- 
eral statements we have made, by the recital of one con- 
crete illustration. Wingfield Watson had come to Beaver 
Island, from southern Wisconsin, a young married man, in 
the summer of 1852. The King assigned him an “inherit- 
ance” about midway of the island, in the midst of the un- 
tamed wilderness, several miles from Saint James. Here a 
cabin was built, a well dug, and fruit trees set out; in short, 
with no accumulated capital, by the hardest of manual labor 
during the ensuing four years the foundations of a modest 
homestead were reared. While going from his home to Saint 
James at the time of the mobbing, Watson met two men 
armed with guns and knives.t They asked where he lived, 
and he answered, about midway of the island. They re- 
sponded with curses, ordering him to get his things down to 
the harbor the following afternoon or his house would be 
burned over his head. It chanced that Mrs. Watson had just 
given birth to a child and was in no condition to be moved. 
The husband represented this situation, and begged that he 
be permitted to wait until she was in suitable condition to 
travel, but this plea, which might well have moved the heart 
of a savage, succeeded only in provoking a fresh outburst of 
cursing. The next day the husband set forth on foot along 
the King’s Highway, which was but a semblance of a road,

winding in and out through the stumps of the cedar swamp, 
while after him staggered the invalid wife. Arrived at the 
harbor, this scene confronted them: 


Whole groups of Mormons, with their cattle and other moveables 
might be seen here and there along the beach of the harbor, waiting 
to be taken off on the first boat in. Soon after a big steamer, the 
Keystone State, arrived and all was hurry and bustle among the 
mob. They rushed the Mormons aboard, men, women, and children, 
with oaths and curses. “ you, why don’t you go faster?” 
This last was addressed to my own wife, who had not yet been quite 
three days confined of our second eldest child, and was scarcely 
able to walk, while I led the way with the babe in my arms. As 
many as could be huddled together on that boat were rushed aboard ; 
but when they looked around to see what was going to be done with 
their cattle and things, they were casting off the lines and putting 
out. 


Arriving at Chicago, this mass of destitute humanity was 
unloaded on the dock and left to its fate. With little or no 
means, dazed and discouraged by the treatment they had 
undergone, adrift in a strange city, men, women, and chil- 
dren sat in the broiling rays of the July sun for nearly a 
day; the only touch of kindness remembered during all this 
experience was the act of a profane warehouse keeper, who, 
seeing the desolate throng, 


set of villains that had robbed us and sent us 
there; and like a man of some patriotism and feeling threw open 
the warehouse door saying “here ladies and gentlemen, get in here 
out of the sun and sit down till you can get places to go to.” 


cursed the 


If a profane warehouseman was thus moved to pity by 
the chance sight of the victims of the mob, we need not 
wonder that God looked down from heaven in displeasure 
at the treatment accorded His chosen people. In times of 
great public stress people are wont to believe they witness 
miraculous signs of His intervention. Thus, at the beginning 


THE DownFALL OF THE KINGDOM 177 


of the World War an angelic presence came to the English 
army retreating from Mons, to protect it from the pursuing 
Germans. At Beaver Island one of the Mormon women 
reported that shortly before sunset on the last night of her 
stay one of her boys came running in and begged her to 
come to the door and look at an object in the heavens. Com- 
plying, she saw over the island to the west a spindle-shaped 
body of a beautiful golden color. After a time it began to 
separate, forming the letters Z I O N of the same beautiful 
color. Again the picture changed, forming this time the 
letters J S. Many other persons witnessed the spectacle and 
it was a common topic of conversation next day on the boat 
with passengers and crew.* 

From the gentile point of view, the Saints had received 
less than they deserved, and their expulsion was regarded 
as an act of tardy and inadequate justice. As in wars of 
greater fame, so in this one, clashing economic interests 
combined with differences of religion and with simple igno- 
rance to create in the adherents of each party to the conflict 
an exaggerated conception of their opponents, and the con- 
viction that they themselves were enlisted in a righteous 
cause. From the circumstance that the Saints were scattered 
far and wide, while their gentile conquerors remained in 
possession of the field to pass their version of the conflict 
down to their descendants, it has come to pass that in all 
the adjoining region a body of legend which currently passes 
as unquestioned history has developed, showing the Mor- 
mons as piratical criminals and scoundrels, and their gentile 
opponents as virtuous upholders of law and order.f One of

brethren in a quiet way” that he thought they would have 
to draw off before their enemies.* This advice seems not to 
have been communicated to them, however, and while they 
vainly awaited some word of guidance from their chief the 
mob descended upon them. Arrived at Voree, Strang was 
carried to the home of his parents and there tenderly cared 
for by a few of his loyal followers until the morning of July 
9, when his career on earth came to its end. Present at the 
deathbed were two of his polygamous wives,t Apostles 
Hickey and Chidester, and Sheriff George Miller. The 
Prophet was borne to the grave attended by a handful of 
Saints and a “respectable congregation” of gentiles. The 
burial was in the Voree cemetery, but the Saints’ title to this 
land was not yet perfected, and the plot of land including 
the grave of the King was later secured by a neighboring 
farmer and converted into a cowyard. At a later time a 
daughter of Strang caused his body to be removed to the 
Burlington cemetery, where it rests today in an unmarked 
grave, its surface as flat as the surrounding prairie, and its 
location known to but a handful of individuals. S72¢ transét 
gloria mundi. 

With Strang’s death died his Church, and for this fact the 
Prophet himself is chiefly accountable. His claim to the 
prophetic office was based entirely upon the propositions 
that God had called Joseph Smith to be his representative 
upon earth in initiating the “dispensation of the fullness of 
times”; that Joseph was divinely authorized to name his 
successor in the prophetic office; and that before his death, 
by divine guidance he made choice of James J. Strang. On

this foundation Strang built his Church, and through a dozen 
years of reiterated argument he had impressed his followers 
with the conviction that without a head the Church was 
unable to function, and that only through him could such 
a head be named. The taking off of Smith was so sudden 
that there would have been no cause for remark had he 
failed to name his successor. Strang lived more than three 
weeks after he was shot, during most of this time in entire 
possession of his faculties, and enjoyed the fullest oppor- 
tunity to make provision against his impending demise. 
Yet he steadfastly refused to make any, merely advising 
his followers to look after their families as best they could 
until they should find out what to do.* Two days before 
death he said the active part of his life was at an end and 
the “bearing off” of the Kingdom must now devolve upon 
others.f The next day he began visibly to fail and Edward 
Chidester, one of the apostles, asked if the Church were not 
going to have a successor appointed through him, but he 
merely answered, “I do not want to talk about it.’’ Within 
less than twenty-four hours’ time he was dead, having wil- 
fully entailed upon his exiled flock the added burden of not 
knowing where to turn for guidance or leadership. 

Under these circumstances the ecclesiastical organization 
disintegrated, more slowly but scarcely less surely than had 
the material settlement of Saint James before the onslaught 
of the gentile mob. Many went over to the Reorganized 
Church, the headship of which was at length assumed by 
young Joseph Smith in 1860. Some became Brighamites;

us; You have it second hand, I had it from his own mouth.” Letter of

THe DowNFALL OF THE KINGDOM 181 


while the great majority, probably, abandoned, sooner or 
later, all pretensions to Mormonism. 

Several feeble efforts were made to reconstruct the church 
organization, but all were brought to naught by the immov- 
able obstacle that nothing could be done without a Prophet, 
and Strang had died leaving no word as to his successor. On 
February 10, 1857, five of the apostles assembled “‘in the 
wilderness” six miles from Racine and spent the entire 
winter night praying for direction, with the result that they 
became satisfied that the Twelve could not lead the church without 
a Prophet; and concluded to take care of ourselves and families; 
and when occasion offered, minister to the necessities of the Saints, 


according to our abilities, until we have the word of the Lord to 
guide us to other duties.* 


In December, 1863, 112 followers of Strang assembled 
in conference at Hixton, Wisconsin, agreed to make an 
overture to young Joseph Smith to accept their headship on 
the strength of his having been called thereto by ‘“‘the 
Prophet James J. Strang.”’ This was obviously a counsel of 
despair, not justified by the facts, and now as always during 
his long career Smith spurned all overtures based on a 
recognition of Strang’s pretensions. About the year 1882 
some effort was made to induce Charles J. Strang, eldest 
son of Elvira Field, to put himself forward as successor to 
his father, but this project was negatived by his emphatic 
refusal to lend himself to it. 

The last surviving apostle, Lorenzo D. Hickey, died at 
Coldwater in the year 1900. A week before his demise he 
urged Wingfield Watson to pay him a visit and, the latter 
complying, ordained him to the office of presiding high 
priest. Watson was converted to the cause in young man- 


* Warren Post, Apostolic Testimony, p. 27. 
+ Letter of Watson to the writer, April 14, 1919. 


182 Tue KINGDOM OF ST. JAMES 


hood in 1852, and for over seventy years, until his death in 
October, 1923, with unwavering steadfastness continued an 
ardent champion of the prophetic pretensions of Strang. 
After the death of Hickey he struggled as best he might to 
play the role of leader to the handful who still persisted 
in the faith. He gathered a number of the church records 
for preservation, and gained control of a portion of the 
original site of Voree, holding it in trust for the Church. In 
October, 1919, a conference was held at Kansas City (the 
first in twenty years) and a tiny monthly paper was estab- 
lished at that place to serve as the organ of the Church.* 
Although it has since been suspended, the effort to maintain 
the church organization is still continued. Mr. Watson, 
before his death in 1923, ordained Samuel H. Martin of 
Pueblo, Colorado, as presiding elder to succeed him as head 
of the Church, and in July, 1928, some forty adherents 
attended a conference convened on the site of deserted 
Voree. The little band of zealots still eagerly awaits the 
fulfilment of the revelation vouchsafed to Joseph Smith 
in September, 1832, and confirmed by Strang, to the effect 
that before all who were then living shall have passed away, 
the erring Saints will be brought back from Utah and else- 
where to the gathering places divinely appointed, while at 
Independence, Missouri, the city of New Jerusalem will be 
established and a temple reared to God “‘in this generation.” 

To the simple historian it seems clear that whatever the 
future may hold in store for Mormonism, the Strangite 
Church is a thing of the past. The man who brought it into 
being is the man who sealed its doom. For it was not the 
act of the gentile mob that broke up the Church—the Saints 
had triumphed over far greater persecutions in Missouri and 
Illinois—but the deliberate refusal of Strang to name his 


* The paper was called The Latter Day Precept, edited by John Flanders. 


THE DownFALL OF THE Kincpom 183 


successor. He had built up an organization which he, better 
than any other living man, knew could not persist after his 
death without a duly ordained successor. When the time 
arrived for naming one, he refused, like England’s Virgin 
Queen, even to consider the question. Thereby he repaid 
those who had forsaken homes and friends—oftentimes even 
wives and children—to follow after him by destroying their 
dearest interest in life, their Church, which he had made 
their all. Little wonder that most of them sooner or later, 
including even the women who had become his wives,* lost 
faith in the religious pretensions of the man they had fol- 
lowed so blindly. That a few were found whose faith has 
outlived the buffetings of three-quarters of a century con- 
stitutes a striking illustration of the constancy of which 
mankind is capable. 


* Elvira Field had, in later life, no church connection, although she 
expressed an interest in Christian work. Betsy McNutt became a Reor- 
ganized Saint, but before her death abandoned this for another faction of 
Mormonism. Phoebe Wright, together with all her father’s family, was 
completely disillusioned by the Prophet’s downfall, and in later life had 
nothing to do with Mormonism. Sarah Wright told the writer that she 
concluded “that my husband was like some other men who sought to build 
up a kingdom in the midst of a republic,’ and fearful that her son (born 
after Strang’s death) might some day try to follow in his father’s footsteps 
she made every effort to keep from him all knowledge of Strang’s career, 
and until he was almost grown he did not even know who his father was.
