Sources of Information 


that he who would study the history of the Kingdom of Saint 

James must pursue his search for information along bypaths 
far removed from the usual highways of American literature. It seems 
desirable, therefore, by way of rounding out the study we have made, 
to submit a short account of the more important sources, printed, 
manuscript, and oral, from which the narrative has been derived. 

The best general account of Mormonism as a whole is William A. 
Linn’s The Story of the Mormons from the Date of Their Origin to 
the Year rgor (New York, 1902). It contains, however, almost 
nothing concerning the Strangite phase of Mormonism, probably for 
the reason that the author found but little upon it and failed, there- 
fore, to appreciate its significance. 

The spokesmen of Mormonism, like the Puritan founders of 
Massachusetts, have been acutely aware of the importance of their 
doings, and they have produced in print an ever mounting flood of 
literature concerning them. With a zeal which might well be com- 
mended to the older and more orthodox religious sects, the Church 
of Latter Day Saints has maintained from its infancy an official 
press and an official church historian to insure the due cultivation of 
its historical garden. Naturally, this output of literature is partisan 
and propagandist in character, as is, indeed, practically everything 
written about the doings of the Mormons. For the Reorganized 
Church of Latter Day Saints there is an official history in three 
volumes by President Joseph Smith and Apostle Heman C. Smith, 
published by the Board of Publication of the Reorganized Church 
of Jesus Christ of Latter Day Saints (Lamoni, Iowa, 1900-1903), 
entitled Hzstory of the Church of Jesus Christ of Latter Day Saints, 
805-1890. Since the Reorganized Church chiefly sprang from the 
ruins of the Strangite organization, the official history contains 
considerable incidental information about the antecedent faction. 


|: will be evident from the footnotes of the preceding narrative 


APPENDICES 185 


For anything approaching a complete record of Strangite Mor- 
monism, however, one must turn to the literary output of this faction 
of the Church of Latter Day Saints. Unfortunately, due to the 
circumstances recounted in our narrative, existing examples of this 
output are so obscure and so rare that only by much painstaking 
search can one gain access to even a respectable portion of the whole. 
Probably the present writer has pursued this search more diligently 
than anyone else, and a réswmé of the results achieved will constitute 
an important part of our discussion. 

Strang had been an editor before he became a Mormon. He was 
keenly aware of the value of publicity, and he constantly strove, both 
by the agency of oral discussion and the use of the printed page, to 
place his cause before the public in a favorable light. In the infancy 
of his movement at Voree he proceeded to establish an official organ 
to which the name Voree Herald was given. Issued monthly at first 
(with some irregularity) and later as a weekly, it ran from January, 
1846, until the spring of 1850. The name of the paper was changed 
once or twice, and there was a succession of nominal editors. The real 
editor throughout the entire period of its existence was Strang him- 
self. He wrote much of the contents of the Herald, and it is probably 
safe to say that not a line was ever published in it which did not have 
his tacit, if not active, approval. During most of the period of its 
existence the Saints were beset by poverty, often by dire poverty; 
this fact suffices to explain the irregularity of appearance to which 
the Herald was subject.* There is no complete file of it in existence 
at the present time. The State Historical Society of Wisconsin has a 
fairly comprehensive file, and a considerable representation is pre- 
served in the New York Public Library. 

The successor to the Voree Herald was the Northern Islander, 
established by Strang at Saint James in the early winter of 1850-51.7 
Like the Voree Herald in its latter days, the Northern Islander 
was issued as a weekly paper; like its predecessor, too, its appear-

hensive account of his publications to that date. The early issues of the 
Voree Herald were printed at different places—at Voree, Elkhorn, and 
Southport, Wisconsin, at Ottawa, Illinois, and. at New York City. In all, 
from Jaunary, 1846 to June 6, 1850, one hundred eighty numbers were 
issued. 

+The last number of the Voree Herald was issued June 6, 1850; the 
first number of the Northern Islander was issued on December 12, following.

ance was highly irregular. Particularly was publication likely to be 
suspended during the long winter season, when Beaver Island was 
isolated from the outside world. In all, ninety issues of the weekly 
were published in the period from December, 1850, to June, 1856.* 
On April 1, 1856, publication of a daily edition was begun. The 
announcement concerning it stated that it would be issued six times 
a week from the opening to the close of navigation, and once a week 
while navigation was closed, for the price of six dollars per year. 
The subscription for the navigation season only was to be two 
dollars. It seems improbable that Strang really expected this venture 
to pay financially, or that many paying subscriptions would be re- 
ceived. Thirty-three issues of the daily had been published when 
Strang’s assassination brought Kingdom and paper alike to a sudden 
end. The Northern Islander was the first newspaper published in 
northern Michigan. Everything considered, it was a surprisingly 
good paper. For any history of the Strangite Kingdom it is indis- 
pensable, but no complete file of it remains in existence and the only 
considerable run of which the writer has any knowledge is held in 
private ownership and has never been accessible to the public. The 
owner, prior to his death in 1923, was Wingfield Watson, and by 
him the writer was accorded every opportunity to consult the file, 
including the loan of it for several months.+ 

Both Voree Herald and Northern Islander were, of course, per- 
sonal organs of the Prophet, conceived primarily for the promotion 
of his designs. In the Voree Herald much space is devoted to fac- 
tional disputes between the warring Saints, and practically none to 
disputes between Saints and gentiles. The Northern Islander, on the 
contrary, is crowded with reports of gentile persecution, accompanied

APPENDICES 187 


by mouthings of defiance and protestations of Mormon innocence of 
wrongdoing. Both Voree Herald and Northern Islander contain nu- 
merous official documents, such as revelations, translations of holy 
records, and reports of church conferences. Certain of these were 
also printed in pamphlet form. The last issue of the Daily Northern 
Islander (June 20, 1856) carried an advertisement of certain of 
these publications. The items listed included The Prophetich Discus- 
sion (canvassing the claims of Strang and Brigham Young), 44 pp. 
quarto, twenty cents; The New Dispensation, by Oliver Cowdery 
(pages not stated), quarto, twenty cents; and The Diamond, 20 pp., 
octavo, five cents. The advertisement also announced that in May 
would be published (evidently the advertisement had been running 
for some time) Sacred Hymns, which was to be a small volume of 
172 pages, bound in fine morocco, to sell for fifty cents; and The 
Book of the Law of the Lord, 350 pp., octavo, bound in calf, to sell 
for two dollars. 

Other than this advertisement, the writer has found no clue to 
the book of hymns, and it seems reasonable to suppose that it still 
remained unpublished at the time of the dispersion of the Saints. 
The story of the Book of the Law of the Lord is deserving of 
separate recital. 

If Strang’s pretensions be valid this should be the most interesting 
book ever published in America, if not, indeed, in the world. Other 
religions have their sacred writings, and the Saints themselves had 
the Book of Mormon. But these are mere chronicles of past events: 
the Book of Mormon, for example, is the history of an ancient race; 
the Bible, in addition to this, is a literary miscellany, the product 
of many writers over a long period of time. But the Book of the 
Law of the Lord is God’s own framework of government for his 
people on earth in the present and all future time. From the point 
of view of our narrative, it was the capstone of Strang’s prophetic 
career, the climax toward which all his labors tended. In theory, if 
not in actual fact, it made him supreme ruler over all the earth; 
ironically enough, his Kingdom had vanished into thin air before 
the completion of publication of the book on which it was based. 

From the beginning Strang had encouraged his followers with 
the promise of holy records, which through divine agency he would 
disclose to them. A foretaste of these was furnished in the Voree 


188 Tue Kincpom oF St. JAMES 


plates, produced from beneath the roots of the oak tree in September, 
1845. Precisely when the Law of the Lord took shape in Strang’s 
mind we are unable to say. Mormonism owes its origin to a sacred 
record, translated by reputedly divine inspiration, and it was only 
logical that Strang, as Smith’s successor, should encourage his fol- 
lowers to expect a continuance of such divine favors through his 
agency. At Voree, as we have seen, God commanded them to build 
him a house, wherein he might carry on his work of translating. 
Despite the backwardness of the Saints the translation evidently 
went on, for in April, 1849, we find Alden Hale writing to the 
Prophet concerning plans and procedure for publishing the “new 
translations.”* The letter discusses the procuring of paper, type, 
and implements for binding the publication, and it affords the first 
tangible clue that has been found concerning it. Some time later 
the first edition of the Book of the Law of the Lord was issued, a 
volume of 80 pages. No copy of this edition is known to be in exist- 
ence, although Wingfield Watson informed the writer that he 
had owned one which some time before the conversation he had 
loaned to a fellow Saint in Tennessee, by whom it had not been 
returned. 

In the absence of any copy it is impossible to say how this first 
edition of the Law of the Lord differed from its successor.f In one 
of the earliest issues of the Northern Islander (February 6, 1851), 
Strang printed the ten commandments (later incorporated in the 
Law of the Lord as Chapter I), and stated by way of introduction 
that they had been newly translated “from the records which Lehi 
brought from Jerusalem.” Three months later he displayed these 
“records” to certain of the faithful in attendance upon the April 
conference at Saint James. Warren Post, one of the Saints thus 
favored, states that eighteen brass plates, nine inches long and seven 
and three-eighths inches wide, were shown to him and several others. 
The witnesses “handled them and saw the engravings thereon, and 
were satisfied that they were of ancient workmanship; and the real

The work of translation must have been often interrupted, for 
not until January 24, 1856, was publication of the “records” begun 
in the Northern Islander. This time the laws proper were accom- 
panied by an extensive annotated comment, supplied by the Prophet. 
Elaborate pains had been taken to render the printing of the “trans- 
lation” accurate, and at the close of each section, or paragraph, a 
statement of the number of words and letters contained in it was 
printed. The type of the instalments printed in the Islander was 
saved and used in printing the book, 320 copies of which were struck 
off. While they still lay unbound in the printing office at Saint 
James, the mob descended upon the place and made spoil of every- 
thing the Saints possessed, and the library and royal press of King 
Strang suffered the treatment meted out by the Moslems of old to 
the library of Alexandria. 

But if the fleeing Saints were bereft of all other earthly posses- 
sions, they took particular pains to preserve the Law of the Lord 
from destruction. The unbound sheets of the volume were boxed and 
shipped to Racine, where Benjamin Wright, an apostle in the Church 
and a father-in-law of the Prophet took charge of them.t From this 
time on the fate of the volumes becomes progressively more obscure. 
In December, 1863, the followers of Strang held a church conference 
at Hixton, Wisconsin, where, among other business, they discussed 
having “100 or more” copies of the Law of the Lord bound for better 
preservation. Evidently a large part, if not all, of the edition as 
printed was still preserved intact. Their further disposition may be 
surmised from the following facts which the writer has assembled: 
on January 24, 1893, Wingfield Watson quoted Dr. R. G. Thwaites, 
Secretary of the State Historical Society of Wisconsin, a price of 
two dollars for bound, or one dollar and fifty cents for unbound 
copies of the book, which implies that at this time he must have had 
a number of copies on hand. On January 19, 1920, Rev. A. N. 
Somers of Westboro, Mass., informed the writer that about the 
year 1890 (when he was preaching at Black River Falls, Wis.) 


* Warren Post’s Personal History (MS), p. 10, and his Apostolic Testi- 
mony (MS), p. 58. 

+ Letter of Edward Chidester to Warren Post, August 3, 1856, copied in 
the latter’s Apostolic Testimony, pp. 15-19. 


190 Tue Kincpom oF St. JAMES 


Strang’s daughter, the “Princess Eugenia,” had about twenty-five 
copies of the book, two of which she gave to him, and one of which 
he in turn gave to the State Historical Society. Charles J. Strang 
likewise had a number of copies about the same time, and these he 
had bound, supplying to them a one-page explanatory note of his own, 
and a title page and a preface, the latter taken from the first edition 
of the Law of the Lord. Finally, the writer, about half a dozen years 
since, found a former follower of Strang living in central Wisconsin 
who had in his possession the unbound sheets of upward of thirty 
copies of the book. They had been kept in his attic in cardboard 
shoe boxes over a period of years, during which the mice had dis- 
covered them to be a convenient nesting place. From the remains as 
found, the writer was able to assemble eight or nine practically 
perfect copies, a couple of which are still in his possession. The 
others have gone into the hands of collectors. 

But one remaining publication of Strang calls for present atten- 
tion. Fond of debate and a skilled controversialist, he frequently 
supplied contributions to gentile publications, sometimes over his 
signature, and at other times with his authorship concealed. From 
January to April, 1854, he published in the Northern Islander a 
series of articles of a descriptive and historical character, which 
later in the same year he issued as a paper-covered pamphlet of 
forty-eight pages, with the formidable title Ancient and Modern 
Michilimackinac, Including an Account of the Controversy between 
Mackinac and the Mormons. The reader quickly perceives that the 
real purpose of this publication is that suggested by the subtitle. 
The story of the controversy between the Saints and their enemies is 
told, of course, from the Mormon point of view. Its character as 
truthful history has been angrily denounced by various gentile 
writers. Undoubtedly Strang was a writer not without guile, able on 
occasion to temper his narration to Saintly needs and sensibilities; 
but as between it and the usual gentile compound of hatred, fiction, 
and fable, there can be no doubt that Strang’s narrative is much 
the saner and more reliable. Ancient and Modern Michilimackinac 
has been reprinted at least twice; by the St. Ignace News and Free 
Press in 1885, and by Wingfield Watson in 1894. 

The story of Strang’s Kingdom affords a fertile field for the 
feature writer and the fictionist, and a considerable body of litera- 


APPENDICES 191 


ture of this kind has been based upon it. Whatever entertainment 
the reader may derive from these writings, viewed as sober historical 
contributions they are practically worthless. Commonly they reflect, 
with more or less fidelity, the compound of legend and prejudice 
which in the region adjacent to the northern end of Lake Michigan 
and in certain sections of southern Wisconsin still passes current as 
veritable history. The character of this prejudice must be understood 
if one is to comprehend the real character of the narratives based upon 
it. The feature writer and the fictionist are interested in producing 
a good story rather than in ascertaining the actual truth about an 
obscure episode of the past. They willingly reproduce the tales about 
the Saints that they find in circulation, and by dint of repetition and 
widespread publication in the metropolitan press a legend has gained 
current acceptance in the Great Lakes region which bears but faint 
resemblance to historical truth.* 

One narrative of this type merits notice here, not only because 
it illustrates perfectly the gentile legend in question, but also because 
it has itself become a chief quarry of material for further sensational 
narratives. Mrs. Elizabeth Whitney Williams was born on Mackinac 
Island, apparently about the year 1845. Her father was a boat 
builder who moved from place to place as circumstances might dictate, 
and all of the family affiliations were with the gentile fisherman 
element of Mackinac and vicinity. Mrs. Williams grew up under the 
influence of this environment; she married the keeper of the light- 


* The attitude here noted is effectively characterized by Henry E. Legler, 
whose investigations were conducted in the middle nineties. In Walworth 
County, Wisconsin, the Reorganized Saints have (or then had) a humble 
rural church. Legler reports that when he visited the community he found 
the gentiles deeply prejudiced against their Mormon neighbors; they related 
“with bated breath” stories of witchcraft on the part of the Mormon elder. 
“The narrators evidently believed the stories implicitly, the grotesqueness 
and impossibility of the performances alleged to have occurred scarcely 
paralleling in extent the credulity of the country folks.” 

An amusing manifestation of this local prejudice against the Saints at 
the time of Legler’s visit to the neighborhood is related. In the village of 
Springfield lived a few Mormons who were in the habit of holding meetings 
in the schoolhouse, and the ancient rural custom was followed of ringing 
the school bell to give notice of the meeting. Yielding to gentile pressure, 
the school trustees had the bell removed. The Mormons thereupon retorted 
by placarding the village with notices of their meeting on which the words 
“Curfew shall not ring tonight” were prominently displayed. Parkman Club 
Publications, Nos. 15-16 (Milwaukee, 1897), pp. 1-2.

house at Saint James, and after his death by drowning, succeeded 
him in the service. Her parents lived at Saint James for a short 
period during her early childhood, and were residing in the Pine 
River settlement when the affray with the Mormons occurred in the 
summer of 1853. In this fight her father and her elder half brother 
participated, and the latter, at least, was an actor in the mobbing of 
the Mormons after the shooting of Strang in 1856. In 1905, when 
Mrs. Williams was about sixty years of age, she published at Harbor 
Springs a semi-autobiographical narrative of 229 pages entitled 4 
Child of the Sea; and Life Among the Mormons. Adequately to 
characterize this volume in brief compass is difficult. That it should 
reflect the gentile viewpoint toward the Mormons is but a matter of 
course; but that, purporting to be a narrative based on personal 
knowledge and experience it should be compounded largely of fancy, 
fiction, and misinformation, is not. Despite the assurance and pre- 
tended first-hand authority with which it speaks, the present writer 
has derived no help whatever from Mrs. Williams’ narrative. Save 
as it reflects the existing body of gentile myth and tradition con- 
cerning the Mormons, it is without historical value. 

But one writer, hitherto, has made a serious effort to produce a 
comprehensive and unbiased account of the Strangite movement. 
Henry E. Legler, in his later years librarian of the Chicago Public 
Library, was greatly interested in the study of local history. He was 
long a resident of Milwaukee, and a member of a small group of 
Milwaukeeans of like tastes who associated themselves under the 
name of The Parkman Club. The members prosecuted a number of 
local historical studies which were subsequently published as the 
Parkman Club Publications. Mr. Legler’s contribution to the series 
was a study of the Strangite movement, published in 1897 as Nos. 15 
and 16 of the series under the title, “A Moses of the Mormons.” Mr. 
Legler enjoyed a rare opportunity to make an exhaustive investiga- 
tion of his subject, since many of the actors in the story were still 
alive and accessible for interviews. However, he contented himself 
with a comparatively slight study of 80 pages, only half of which is 
the author’s own contribution. Slight as it is, Mr. Legler’s investiga- 
tion still remains without a competitor, the only one ever made of 
Strang’s prophetic career. It has been several times reprinted, and 


APPENDICES 193 


is frequently utilized by the newspaper feature writers, as the quarry 
from which their tales are derived. 

When the present writer turned his attention to the Strangite 
movement, about a decade ago, the opportunity to derive information 
first-hand from those who had participated in it had largely passed. 
Some, however, were able to afford important assistance, without 
which the present study could not have been produced. Chief among 
these was Wingfield Watson, who accepted Strang’s leadership in 
1852 and for seventy years thereafter, despite almost every dis- 
couragement that can be imagined, remained staunchly loyal to the 
Prophet’s claims and teachings. From the death of L. D. Hickey in 
1900 until his own demise in 1923, Mr. Watson strove to safeguard 
the interests of the sadly diminished Church. He accorded the writer 
frequent interviews, and patiently answered the many questions 
which were propounded to him. He likewise accorded the freest pos- 
sible access to his collection of church records. In the summer of 
1919 he accompanied the writer on a journey to Beaver Island, his 
first visit since he had been despoiled of his property and driven 
into exile sixty-three years before. Mr. Watson was a gentleman in 
the finest sense, a sincere and kindly man, who devoted his all in 
life to the pursuit of a vain and impossible ideal. The writer holds 
him in deepest respect, and gratefully records this tribute to his 
memory. 

Aside from Mr. Watson, a son, a daughter, and a widow of the 
Prophet have afforded much information and assistance to the author. 
Charles J. Strang, eldest son of the Prophet and Elvira Field, pro- 
cured, during his active life, as many of his father’s papers as he 
could obtain, with the intention, apparently, of writing a narrative 
of his career. When he died, the papers were given to his brother, 
Clement, who likewise entertained the design of publishing an ac- 
count of his father’s life. When the writer established contact with 
him and disclosed to him his own project already far advanced, 
Mr. Strang cheerfully turned the manuscripts over to him and 
resigned his own design of utilizing them as the basis of a narrative. 
It should be stated in this connection that neither of the brothers 
adhered to their father’s religion, or desired any publicity for them- 
selves. They were animated, rather, by the laudable motive of 
defending his memory against prejudiced and untrue attacks. The 


194 THE Kincpom oF St. JAMES 


importance of these manuscripts to the present study will be obvious 
to everyone who reads it. 

Mrs. Sarah Wing, the last surviving wife of Strang, answered 
numerous inquiries which the writer propounded by letter over a 
period of two or three years; when it was finally possible to pay her 
a visit, however, advancing infirmity had rendered her memory so 
unstable that but little further information could be derived from 
her. Eugenia, the daughter of Strang and Phoebe Wright, and 
Zenas Wright, brother of Sarah and cousin of Phoebe Wright, have 
also favored the writer with important information. Roscoe B. Mar- 
tin of Forestville, New York, and Charles M. Reed of Sinclairville, 
both former presidents of the Chautauqua County Historical Society, 
have supplied the portrait and the life mask of Strang. The portrait 
is from a daguerreotype owned by Mrs. Eva Allen, a grand niece 
of the Prophet; the life mask, now in possession of the Chautauqua 
County Historical Society, was until recently owned by J. S. Fowler 
of Jamestown, a grand nephew. 

It is perhaps unnecessary to add that no one of the many persons 
from whom information or other assistance has been derived is in 
any way responsible for the conclusions reached and the opinions 
ventured by the writer. 


II 


The Diary of James J. Strang* 


AY 29th [1831] Today I read seven hours in Volney’s 
Ruins reading more than 360 pages. It is an admirable 
work and well executed. 


July 4th Celebrated the anniversary of our national Independence 
at Goves. was present: but [arrived] after the oration was delivered. 
it was said to be smart but badly given. toasts were decent some very 
good. Mr. Corey, marshal. Dr. Crum president. Dr. Brown speaker. 


31st Last night had the heaviest thunder and rain we have had 
in some time, perhaps four or five years. the cre[e]ks are very high 
overflowing several acres of land: some damage done. 

Our debating school was given up as soon as it went into operation 
to prevent contention with the prejudiced bigots. 

The revival meeting lasted a fortnight or more. Some dozen or 
twenty were converted, principally small boys and very young ladies. 
Nearly half have allready returned to their wallowing again. 

Unkle S. H. Dillingham visited us lately: we were able to shew 
him the largest corn in the country though it was planted in June. 


Aug. 28th I have been making records of our debating society. 
Since it was reestablished it has been prosperous. We have had 
several sharp debates lately, one last evening. The state of Europe 
is at this time truly precarious. Poland in a dreadful contest for 
independence. England all in ferment about reform. Belgium in a 
struggle with foreign ministers and even members of her own 
congress whether to deliver hers to the (un) holy alliance or not. 


* The manuscript notebook in which the diary was kept has suffered 
much from misuse, and considerable portions are missing. The greater 
portion of the volume was owned a few years since by Henry Denio of 
Lamoni, Iowa, a grandson of Strang, to whom it had come, apparently, from 
his grandmother, Betsy McNutt. By Mr. Denio the manuscript was loaned 
to me for the purpose of copying. At a later time, on gaining access to the 
Strang manuscripts, about a dozen sheets of the original diary were found 
therein, and the contents of these have been fitted into their appropriate 
places in the present copy. 


196 Tue Krincpom oF St. JAMES 


Oct. 9th Our debating school is very prosperous though we have 
but a few speakers. Five is the extent. But we expect more soon. 

Mr. Pearce* an excellent speaker and my only rival has gone 
away. He intends to go on a canal next summer and wishes me to go 
at $18 per month. Not long since Mr. Pearce wrote to me a poetick 
letter to which I replied in like manner, but only in criticism. At 
the same time I wrote a song on a subject similar to his letter, which 
has since been common singing with the boys. Since that I have 
written considerable: today I have been writing a letter which was 
begun friday evening: it is my masterpiece. Last evening I wrote a 
song of sixteen lines after debating school. 

About a week since I wrote to Grand Father, also to Manly. It is 
wet weather. 


Oct. 26th Last night we had a sharp debate on the question is man 
more inclined to good than evil? I was never before so worried with 
speaking. I have imbibed a habit of speaking very loud: last night 
was heard distinctly twenty rods in a house. I had a dispute with 
Mr. Smith the amount of which is “Shall the accuser prove the 
accused guilty or shall the accused prove himself innocent?” It 
arose from the following circumstance: in a debate not long since 
he accused me of quoting history which I had never read and quoting 
it falsely. I replied giving him reasons to know I had read it and 
had quoted it right and chalenged him to his proofs. He very mod- 
estly replied to my reasons and give me liberty to prove the accusa- 
tion false. Last evening I prepared the way for training him and in 
reply to me he said he had a right to accuse me of what he pleased 
and the accusation stood good until I proved to the contrary. I shall 
train him shortly. 


Nov. 2d By a singular change of circumstance I am to be teaching 
school in Randolph} in a few days. I had formerly given up the 
idea of teaching schoo] at all but I am now very earnestly desired 
to teach for the people in that place, and as I intend to make serving 
the people my business through life I will do it now. yet this is not 


* Benjamin Perce, who is mentioned in later entries of the diary. In the 
autumn of 1836 his sister, Mary, became the wife of Strang. 

yA village in southwestern Cattaraugus County, some twenty miles 
southeast of Strang’s home. 


APPENDICES 197 


possible to my mind. Last saturday I attended the debating school: 
the question was “In which is exhibited the greatest curiosity, animate 
or inanimate life?” The affirmative was ably represented by Messrs. | 
Warren Smith and Slater: on the negative I had the privilege of 
speaking alone. The question was hotly contested on both sides: 
decision for the negative. I had formerly spoken on the same question 
and came off where I went on. I trained Smith a week ago saturday in 
blank verse, and he bore it patiently. It began as follows 


“To him who rides on slander’s foaming steeds 
Who loves to charge a friend with basest deeds 
Who speaks for naught but wrathful vengeance wrong 
Who hopes to rise by slander’s blasting tongue” 


The whole lingo consisted of sixty lines but part of it was so 
savage I thought it not prudent to recite it in publick. 


Noy. 25th Today I have bought some toys for the school to the 
amount of $1.20. also a few books; they are— 


Book of Nature—Good $1.87%4 
Lexicon of Useful Knowledge $1.50 
American Constitutions $1.25 


Life of Napoleon $1.25 


And a few school books and others of less consequence. The books 
I bought for company next winter. Tomorrow I shall start for my 
school. 


Randolph 
Nov. 7th 1831 Well I have now set down for one winter; for one 
winter of hard labour too. I think from the present appearance of 
weather for it has snowed steadily since I left home, and the snow 
is here a foot deep and fast falling. I am some tired of wallowing 
through the mudy snowy roads. but I shall have a resting spell of 
two days before I begin school. 


Noy. 30th Commenced my school today and had but seven schol- 
lars. This is but a small part of the forty promised. The snow 
continues falling. 


Dec. 31 I have now taught school five weeks and have thirty-five 
schollars: this is a little more like doing business. I would not care 


198 Tue Krincpom oF St. JAMES 


if there were twice that number every day. The school was quite 
untrained when I came here: not a single schollar knew his place 
or duty. but I have succeeded in regulating them and learning them 
what to do without punishing a single schollar. I have some hope 
of being able to get along with the school without any considerable 
difficulty. 

I wrote to father two weeks ago and sent for papers. I have not 
had any news of any consequence since I have been here. I expect 
some papers from Dr. Simons who has given to me nearly two years. 

This is the last day of the year a year somewhat conspicuous for 
the great events which have happened, if civil revolutions and con- 
tests can be called great events. During the year which is about to 
close there have been insurrections in allmost every country in 
Europe. Belgium has gained her independence by accepting Leopold 
for king some of the German states have changed sovereigns: all 
the rest have been crushed by military force, a fine way to decide 
the rights of man. 

We have had deep snow ever since I have been in this town. 


Jan. 1st 1832 Another year has now commenced and I hope it 
may be a year of greater profit than that which has passed, not that 
I have reason to despair in looking back on the passed time of 
my life, but I find my improvements less than what they might 
have been by better use of the same time. I might also have saved 
some time without lessening my ease, and an accumulation of gain 
would add much. 

I expect nothing this year more than a gradual change of cir- 
cumstances. In looking over the evils of life I confess I am some 
times allmost tired of life. To see men in allmost every station in 
life seeking to injure each other in every possible manner; perpetu- 
ally striving with all possible force in matters in which they have no 
concern; one nation making war upon another merely for conquest, 
or even military glory; one people propagating its religion by fire 
and sword; another reviling the first for its cruelty, and yet with 
the more deadly weapons of calumny and slander in its own cause, 
persecuting by word and action in common life all who dare to believe 
a little more or a little less than its own standard; parents teaching 
their own children to believe certain principles without evidence of 
their truth, and even that it is blasphemy to ask or wish for it; men 


APPENDICES 199 


acting in disguise in all their intercourse with each other, and this 
to such an extent that the most intimate friend frequently proves 
to be in heart a most virulent enemy, professing friendship merely to 
find an excuse for injuring his fellow; in a word to see the human 
heart a mere puff of air and at the same time a pit of corruption 
which neither the waters of Syria or India nor even the blood of 
“The Son of God” can cleanse it is indeed enough to make one wish 
himself excluded from the face of man. But this is no painted story 
nor is one-half of the evil yet told; for man has not only dissembled, 
deceived, made war without cause, given cause to those who dare 
not or could not defend their rights, and compelled his fellow to 
believe without evidence or intuition but he has called the God whom 
he says delights in goodness and will punish all evil doers to be a 
witness and a helper in his evil deeds: he has made him a partner in 
his contentions and his strife, to be the author and the lover of evil: 
to be changeable minded: to be capable of willing acting and repent- 
ing of it: to be effected [with] passions like his own, and indeed he 
has completely made man in his own image morally and physically 
and then has declared that God made man in his image. Such is 
the human heart changeable and polluted in the best of men and in 
their best times. Among such beings is my lot cast. I, like the rest. I 
am ashamed of it but it is allmost true, not quite. 

Is“it possible that anyone should wish to have eminence who be- 
lieves all this? Yes it’s possible: it is true I ask no more than what 
this life may give—if it will but I tremble when I look forward to 
future prospects. I am resolved to [devote] my life to the service 
of mankind. 


Jan. 3° This morning I wrote to my brother by Mr. Smith, send- 
ing a poem of forty-eight lines to the Philositorian Society and 
promising a composition if it would be acceptable. The poem was 
one which I read to the secretary while there but neglected to leave 
a copy. 

I keep house alone for Mr. Smith this week and it gives me a 
better chance for study than I could otherwise enjoy. Snow continues 


falling lightly. 


Jan. 7th Mr. Smith has just returned this evening and brought 
a letter from my oldest sister a composition of hers, some of my 
papers, and a few newspapers, for all of which I am very thankful. 


200 Ture Kincpom oF St. JAMES 


The papers contain the President’s message and the report of the 
Secretary of War and Postmaster General. The message has the 
merit of being short, simple and probably correct in general. It 
exhibits the country in a most prosperous state as regards money 
affairs; The opinions concerning the Indians is probably correct 
except as to their right of protection from the United States etc. 

On the whole I think “The Hero” will get a pretty good name at 
his term’s end, though he will never be beloved by the people. 

Snow is melted some. 


Jan. 15th This is the third fair day we have had and it is very 
pleasant now. This has been a very stormy winter but I have gen- 
erally felt pleasant amid the contending elements. Mr. Smith*

We, have some reason to fear that our debating society will share a 
hard fate. 
[About six-sevenths of page of cipher] 
yet Mr. Smith says I tell a falsehood when I say the last year of my 
life was the happiest and when I say I enjoy more happiness than 
I endure misery he is nearly out upon me. The difference is he is a 
restless Christian and I am a cool Philosopher. 


Feb. 14th Last evening the young gentlemen in this settlement 
(Smith’s Mills) went up to Rugs Settlement to attend a debate on 
a chalenge. They had their own question-side, presiding officers, 
time of meeting, place of meeting, and even did not inform us the 
question Not satisfied with these advantages they even would not 
allow us our turn in speaking and then gained the decision by crowd- 
ing one presiding officer out of the chair and appointing another in 
his place After the discussion was closed one of the officers vetoed 
because he advised to decide against them. 


Feb. 18 I have been making arrangements for a splendid exhibi- 
tion at the close of the school year. This evening was appointed for 
recitation of one piece but the house was crowded with spectators 
and it was necessary to give way. If scholars do not care enough 
for instruction or spectators for exhibition to mind their own busi- 
ness I can [use the?] time and money for other purposes. 


Feb. 19th Next week closes my school. I know not what business I 
shall then engage in. Day before yesterday I mailed a letter to uncle 
John W. James offering to go to New York as clerk for him. 

Feb. 19 Today I have not felt so smart as sometimes on account 
of a light cold. Let it go off as it came! My constitution is growing 
stronger continually. 

Our Debating Society has finally succeeded and has been got 
thoroughly into operation. We have had three debates, one was a 
very sharp one, and I had the honor to carry the question allmost 
single handed. We have another next evening. I shall make a very 
little preparation for it. 

I expect to dismiss my school soon and leave the place. The time 
for which I engaged is not up, but I anticipated such a close from 
the commencement, for the people want their boys to work in the

sap works. I made the bargain with Mr. Draper for when he feels 
rich he feels very rich. 

There is no complaint against me and they offer me a good recom- 
mend. 

Some young chaps have treated me rather indecently and I would 
have trained them if they had come in the house but they were wise 
enough to stay away. They said if I would come out doors they 
would dress me, but when I went out they kept a proper distance. 
I wanted to train them in the house but thought it wise not to meddle 
out of doors. I wrote home a short time since and informed my 
parents that there was a prospect of my coming sooner than I had 
expected. 

The winter season is now nearly past and I am sorry I have not 
made more improvement in preparing for my great designs [z lene 
of cipher| but I have, 

But yet I feel as if I had gained some.

presents.

Hanover 


March 9th Once more I find myself in my own town and among 
my own friends and with feelings of real satisfaction I again com- 
mence a renewal of my connections.

I arrived in town last monday and have called on most of my 
friends allready: the town is revolutionized in some respects very 
much, a dozen or twenty couple married and as many farms near 
home changed owners. 


March 18th I am allready beginning to neglect my diary from 
over much labour. I have found time to do some writing but have

March 21* Birthday I am 19 years old and am yet no more than 
a common farmer. It is too bad. I ought to have been a member of 
Assembly or a Brigadier General before this time. If I am ever to 
rival [cipher] which I have [czpher]. 

In the last year I have learned all that I profess to know. That 
is that I am eager and mankind are frail and I do not half know 
that: nevertheless I shall act upon it for time to come for my own 
benefit. 


Apr. 22d Time passes on very agreeably but with but little profit. 
I am at a loss what to do: sometimes I think of going into the service 
of the fur company: sometimes of studying the law, and sometimes 
of speculating either in this or some foreign country: [2 lines of 
cipher | 

Today our debating school is to be revived but not without opposi- 
tion for some of the common school district have appointed them- 
selves our conscience keepers but we are not to be driven. 

I have not yet begun my correspondence with Mr. Crowley on 
account of the uncertainty of my remaining in this place. 

This day makes just one year since I cut my foot. It is yet weak 
and caluced but I can outrun any of my fellows. 


May 27th [3 lines of cipher] It is a difficult business for me, but 
I wish to try if there is the least chance my mind has allways been

204. THE Kincpom oF St. JAMES 
June This is a flying time with me. [5 lunes of cipher] 


July 1st I have neglected my writing to long. Aunt Sally Jones 
and Clarissa C have made us a very good visit. They were 
well pleased with the place. I returned with them as far as Buffalo; 
it was the first time I ever visited a city. I and my friends were well 
pleased with the place: it is in a flourishing condition: the people 
were taking all possible means to prevent the introduction of the 
Spasmodic Colera which has for fifteen years been destructive on 
the eastern continent and has just broke out on this. Jerome son of 
my friend Dr. Simons is dead having been afflicted with a long and 
distressing illness, he was a very amiable lad. The Queen Mab and 
the Age of reason have been my companions of late. Shelley is at 
least equal to Paine. It was not till very lately that I was able to 
obtain such books. [z lene of capher] 

I have finally concluded to study the law next winter unless I 
can get better business before that time. 


July 5th The American patriot’s Sabbath, the anniversary of our 
national Independence. Thanks to the frailties of King George, but 
more to the firmness and unyielding patriotism of our own beloved 
leaders that whilst Greece torn with internal convulsions has per- 
mitted foreign autocrats to appoint her a king without asking her 
council, whilst France is still domineered over by a bourbon tyrant 
and whilst the liberty of Frenchmen is a thing rather wished for 
than believed to exist, whilst England trembles and totters to her 
very foundations with the racking of internal convulsions, whilst 
Belgium holds her national existence only by the hated will of the 
tyrants, whilst the death knell of Poland is closing its sound upon 
our ears, and a dark dismal and bloody cloud hangs over the once 
beloved republicks of South America, whilst a doleful and horrid 
darkness overshadows almost all the earth, and the ignorant multi- 
tude pay obeisance and adoration to the cruel tyrants that oppress 
and rob them, we are permitted to enjoy national and civil liberty to 
be our own governors and legislators and to speak and to think 
according to our own consciences. 

Long may our liberty continue, may bigotry and religion cease 
to tramel it, may the absolute despotism which superstition has 
established over the minds of some work its own cure. 


APPENDICES 205 


The day was appropriately celebrated at Fayette. A procession of 
about 500 people marched from the village to a grove and heard an 
oration from Esq. Mullet. Tables were set under bowers and toasts 
drank and cannon fired. The young people attended a ball from 
3 o'clock till sunrise. The bill was $2.75 per couple and amounted 
to about $100 on the company. This sum was sufficient to procure 
respectable entertainment for the whole company purchase a hand- 
some library of 100 volumes with the necessary appendages and to 
make every person present a sharer in the benefits of it. 


July 27 From my infancy I have been taught that mankind was 
totally depraved, and my own observation and experience have 
demonstrated that the heart of man is an impure fountain from 
which bitter waters are perpetually flowing. I have long been per- 
suaded that the depravity of man originated in his unnatural habits 
and when I first made the discovery I firmly believed that I had also 
found a cure: that if the depravity of man originated in his un- 
natural habits, a return to those which are natural would effect a 
cure; that though the cure might be slow yet it would be certain and 
invariable. All this I still believe, but what are our natural habits 2 

I have searched and searched in vain. The question is yet un- 
surmountable, and so far as I know or in all probability ever can 
know, vice, misery, and wretchedness; gold, monarchy and murder; 
kings, priests, and parisites are man’s perpetual associates. My heart 
sickens at the thought, and my whole soul shrinks with horror at 
the idea, and I wish that I had not been born. 

[3 lines of cipher |

confirm to be [one and one-half lines of cipher| unless I find better 
business. 


Aug. 21°* Today my good parents go to visit brother and fetch 
home sister. [Paragraph of cipher | 


231 This commenced the sickest day I ever suffered since my 
remembrance but now (evening) I am again smart for a sick person: 
that is I am able to walk about. I was taken with pains, purging and 
vomiting. Some call it colera but I think otherwise. 


206 Tue Kincpom oF St. JAMES 


25‘ Attend to business as usual but I am weak. 


31st Tomorrow the train bands meet: what noble soldiers they 
are and what an officer I am: that beats my grandmother’s relations. 


Sept. 16 My head is filled with projects. I propose publishing a 
literary and scientifick journal and am only waiting for a printer. 
This is of minor importance and I shall undertake it merely to . 
introduce myself to the publick. 

Moreover I hope very soon to form a Literary Association and 
besides this I shall make an effort for a “mutual instruction military 
Academy” for young militia officers. By the way I am fourth corporal 
after having failed of an election to a higher office. The cause of my 
failure was my refusal to buy men’s votes with rum and I am more 
displeased with that than I should be with a dozen such effects. 


Oct. 15th Late in the evening. I have just finished a constitution 
for a literary society at Forestville. An Academy has lately been 
established there by Mr. S. a. Hutt. 

Allen Smith who married aunt Polly six or seven years ago ha[s] 
just left the country, I hope forever, he having another wife. I 
opposed the match with unshaken firmness till opposition was vain. 
When true to a maxim of mine that it is better to submit to anything 
than to protract dissension forever I finally gave in my submission, 
but with a heavy heart and a deep-rooted conviction that she had 
sold herself to misery. I have neve[r] had occasion to change that 
opinion. Her days have been spent in care and distress, and her 
nights in misery and mourning. He deceived her in anything that 
concerning her happiness, pretending to have a handsome property 
he proved to be poor pretending to be amiable, kind and affectionate 
he was disagreeable harsh and relentless. He became a minister of 
the Baptist denomination, preached in several churches and was 
very successful; what an argument for infidelity; though he always 
got in a jumble; and finally when he was found out went away with 
nearly half his property (except household furniture) leaving Polly 
with two children in poor health and less property than he received 
by her—more of this at another time perhaps. 


Nov. 11th The commencement of winter the emblem of life’s 
latest stage is close at hand. Reviewing the scenes of the past summer

I have reason to know that I have made some advances towards the

Jan 1st The New Year begins in total darkness: at the commence- 
ment the sun was at antipodes the moon had forgotten to shine on 
this dark faced globe, and dreary dismal clouds shut out the reflected 
light of distant frowning planets, while the proud stars at a more 
respectful distance made us feel their conscious superiority. This is 
only the work of nature: Nature never works for man alone but 
keeps her own unvarying course regardless of mens prayers and 
wishes 

It is never to early to begin to do well, nor is it ever late enough 
to excuse beginning: but yet the first day of the year is an auspicious 
time for commencing new plans of improvement. Yet our people 
generally use this day to increase their profligacy (or to make it 
immeasurable). It is astonishing to observe what waste of time, 
talent, and money people will make for show on our common holy 
days. Turkey games occurred yesterday in many places but they are 
degraded to mer[e] drunken revels instead of being nurseries for 
marksmen. The young people generally attended balls and as the 
ground is muddy and the rain falling they have no very pleasant 
sport in their rides at least. 

By careful calculation I am convinced that the expense of balls 
in this County will not be less than six thousand dollars—not a very 
small sum to pay for sport of so short continuance. 

These are light affairs compared with others in our country and 
in the eastern world; the whole civilised world is verging to scenes 
of darkness horror and bloodshed. Disunion with all the horrors of 
anarchy and civil war are stareing us in the face, while our people 
are setting by in regardless security confident in the strength of our 
goverment and believing that our Constitution is an effectual shield 
against all evils, and an unfailing security of liberty and justice 
when corruption lurks in midnight darkness and stalks forth at 
noonday, when the highest officers under our constitution are engaged 


208 Tue KINGDOM OF ST. JAMES 


in the vilest frauds against goverment, and the people wiil elect 
to office men who declare themselves only fighting for the spoils 
of the vanquished Men who know and ought to know that allmost 
every article of our Constitution has been violated by the highest 
functionaries of goverment, time and again and with impunity, 
and that attempts to bring the offenders to justice have been laughed 
at by the great body of the [nation?] These things have varied as 
different parties were in power but not for the better. We loose by 
every chang and are gradually going on from bad to worse. What 
the end will [be] no one knows but we have reason to anticipate the 
worst that any visionary has dreamed of or feared. 

The politicks of the states south of the Potomack have allways 
been inclined to disunion and of those east of the Hudson, to 
consolidation. Those at the south belong to the great republican party 
and in its early ascendancy gained great influence to the total ex- 
clusion of those on the other extremes. Of course the goverment 
inclined to disunion also, or in other words there was a gradual 
curtailing of the powers properly belonging to the general gover- 
ment. On the total failure of the old federal party and the breaking 
out of the republican on account of its diversity of interests and lack 
of opposition, the old leading chiefs of the federal retired and those 
who were not universally known fell in with the various parties 
formed of the republicans and thus gained a handsome and growing 
interest in the affairs of goverment. 

The southern politicians viewed these things with disquietude and 
alarm. They knew full well that they were fast looseing in their 
portion of representation: every other part of the country increasing 
in population faster than they, and they knew as well that their 
statesmen were faster looseing their portion of influence in the na- 
tional councils. Every means in their power was tried to arrest the 
course of events but tried in vain. They anticipated failure in those 
attempts and busied themselves in preparing the public mind for 
more desperate means when it should be their interest to make a 
final attempt. In the meantime they were courted by such parties as 
were corrupt enough to pay them for their assistance and by the 
Jackson party successfully. Under his administration they gained 
some of their former influence but his sentiments were not con- 
genial with theirs and as soon as he saw himself strong enough with- 
out them and they saw he would never assist them in their ultimate 


APPENDICES 209 


designs both parties gladly denounced the alliance, and the south- 
erners prepared a law for the fatal rupture which must ruin them 
or the government. The Tariff is the great burden of their com- 
plaint and to it they impute all the evils and suffering from slavery 
luxury and misgovernment—as well as that of producing nothing 
but what the southwestern planters produce in three times as large 
quantities with the same labour. 

The South Carolinians take the lead in this business and hold 
that any state has a right to nullify laws made by congress and 
solemnly declared constitutional by the Supreme Court whenever such 
state shall deem them unconstitutional. The Legislatures of the 
southern states in general probably will not concur in this. But it is 
supposed by those who observe closely that they will generally agree 
that a state may secede from the Union without incurring the dis- 
pleasure of the general government or the other states. 

As yet but little has been done that wears a permanent character. 
The Legislature of South Carolina have called a convention to con- 
sider the Tarriff laws which convention declared said laws void in 
that state, commanded the authorities to prevent their execution and 
imposed a detestable test oath on officers judges and jurors. 

some have entertained a hope that the valiance of their proceed- 
ings would work a reaction in publick sentiment and perhaps it 
would in this country where people are a little more considerate but 
I venture to predict (and with sorrow) that this will not be the 
case, that there will not be a reaction and that they have taken their 
ground for success or ruin. 

The President, Gen. Jackson, has issued a proclamation inform- 
ing them of the consequences which must result from such a course 
and declaring that the laws shall be executed and the Union shall 
be preserved. This proclamation is very popular and people generally 
believe it will put an end to nulification that the tariff will be modi- 
fied and the South Carolina convention will be recalled and will 
repeal the odious ordinance of nulification but it will not be so. 
they will not retreat unless they know their only alternative. Sub- 
mission or death. Nevertheless The Proclamation will have one good 
effect; that is, it will call around Gen. Jackson all or nearly all 
those leaders and statesmen who have the confidence of the people 
in those states which have allways opposed his pretensions but which 
are devoted to the Union. 


210 THE KINGDOM OF ST. JAMES 


The proclamation is an able state paper and if Jackson is the 
most popular man in the United States it will effect [its purpose] 
if anything will. Possibly its firmness may drive the nulifyers but 
its arguments will not convince them. They have run mad—the 
disease is epidemick and unless soon arrested in its course our govern- 
ment is a nulity our liberty the liberty—of the assassin! justice a 
mockery and perpetually changing power the only rule of right. 

We are quite anxious to hear how Virginia receives the President’s 
Proclamation Much, perhaps the very existence of the Union de- 
pends upon his course. People generally have much confidence in 
her but for myself I fear the very worst. 


Jan. 2d I am studying the Law. began 17th Dec. Esq. Fraser is 
my tutor. I get a lesson in Blackstone’s Commentaries of about 20 
pages a day. I know not what my prospects are but this much I 
know—I never will be a member of the bar when I am afraid of any 
of equal experience in the state. I should rather be the best hunter 
in an Indian tribe than a commonplace member of the New York 
bar. However it is useless to say what I will not be though well 
enough to think what I will do. So I think I will study faithfully 
day and night for seven years if by so doing I can make a respect- 
able lawyer, and if I cannot I think I will try the mercantile busi- 
ness. Nevertheless if called upon in a good cause I should prefer 
the profession of arms if I could accommodate my mind to it. 


Jan. 3rd Just heard from Virginia—affairs go the very worst 
possible. The governor is a nullifier and the Legislature not much 
better A committee of 21 have agreed to report resolutions affirming 
the Virginia doctrines I believe with some new and revolting features. 
The Nulifiers are requested to desist in their present course but are 
backed up in all their objections. The president’s proclamation is 
condemned in severe terms, and congress is caled upon to see that 
the course therein contained be not followed and the doctrine is 
plainly avowed that a State has a right to secede from the Union 
and that its citizens are not guilty of treason in taking up arms 
against the government at the call of the state authorities. 

It is reported also that the proclamation was very illy received 
in Charleston. I very much fear it will pass like the torch in the 
parched plains of Africa or like the Jumblers’ fire burns in the 


APPENDICES 211 


mountains of India which sheds a gloomy light in the midst of thick 
surrounding darkness but to discover the spoliations of all that is 
beautiful in nature; the destruction of all the monuments of art, of 
the overwhelming ruin of the kings who rashly make it the declara- 
tion of their own destruction. 

Our honest people are still confident in the success of the presi- 
dent, believing that a few hundred troops which are being sent on 
to Charleston will effectually protect the revenue officers and that 
the Nulifiers will not venture to strike the first blow. 


Jan. gth Evening. We have had a debate this evening on the pro- 
priety of our government’s removing the Indians without their 
consent. We have debates allmost every week. our numbers are few 
but there are three faithful ones in the number. Mr. Lakatt one of 
our principal members has a peculiar faculty, such as I never met 
with in any other person, of appearing sensible and even reasonable 
in the greatest absurdities. He is skillful in the management of a 
question and is not particular as to which side he takes on any 
question. He is a particular friend of mine. 


Jan. 10th News again from the south. The President’s Proclama- 
tion has rather exasperated than killed the Nulifiers. They are pro- 
ceeding in the most determined manner. several bills for executing 
the ordinance of nulification are in progress in both houses of the 
Legislature. The governor Gen. Hayne has at the request of the 
Legislature issued a proclamation in answer to that of the president 
which is well calculated to arouse the spirits and to inspire the ardor 
of the people to whom it is devoted. It is a most masterly production 
and in a good cause it would be to its author a memorial more im- 
perishable than a pillar of marble. 

Why are such men to be leaders of such a party. Men who have 
had the highest places under our government, and the most unex- 
ceptional places in the confidence of the people. It seems as though 
they were led by some blind and causeless fatality within as though 
the fixed mind had forsaken its utmost purpose and the whole soul 
had become the slave to objects which it knew not, and the patriot 
who but yesterday was pure as ether today has murdered himself and 
poisoned his friends that he might ruin his country. I have regarded 
Gen. Hayne as a father and only regretted that I had not a personal 


21D Tue Kincpom oF St. JAMES 


acquaintance. but though I will not say he is ruined I fear his 
country is undone. O! curse the fatal star that shone upon his birth 
too brightly. But I am indulging in imagination. Our country may 
not be ruined. Even now the business may be settled and if not new 
governments may be built on the ruins of the old, perhaps securing 
liberty to the people or perhaps power to the tyrants. 

The whole world has her troubles also and fears are entertained 
that the Belgium question may embroil all Europe in war. Great 
Britain and France have allready interfered in favour of Belgium 
and Russia and Prussia are known to view these things with dis- 
quietude and distrust. 


Jan. 27. Our political affairs are rather growing worse though 
they have not yet come to an exact crisis. The day on which the 
Carolina act of Nulification is to take effect is just at hand; only 
four days hence. Congress are busily engaged on a new Tariff bill, 
reducing the rate of duties to the standard of 1816, which is merely 
nominal. Perhaps it might have been well to have kept it at [the old] 
rate but it is too late now. The rate of duty on foreign merchandise 
was gradually increased from that time down to 1828 when it had 
become a perpetual protection to American Manufacturers. Now an 
attempt is being made to suddenly reduce it to a mere nominal sum 
and of course to completely ruin those who have invested all their 
fortunes in manufacturing under its protection. The plan may suc- 
ceed and may not. Nothing can be gained by it. The manufacturing 
states would suffer a complete ruin by its effect. And we of the 
western part of the Empire State might feed wheat to our hogs and 
set the girls to making linnen or doing kitchen work for 30 or 40 
cents per week. 

If reports are true the President has exposed himself to the just 
contempt of all the world. His proclamation was worthy the place 
he occupied and was expected to draw round him a host of statesmen 
who had long been opposed to him. In that proclamation he told the 
people that the laws should be executed and the Union should be 
preserved ; that the government authorities were not to be intimidated 
and that the menaces of the nulifiers were vain and nugatory. 

But now if we have the truth he is intimidated and is very 
anxious by timely and unjust concession to back out of his own 
ground, to yield to a snarling faction, to buy the favour of the 


APPENDICES 212. 


Nulifiers at the expense of the Union to leave the New England states 
the choice of secession or total ruin. 

There is no knowing his course A day beforehand. Every paper 
which he publishes is in a new style and his Messages to Congress 
would not be thought to be the same man’s work. Nor does anyone 
pretend to believe he is the author of any of his state papers. This 
accounts for his published opinions being so far superior to his 
actions and private conversation. I am getting along quite well with 
my study. Have allready read one book of Blackstone except so 
much as relates to personal property. The study suits me and I 
think I shall continue it unless the field of battle lacks one man.

there is to be a literary paper in the commencement and that all-

Feb. 3d Sunday. The superstitious hold it waste but for myself 
I know of no time to go to do a good deed. 

Yesterday I finished the first two books of Blackstone. I study 
about fifteen hours a day but one-half of this time is given to poli- 
ticks and miscellaneous works, and I can only get about five days in 
a week. [one and one-half lines of cipher] 

The President has communicated a message to both houses of 
Congress on the Carolina affairs, together with all the papers on that 
subject which he could obtain. There was some debate in Congress 
when these papers were submited. Mr. Calhoun (who has resigned 
the office of vice-president and accepted that of senator) made an 
animated speech against the president. There is no knowing how this 
business will end. Congress had been for some time engaged in a 
new tariff bill, but it is doubtful whether it will pass. The nulifyers 
wish not to have any reduction of the tarriff unless it is totally abol- 
lished. The Jackson men wish not to reduce it unless by so doing 
they can gain the nulifyers because they would lose the eastern 
strength and the manufacturing strength will not consent even to 
a partial reduction except to avoid civil war. There are many mem- 
bers who are willing to very much reduce the tarriff merely to avoid 
civil war but who will never consent to it short of an absolute pledge 


214 Tue Kincopom oF St. JAMES 


on the part of the leading Carolina statesmen that they will retract 
their position. This pledge never will be given but by absolute 
necessity. The political existence of Calhoun Hayne McDuffie Hamil- 
ton and several others who have figured in the highest political 
circles depend upon their success in this undertaking. they have 
140,000 gallant spirits to back them up: they are a hot headed race 
and they will not retract unless influenced by sternn steel. They may 
compromise but they only will be the gainers in such a case. They 
will give nothing but by force or for a chance of taking double 
another time. 


Feb. 8th On the oth Dec. the Belgian [revolution] was effectually 
commenced. We had the news by the first [of] this month. How it 
will end is uncertain but this much is certain, that France will shake 
Europe like a sail in the wind. her king will rise or fall like a 
meteor in the air, his chance is precarious, but the course of his 
people is certain. The eastern powers still stand aloof, but [the] 
Prussians are making immense preparations to meet their wants.

Feb. oth I rather expect, or conjecture that Virginia will back 
out of her favour to Nullification a little but will stick to her old 
doctrines generally. She will not approve the president’s Proclama- 
tion or the tarriff but will stick for the Union if she can regain her 
former influence. North Carolina I think is rather gaining for 
Nullification and Georgia will probably stand strong for it if cir- 
cumstances favour some other southern states may cast some influ- 
ence that way. 

Thinking again of all the circumstances at the latest dates I am 
inclined to think Congress cannot reduce the tarriff at present. Last 
[news] from Washington was allmost the last of January. The 
tarriff bill was not nearly completed. Powerful exertions were making 
to delay until Congress could get some facts to act upon. many who 
were willing to reduce the duties wish to know something of the 
effect of particular species of industry. If they proceeded immedi- 
ately large districts might be ruined by the operation of the law if 
they delayed but three days it would be too late. The bill could not 
even be published in Carolina before the die would be cast. More- 
over if the bill was passed it would not do to have it take effect im- 


APPENDICES 25 


mediately. It would ruin half our merchants to permit foreigners 
to run in goods of the like kind and glut the market while their 
present stocks are on hand. 


Feb. 29th The French took Antwerp citadel the last of December 
and since evacuated it and the Belgians have taken possession: there 
is again a small prospect of a general pacification in Europe. With 
us it is different, hostilities had not at the latest dates commenced. 
Indeed there is no certainty as to when the crisis will come. the 
nullifyers are making great preparations and yet delay action. It 
is probable they are in hopes of getting the assistance of other states 
by putting on the appearance of moderation. They have 20,000 
volunteers in arms. 

The Amaranth a literary paper just commenced at Westfield is 
to be removed to Forestville the seventh of March. This is a favour- 
able circumstance for our literary geniuses. I hope it may give a 
spur to the Delphian Society.

March 20th How strangely beautiful this night: it is the eve of 
my nineteenth year’s end. I fancy it’s a picture of myself. The 
piercing red flashes of the noiseless and unseasonable lightning in 
the distant west; the solemn rolling of distant and eternal waters: 
the beautiful murmur of a pleasant waterfall in an adjacent brook: 
the more than usual warmth of the season moderated by a pleasant 
western breeze: the uncouth darkness which mantles the whole face 
of heaven enveils the throne of the Spirit of Nature, spangled over 
by the bright radiant stars of a western sky and broken only by the 
distant flashings of the deep tinged lightning, conspire to make the 
scene solemnly beautiful, grand and majestic. Did I say I fancied 
the scene a picture of myself. Others may judge of me: I will not 
say it again, but my evening’s walk was surely the journey of my 
past life. I was alone: the works of nature and of art surrounded me: 
those of art were prominent: they attracted my attention: I gazed 
upon them: I turned my eyes to their glimmering lights: they did 
not dazzle but bedimmed my eyes: I turned from them in unsatisfied 
silence: the beauty of the scene around me attracted my attention: 
the sun had hid her face beneath the western horison and the moon 
was veiled in clouds far over the eastern waters: I remembered them 


316 Tue Kincpom oF St. JAMES 


not: but those wonders of nature which were before me caught my 
attention: I gazed on the stars in solemn silence: I saw darkly the 
work of nature all around: ’twas mysterious and unaccountable: all 
was majestic and grand but I understood it not and turned away 
and viewed the airy works of man: everything around me now 
reformed: beauties appeared at a distance: but like the rainbow, 
they were beyond my reach: but the illusion drew me on: the dark- 
ness grew thicker, the sun apeared not, nor did the moon unveil her 
face, even the light of the stars dazzled my eyes to blindness, so long 
had I gazed on the shades around me: the lightnings played vividly 
on the far distant horison. On a sudden it flashed brightly around: 
the whole scene was enlightened and for a moment the illusive works 
of man appeared as they were: distorted uncouth and broken. I closed 
my eyes with the pain at the excess of light but the images were still 
before me: I saw them and considered: Ah the deception was before 
me I knew they were the works of art but I believed them the art 
of him who veils his face in light, and whose figure marked the 
changeless course of the star, I turned my eyes to the stars for light 
and to the works of nature for instruction: The darkness dispelled: 
the shades retired: the illusive works of man disappeared as I passed 
on, and the spirit of nature moved me with a holy inspiration: the 
sun had not yet appeared nor the moon arisen in the east but there 
was a glory in the light shades of evening like Milton’s twilight in 
heaven. The face of nature was serenely beautiful not an unseemly 
mark appeared the human ken is short and the light of nature shone 
but dimly, but it was in an elusive light [2 words of cipher| 


March 21, 1833 This is my birthday. I arise in hope bright 
prospects are before me but all depends upon most untiring exer- 
tion. 20 years of my life have already passed and what have I 
done. O! what have I done? Nothing! Nothing! Nothing but com- 
menced doing. The last winter has given me a fair start. I know I 
have done well in my study and I believe my prospects are fine. 
Whether I ever shall make a first rate Lawyer or not is uncertain 
but my study for two years to come will be useful in business which 
I can follow if I do not practice that profession. Indeed in almost 
any business a knowledge of the fundamental principles of law and 
of some part of the detail is very useful if not absolutely necessary.

January 1st 1834. Thus begins the new year. I am confined to 
labour as usual, but will [it] be beneficial in inuring me to habits 
of industry: so, so far from complaining that I have no leisure I 
begin to be glad that I am loaded with business. 

My school is large and my health rather poor, but I find time 
to study considerable. 

Jan. Last week my school was very full averaging seventy five 
scholars. I have in all 70 writers more than forty in Arithmetick and 
fifteen or twenty in Grammar and Geography each yet I neglect 
none. 

The affair with Foster whom I dismissed from school did not end 
without some trouble (very agreeable trouble as far as I was con- 
cerned for I should not object to one fracas): and might not short 
of blood shed if I had [page torn] convinced him and four or 
[page torn] others that I was willing to [page torn] a little. As 
it was I merely had to be seen with a brace of pistols which I immedi- 
ately disposed of and when they had concluded they were on the 
wrong tiger track it was my turn to laugh at them for being fright- 
ened with an iron candlestick,—but another name for a pistol. 


Jan. 30th. I have a real rest, having only about sixty schollars on 
an average this week. Thus far I have had a very pleasant winter

[beginning of page] it is very uncertain about his wanting to engage 
me now. 


Feb 22 Today I have been to Fredonia to purchase presents for 
my schollars. Night finds me the most down that I have been these 
three years. Indeed I am about worn out for the present. School 
teaching does not agree with my health generally, but this winter 
my labours have been intolerable. No teacher in my acquaintance 
ever did half so much. 


Feb 29th Today I am housed up for the first time in four years 
except when I cut my foot. I shall be about again soon. It is not 


218 Tue KINGDOM OF ST. JAMES 


my lot to be sick long. I had my portion of that in earlier days. I 
have sent word to my school. They will probably have the exhibition. 
Town meeting is at hand and I should like an election to a place 
which would produce a little pay and leave me a little liberty to 
study. But there are two difficulties, first I am under age, second I 
am too inflexible too independent for the present dominent party 


March 3d Just returned from a visit to my friends in Collins. 
I visit very little, less than I wish to. 

I find myself nominated on what is called the people’s ticket for 
constable. There is little prospect of success yet there is nothing 
like trying. A nomination from the other party would [be] better 
because that is stronger: for as to principle and talent there is little 
choice. The office is worth $300 a year and would be good exercise 
while studying [end of page| 


March gth Town meeting today and of all meetings for publick 
business which I ever witnessed or heard of none equaled this for 
folly and corruption. Only 472 votes were polled and from these the 
chairman found more than five hundred for allmost every office in 
town. One man who run on both tickets had 711 votes and for the 
office of Justice both candidates together had a still greater number.

March 20th Time passes on but improvements do not allways keep 
pace, however I am trying what I can do in that line by again read- 
ing law. I now read with Esq. Rathbon at Silver Creek. I would 
embrace other business if it offered a fair prospect but shall not 
neglect this for any visionary project. I have not yet heard from 
uncle John but I do not think he will send for me. I was rather 
too late in writing to him and now the pressure in the money market 
is so great that business is at a dead standstill. Such times have not 
been experienced in sixteen years and the first question on Change is 
not who has broke but who remains. This is in consequence of a 
dispute between the National administration and the United States 
Bank. Gen. Jackson commenced a war upon the Bank immediately 
on coming into office and has continued it ever since. The deposits 
of publick money are removed to local Banks and the United States 
Bank in consequence has narrowed its business several millions while 


APPENDICES 219 


the local banks do not increase theirs in proportion lest they should 
be broke in a sudden pressure. There is no doubt the United States 
bank may be dispensed with and individual and publick business 
continue: but a proper plan has not yet been adopted and we have 
no prospect of one. 


Mar. 21st Surely this is no strange day: much like other days: 
the sun rose at six and goes just fast enough to set at six though if 
it was more dependent than I am and ever have been I might think 
as it keeps behind clouds and winds and storms that it had a mind 
to play a prank and shorten or lengthen a day. 


so busy in doing nothing as in the last of April and first of May. 
When I have little to do I have no time to do anything. There is not 
a more abominable saying than “not too many irons in the fire.” 
The more the better provided they are not forgotten. While cooling 
the first hammer the second and heat the third regularity and atten- 
tion only are necessary then none will burn 

I am now studying law with Esq. Rathbone but my progress is 
rather slow. I give too much attention to farming at which I am 
also engaged with my father We have had a wonderful season. 
In February [the] roads were dusty and some trees began to put 
forth leaves. 12th of May snow was three inches deep on the hill- 
sides. Many were planting corn but the soil suddenly became too 
light. The leaves of the forest were cut off and now beech timber 
looks as though it had been burned. 


June 23 A miserable hot day. I am almost melted. I have given 
the day to news and light reading thus far. I must now try some 
thing of another sort for by grace of God and will of man I am 
appointed to deliver an address on temperance the first of July and 
all tell me it must be completely written. I believe I could speak 
as well without writing but I shall not try at first. 


July ist Well my time is coming. I shall soon have to make the 
temperance address. Probably there have been some misgivings as 
to the result but it will soon be decided. _ 

Evening. It’s done. After this experiment with my experience in 
debating I may safely ever consider myself perfectly at home in 


publick speaking.
